Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.\n---\n\n# Solve Mathematics Rigorously\n\nProduce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.\n\n## Determine the intended problem\n\nInterpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.\n\nHandle ambiguity according to its effect:\n\n- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.\n- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.\n- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.\n- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.\n\nIf the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.\n\nAnswer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.\n\n## Establish a minimal setup\n\nBegin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:\n\n- domain and assumptions;\n- definitions used in the solution;\n- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;\n- notation that must persist through the answer.\n\nReuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.\n\nDo not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.\n\n## Give the architecture before a substantial proof\n\nBefore a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.\n\nKeep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.\n\n## Execute every nontrivial step\n\nDerive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.\n\nIn particular:\n\n- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;\n- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;\n- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;\n- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;\n- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;\n- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.\n\nAvoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.\n\nDo not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.\n\nVerify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.\n\nIf the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.\n\n## Use examples and checks proportionately\n\nInclude an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.\n\nUse low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:\n\n- substituting the result back into the defining relation;\n- checking dimensions, signs, or units;\n- testing a boundary or small case;\n- comparing with a known special case;\n- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.\n\nDo not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.\n\n## Keep the presentation compact\n\nUse headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Teach a motivated non-specialist at genuine mathematical depth. Expose the simple structure beneath advanced formalism without suppressing difficult steps.\n\n## Prepare from the sources\n\nRead every source the user identifies in full mathematical detail before teaching source-dependent claims. If a source is inaccessible, incomplete, or missing cited material, state the limitation and do not imply that it was read.\n\nBuild an internal source ledger:\n\n- record each source's identity, date, and status, such as preliminary, revised, audited, or final;\n- map notation and assumption changes across versions;\n- identify the precise objects, problem, model, regime, observables, main claims, constructions, arguments, evidence, and unresolved gaps;\n- separate compatible claims from incompatible ones;\n- determine which claims survived later corrections or audits;\n- retain theorem, equation, section, and page locations when available.\n\nDo not dump this ledger as an opening summary. Use it to teach the requested block faithfully. Attribute source claims and distinguish them from your own interpretation.\n\nMaintain an internal lesson ledger across turns: stable notation, completed blocks, the last point the user clearly understood, unresolved confusion, and the next logical block. Minimally recall any earlier definition needed in the current response.\n\n## Control the pace\n\nTeach exactly one natural conceptual block per response. If the user specifies a starting point, begin there. Otherwise begin with the document's objects, assumptions, and concrete setup.\n\nUse this domain-neutral progression as a guide:\n\n1. objects, assumptions, and setup;\n2. exact concrete or finite formulation;\n3. meaning and type of the central variables or maps;\n4. derivation of the main relations;\n5. proof or mechanism architecture;\n6. obstruction, non-closure, or source of difficulty;\n7. proposed abstraction, approximation, or limiting construction;\n8. assumptions connecting the construction to the original problem;\n9. theoretical and empirical evidence;\n10. remaining gaps and falsifying cases.\n\nAdjust the sequence to the sources. Do not jump ahead merely because later material is related. When the user says “next block,” continue with only the next logically necessary idea. When the user is confused, stop advancing and rebuild from the last understood point.\n\nWrite each ordinary lesson turn as a compact, seamless blackboard explanation: a few short paragraphs, only the equations needed now, few or no bullets, and normally no headings. Do not begin or end with a broad recap. Stop naturally after the current block and wait for the user before moving deeper.\n\n## Construct each block\n\nUse this internal order without displaying it as a template:\n\n1. **Minimal recall:** restate only the definitions needed now.\n2. **New object:** define one new object and give its mathematical type.\n3. **Derivation:** obtain its relation from the preceding setup.\n4. **Meaning:** explain the role of every term or condition.\n5. **Analogue or example:** use one structurally faithful simple case when it genuinely helps, then return to the exact mathematics.\n6. **Role:** connect the object to the document's argument.\n7. **Status:** state what is established and what remains unproved.\n\nMake the block self-contained for its present conceptual burden, not for the entire project.\n\n## Enforce notation and type discipline\n\nUse a small, stable notation. Prefer the source's symbols unless they collide or obscure meaning. If renaming is necessary, state the mapping explicitly.\n\nDefine every symbol before using it. For each object, state as relevant:\n\n- its domain, codomain, or ambient space;\n- whether it is a scalar, vector, matrix, function, operator, random variable, measure, index, or coordinate;\n- whether it is fixed, sampled, or evolving;\n- what each index means;\n- its dependence on other variables or parameters.\n\nDistinguish carefully between:\n\n- one function of an entire vector and a vector of coordinatewise functions;\n- an index and a coordinate;\n- a fixed label or parameter and an evolving state;\n- a representative element and a probability law over elements;\n- a mathematical state and a numerical representation of that state.\n\nWhen writing an integral, identify the integration variable, its space, the measure, and what the integral averages or accumulates. When a distribution is specified only at initialization, do not imply that an evolved quantity retains that distribution merely because it remains a function of the initial label.\n\nDo not introduce shorthand unless the explicit expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Never reuse one symbol for two roles.\n\n## Derive rather than paraphrase\n\nFor every central equation or implication:\n\n1. state the definition or prior relation from which it follows;\n2. show the relevant algebraic, differential, variational, probabilistic, logical, or limiting step;\n3. identify the origin and action of every term;\n4. explain the closest elementary analogue when useful;\n5. state why the relation matters to the main result.\n\nDo not make the user reverse-engineer a displayed formula. Avoid phrases such as “clearly,” “standard,” or “it follows” when they conceal a nontrivial step.\n\nWhen invoking an external theorem, state the exact form needed, list its hypotheses, verify them in the present setting, and explain the resulting conclusion. Check a primary or otherwise authoritative source before relying on a recent, specialized, or uncertain result.\n\nWhen teaching a long proof, devote one block to its architecture before teaching the difficult steps. Explain how the claims, cases, or lemmas fit together without pretending that the architecture is itself the proof.\n\nUse analogies only when they preserve the relevant mathematical structure. State where the analogy stops and return immediately to the exact object.\n\n## Relate levels of description\n\nBegin with the document's concrete objects and exact relations. Introduce an abstract, aggregate, continuum, asymptotic, or numerical description only after showing how it arises from the concrete one.\n\nWhenever the source changes levels, identify:\n\n- the map from the original objects to the new description;\n- what information is retained and discarded;\n- which step is an exact identity;\n- which step uses a theorem and its assumptions;\n- which step is formal, heuristic, empirical, or conjectural.\n\nIf a proposed state or summary evolves autonomously, distinguish internal closure from fidelity to the original system. An autonomous reduced description may still omit information that changes the original system's future.\n\nIf the source uses truncation or approximation, distinguish pointwise or static approximation from the stronger control required by an evolving or iterative system. Examine separately:\n\n- error that leaves the retained representation;\n- feedback from omitted components;\n- amplification or accumulation of error;\n- the time, parameter, or regime over which stability is claimed.\n\nWhen the source calls a construction “finite,” state precisely whether this means finitely many coordinates, finitely many fields, a finite-dimensional state, a finite cutoff, or merely a finite numerical discretization.\n\n## Label epistemic status\n\nClassify each important source-dependent claim using the most accurate category:\n\n- exact identity;\n- proved theorem under stated assumptions;\n- rigorous result for a restricted or finite setting;\n- formal argument;\n- heuristic mechanism;\n- empirical or numerical evidence;\n- conjectural identification;\n- open implication;\n- counterexample or negative result.\n\nDo not silently promote a claim. Express each important assumption as a missing implication:\n\n1. what is assumed;\n2. what conclusion it would justify;\n3. what connecting step is not established;\n4. what observation or counterexample would refute it.\n\nInternal consistency of a proposed theory does not prove that it represents the target object.\n\n## Evaluate evidence and alternatives\n\nFor every numerical comparison, identify:\n\n- the systems or quantities compared;\n- what the reference actually represents;\n- the metric or norm;\n- the scale of the predicted effect and the discrepancy;\n- discretization, optimization, rounding, and sampling uncertainty as applicable;\n- the alternative explanation ruled out;\n- what the experiment leaves unresolved.\n\nDo not infer convergence or a scaling law from too few resolutions. Treat numerical thresholds as decision conventions unless the source proves a mathematical boundary.\n\nWhen the user requests a hostile or devil's-advocate analysis, include only alternatives that engage the declared target and make a distinct mathematical or quantitative prediction. Do not infer global behavior from a local or instantaneous property without proving the connecting step. Do not use possible error cancellation as a complete explanation unless it predicts the observed pattern.\n\n## Repair confusion completely\n\nWhen the user identifies an undefined symbol, ambiguity, missing derivation, or conceptual gap:\n\n- acknowledge the failure directly;\n- do not defend the prior wording;\n- do not append a one-sentence patch;\n- rewrite the entire relevant block self-containedly;\n- preserve compactness and stable notation.\n\nTreat confusion as evidence that the explanation needs rebuilding, not that the mathematics is inherently inaccessible.\n\nBefore sending each block, verify silently that it is faithful to the sources, introduces only one conceptual burden, defines every needed symbol, derives rather than paraphrases, states the status of important claims, stays in the user's language, avoids unnecessary notation and formula dumps, and is compact enough to absorb in one sitting.\n"}]

Hu² ³\n\n## Abstract\n\nAs its width tends to infinity, a deep neural network’s behavior under gradient descent can become simplified and predictable (e.g. given by the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK)), if it is parametrized appropriately (e.g. the NTK parametrization). However, we show that the standard and NTK parametrizations of a neural network do not admit infinite-width limits that can *learn* features, which is crucial for pretraining and transfer learning such as with BERT. We propose simple modifications to the standard parametrization to allow for feature learning in the limit. Using the *Tensor Programs* technique, we derive explicit formulas for such limits. On Word2Vec and few-shot learning on Omniglot via MAML, two canonical tasks that rely crucially on feature learning, we compute these limits exactly. We find that they outperform both NTK baselines and finite-width networks, with the latter approaching the infinite-width feature learning performance as width increases. See arXiv:2011.14522 for the full version of this paper.\n\n<visual_element id="e1">\nThree small scatter plots labeled “NTK”, “Width 64”, and “Width ∞ (Feature Learning)”. The NTK plot includes a legend “type” with blue = state and orange = city. The plots show Word2Vec embeddings of top US cities and states, with cities and states increasingly separated from left to right.\n</visual_element>\n\n*Figure 1.* PCA of Word2Vec embeddings of top US cities and states, for NTK, width-64, and width-∞ feature learning networks (Definition 4.1). NTK embeddings are essentially random (thus trivializing any “transfer” learning), while cities and states get naturally separated in embedding space as width increases in the feature learning regime.\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nThe study of infinite-width limits of neural networks, in particular the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK), has recently solved many longstanding open problems on the optimization and generalization of overparameterized neural networks (Jacot et al., 2018). However, in the NTK limit, (last layer) features learned during pretraining are essentially the same as those from random initialization (Corollary 3.8 and Theorem N.12); this is verified empirically in Word2Vec in Fig. 1. As feature learning lies at the core of deep learning’s far-ranging impact so far (Brown et al., 2020b; Devlin et al., 2019; He et al., 2016) (e.g. without feature learning, transfer learning via pretraining and finetuning, such as with BERT, is meaningless, as we will discuss in Section 3), this insight amounts to a fatal weakness of the NTK theory as a model of neural networks in practice.\n\n**Our Contributions**  We seek to capture feature learning in overparametrized networks by considering other parametrizations and their infinite-width limits. By slightly modifying the standard parametrization (SP), in fact, we can enable feature learning that is *maximal* in a natural sense. We describe how to compute this limit exactly (and rigorously) via the *Tensor Programs* technique developed in (Yang, 2019a;b; 2020a;b). We explicitly calculate this limit for the tasks of Word2Vec (Mikolov et al., 2013a;b) and few-shot learning on Omniglot via MAML (Finn et al., 2017), two standard tasks relying crucially on feature learning. On both tasks, our proposed feature learning limit outperforms both NTK baselines and finite-width networks, with the latter approaching the infinite-width feature learning performance as width increases.\n\nMore generally, we classify a natural space of neural network parametrizations that generalizes standard, NTK, and Mean Field parametrizations. We show what we call the *Dynamical Dichotomy Theorem*, which roughly says any parametrization in this space either admits feature learning or has an infinite-width training dynamics given by kernel gradient descent, but not both. Furthermore, any such infinite-width limit can be computed using the Tensor Programs technique.\n\nIn the main text, we describe relevant related works as they arise, but see Appendix A for a more comprehensive discussion of the surrounding literature.\n\n¹Microsoft Research AI ²Microsoft Dynamics 365 AI ³Work done partly during the Microsoft AI Residency Program. This is a new foundational idea in deep learning theory (as evidenced by its importance to Sho + Guillame\'s excellent talks), and it\'s becoming clear that it\'s often important to understand for both theory + practice.\n\n\nSome central references if you\'re curious:\n\n  * The original NTK paper\n  * Google\'s NTK paper, which I found more physicist-accessible (shoutout to Yasaman)\n  * On Lazy Training, which points out this dichotomy\n  * Tensor Programs IV, which describes the "mu-parameterization" infinite-width limit now gaining popularity in practice (note: this paper has important ideas but is hard to parse -- I suggest watching Greg Yang\'s talk version)\n\nSome more references:\n\n  * Here are some lecture notes by Cengiz + Blake Bordelon that give an introduction to hyperparameter scaling.\n  * Here is a paper by Sho that could also serve as a good intro!\n  * Finally, in a shameless plug, here\'s a recent paper with Greg Yang that I\'m party to, the purpose of which is to give the simplest possible derivation of the feature-learning limit.\n\nTo download: Right-click and choose "Save Link As..."--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAdaptive kernel predictors from feature-learning infinite limits of neural networks (https://openreview.net/pdf/531b961b33ab1b0af4ad543f55159986352f58b7.pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn39search13\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.1 years ago; to gradient flow dynamics the final learned network ... ics of deep MLPs and CNNs under the maximal update ... leads to a feature-learning infinite-width limit.\nneural network training on a variety of metrics, in-\ncluding test loss, intermediate feature kernels, and pre-\nactivation densities. Training Dynamics: Kernel vs. Feature Learning Limits\n\nThe NTK and mean-field (MF) limits are distinct in the infinite-width regime:\n\n  * NTK (Kernel/Lazy Training): For standard or "NTK" parameterizations, the trainable function changes only in the directions set at initialization; internal features remain fixed. Training reduces to kernel gradient descent: $\\mathcal N(0, \\sigma_w^2/n_{l-1})$N(0,σ w 2\u200b/n l−1\u200b)9 with $\\mathcal N(0, \\sigma_b^2)$N(0,σ b 2\u200b)0 the deterministic NTK (Bahri et al., 2023, Yang et al., 2020, Seleznova et al., 2022).\n  * Mean-Field and Maximal-Update (Feature Learning): For "mean-field" or "maximal-update" ($\\mathcal N(0, \\sigma_b^2)$N(0,σ b 2\u200b)1P) parameterizations, features evolve nontrivially. In the two-layer case, the empirical neuron distribution obeys a Wasserstein gradient flow PDE (Luo et al., 2020, Yang et al., 2020). For deeper nets, maximal feature learning at infinite width requires specific scaling of per-layer initialization and learning rate (Yang et al., 2020, Hajjar et al., 2021).\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nOn the Provable Separation of Scales in Maximal Update Parameterization (https://openreview.net/pdf?id=csB1njlpjM)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn39search15\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.2 years ago; 2018), which linearize training in the infinite-width limit, ... the theory of maximal update parameterization (Yang & ... the lens of gradient flow dynamics, revealing distinct phase-\nOn the Provable Separation of Scales in Maximal Update Parameterization\nincorporate integrated learning rate descriptors, yield-\ning a novel theoretical account of the recently observed\n“delay” phenomenon between dynamic learning rates\nand validation loss (Tissue et al., 2024).\nclassification task involving latent word correspondence\nstructures, they demonstrate that gradient descent dynamics\nin a symmetric two-headed transformer with ReLU neurons\nfollow a three-stage progression (Yang et al., 2025).\n2. To arrive at the DMFT, one first notes that the training dynamics of such networks can be rewritten in terms of a collection of dynamical variables (or order parameters)\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nTensor Programs VI: Feature Learning in Infinite-Depth Neural Networks (https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.02244)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn39academia17\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.9 years ago; Title: Tensor Programs VI: Feature Learning in Infinite-Depth Neural Networks ... By classifying infinite-width neural networks and identifying the *optimal* limit, Tensor Programs IV and V demonstrated a universal way, called $\\mu$P, for *widthwise hyperparameter transfer*, i.e., predicting optimal hyperparameters of wide neural networks from narrow ones.\nTitle: Tensor Programs VI: Feature Learning in Infinite-Depth Neural Networks\nAuthors: Greg Yang, Dingli Yu, Chen Zhu, Soufiane Hayou\nDate: Tue Oct  3 17:50:40 2023\n\nBy classifying infinite-width neural networks and identifying the *optimal* limit, Tensor Programs IV and V demonstrated a universal way, called $\\mu$P, for *widthwise hyperparameter transfer*, i.e., predicting optimal hyperparameters of wide neural networks from narrow ones. Here we investigate the analogous classification for *depthwise parametrizations* of deep residual networks (resnets). We classify depthwise parametrizations of block multiplier and learning rate by their infinite-width-then-depth limits. In resnets where each block has only one layer, we identify a unique optimal parametrization, called Depth-$\\mu$P that extends $\\mu$P and show empirically it admits depthwise hyperparameter transfer. We identify *feature diversity* as a crucial factor in deep networks, and Depth-$\\mu$P can be characterized as maximizing both feature learning and feature diversity. Exploiting this, we find that absolute value, among all homogeneous nonlinearities, maximizes feature diversity and indeed empirically leads to significantly better performance. However, if each block is deeper (such as modern transformers), then we find fundamental limitations in all possible infinite-depth limits of such parametrizations, which we illustrate both theoretically and empirically on simple networks as well as Megatron transformer trained on Common Crawl.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nThe Annals of Applied Probability (https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/document?doi=0714d30d128553ba80df318c6d01712f14e5cc1f&repid=rep1&type=pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn39search18\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 months ago; MuP(|Z| > Mu) ≤m(f + BAε) ... Lastly, we provide experiments in more realistic settings which demonstrate that the loss and kernel dynamics of CNNs at fixed feature learning strength is preserved across different widths on a CIFAR classification task.\nTitle: Self-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory of Kernel Evolution in Wide Neural Networks\nAuthors: Blake Bordelon, Cengiz Pehlevan\nDate: Thu May 19 16:10:10 2022\n\nWe analyze feature learning in infinite-width neural networks trained with gradient flow through a self-consistent dynamical field theory. We construct a collection of deterministic dynamical order parameters which are inner-product kernels for hidden unit activations and gradients in each layer at pairs of time points, providing a reduced description of network activity through training. These kernel order parameters collectively define the hidden layer activation distribution, the evolution of the neural tangent kernel, and consequently output predictions. We show that the field theory derivation recovers the recursive stochastic process of infinite-width feature learning networks obtained from Yang and Hu (2021) with Tensor Programs . For deep linear networks, these kernels satisfy a set of algebraic matrix equations. For nonlinear networks, we provide an alternating sampling procedure to self-consistently solve for the kernel order parameters. We provide comparisons of the self-consistent solution to various approximation schemes including the static NTK approximation, gradient independence assumption, and leading order perturbation theory, showing that each of these approximations can break down in regimes where general self-consistent solutions still provide an accurate description. Lastly, we provide experiments in more realistic settings which demonstrate that the loss and kernel dynamics of CNNs at fixed feature learning strength is preserved across different widths on a CIFAR classification task.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nThe Influence of Learning Rule on Representation Dynamics in Wide Neural Networks (https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.02157)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn40academia13\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.9 years ago; Authors: Blake Bordelon, Cengiz PehlevanDate: Wed Oct  5 11:33:40 2022It is unclear how changing the learning rule of a deep neural network alters its learning dynamics and representations. ... In the lazy training limit, this eNTK is static and does not evolve, while in the rich mean-field regime this kernel\'s evolution can be determined self-consistently with dynamical mean field theory (DMFT).This DMFT enables comparisons of the feature and prediction dynamics induced by each of these learning rules.\nTitle: The Influence of Learning Rule on Representation Dynamics in Wide Neural Networks\nAuthors: Blake Bordelon, Cengiz Pehlevan\nDate: Wed Oct  5 11:33:40 2022\n\nIt is unclear how changing the learning rule of a deep neural network alters its learning dynamics and representations. To gain insight into the relationship between learned features, function approximation, and the learning rule, we analyze infinite-width deep networks trained with gradient descent (GD) and biologically-plausible alternatives including feedback alignment (FA), direct feedback alignment (DFA), and error modulated Hebbian learning (Hebb), as well as gated linear networks (GLN). We show that, for each of these learning rules, the evolution of the output function at infinite width is governed by a time varying effective neural tangent kernel (eNTK). In the lazy training limit, this eNTK is static and does not evolve, while in the rich mean-field regime this kernel\'s evolution can be determined self-consistently with dynamical mean field theory (DMFT). This DMFT enables comparisons of the feature and prediction dynamics induced by each of these learning rules. In the lazy limit, we find that DFA and Hebb can only learn using the last layer features, while full FA can utilize earlier layers with a scale determined by the initial correlation between feedforward and feedback weight matrices. In the rich regime, DFA and FA utilize a temporally evolving and depth-dependent NTK. Counterintuitively, we find that FA networks trained in the rich regime exhibit more feature learning if initialized with smaller correlation between the forward and backward pass weights. GLNs admit a very simple formula for their lazy limit kernel and preserve conditional Gaussianity of their preactivations under gating functions. Error modulated Hebb rules show very small task-relevant alignment of their kernels and perform most task relevant learning in the last layer.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSelf-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory of Kernel (https://projects.iq.harvard.edu/files/pehlevan/files/bordelonpehlevan_neurips_2022.pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn40search14\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 10 months ago; alternative theories capable of modeling feature learning dynamics are needed. ... terization to µP with self-consistent dynamical mean ﬁeld theory (DMFT) and recover the stochastic ... 2. ... Thus,\nalternative theories capable of modeling feature learning dynamics are needed.\nRecently developed alternative parameterizations such as the mean ﬁeld [22] and the µP [1] param-\neterizations allow feature learning in inﬁnite-width NNs trained with gradient descent. Using the\nTensor Programs framework, Yang & Hu identiﬁed a stochastic process that describes the evolution\nof preactivation features in inﬁnite-width µP NNs [1]. In this work, we study an equivalent parame-\nterization to µP with self-consistent dynamical mean ﬁeld theory (DMFT) and recover the stochastic\nprocess description of inﬁnite NNs using this alternative technique. In the same large width scaling,\nwe include a scalar parameter γ0 that allows smooth interpolation between lazy and rich behavior\n[18]. We provide a new computational procedure to sample this stochastic process and demonstrate\nits predictive power for wide NNs.\nOur novel contributions in this paper are the following:\n1. We develop a path integral formulation of gradient ﬂow dynamics in inﬁnite-width networks in the\nfeature learning regime. Our parameterization includes a scalar parameter γ0 to allow interpolation\nbetween rich and lazy regimes and comparison to perturbative methods.\n2. Using a stationary action argument, we identify a set of saddle point equations that the kernels\nsatisfy at inﬁnite-width, relating the stochastic processes that deﬁne hidden activation evolution to\nthe kernels and vice versa. We show that our saddle point equations recover at γ0 = 1, from an\nalternative method, the same stochastic process obtained previously with Tensor Programs [1].\n3. We develop a polynomial-time numerical procedure to solve the saddle point equations for deep\nnetworks. In numerical experiments, we demonstrate that solutions to these self-consistency\nequations are predictive of network training at a variety of feature learning strengths, widths\nand depths. We provide comparisons of our theory to various approximate methods, such as\nperturbation theory.\n1.1\nRelated Works\nA natural extension to the lazy NTK/NNGP limit that allows the study of feature learning is to calculate\nﬁnite width corrections to the inﬁnite-width limit. Finite width corrections to Bayesian inference in\nwide networks have been obtained with various perturbative [23–29] and self-consistent techniques\n[30–33]. In the gradient descent based setting, leading order corrections to the NTK dynamics have\nbeen analyzed to study ﬁnite width effects [34–36, 27]. These methods give approximate corrections\nwhich are accurate provided the strength of feature learning is small. In very rich feature learning\nregimes, however, the leading order corrections can give incorrect predictions [37, 38].\nAnother approach to study feature learning is to alter NN parameterization in gradient-based learning\nto allow signiﬁcant feature evolution even at inﬁnite-width, the mean ﬁeld limit [22, 39]. Works on\nmean ﬁeld NNs have yielded formal loss convergence results [40, 41] and shown equivalences of\ngradient ﬂow dynamics to a partial differential equation (PDE) [42–44].\nOur results are most closely related to a set of recent works which studied inﬁnite-width NNs trained\nwith gradient descent (GD) using the Tensor Programs (TP) framework [1]. We show that our discrete\ntime ﬁeld theory at unit feature learning strength γ0 = 1 recovers the stochastic process which was\nderived from TP. The stochastic process derived from TP has provided insights into practical issues in\nNN training such as hyper-parameter search [45]. Computing the exact inﬁnite-width limit of GD has\nexponential time requirements [1], which we show can be circumvented with an alternating sampling\nprocedure. A projected variant of GD training has provided an inﬁnite-width theory that could be\nscaled to realistic datasets like CIFAR-10 [46]. Inspired by Chizat and Bach’s work on mechanisms\nof lazy and rich training [18], our theory interpolates between lazy and rich behavior in the mean\nﬁeld limit for varying γ0 and allows comparison of DMFT to perturbative analysis near small γ0.\nFurther, our derivation of a DMFT action allows the possibility of pursuing ﬁnite width effects.\nOur theory is inspired by self-consistent dynamical mean ﬁeld theory (DMFT) from statistical physics\n[47–53]. This framework has been utilized in the theory of random recurrent networks [54–59],\ntensor PCA [60, 61], phase retrieval [62], and high-dimensional linear classiﬁers [63–66], but has yet\nto be developed for deep feature learning. By developing a self-consistent DMFT of deep NNs, we\ngain insight into how features evolve in the rich regime of network training, while retaining many\npleasant analytic properties of the inﬁnite-width limit.\n2--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSelf-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory of Kernel Evolution in Wide Neural Networks | Pehlevan Group (https://pehlevan.seas.harvard.edu/publications/self-consistent-dynamical-field-theory-kernel-evolution-wide-neural-networks-0)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn40search0\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; Bordelon and C.Pehlevan,“Self-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory of Kernel Evolution in Wide Neural Networks”, Journal of Statistical Mechanics: Theory and Experiment (Machine Learning Special Issue), p.\n\n# Self-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory of Kernel Evolution in Wide Neural Networks\n\n## Publication information:\n\nB. Starting from a dynamical mean field theory description of infinite width deep neural network kernel and prediction dynamics, we provide a characterization of the $O(1/\\sqrt{\\text{width}})$ fluctuations of the DMFT order parameters over random initializations of the network weights. ... However, in the rich, feature learning regime, the fluctuations of the kernels and predictions are dynamically coupled with a variance that can be computed self-consistently.\nTitle: Dynamics of Finite Width Kernel and Prediction Fluctuations in Mean Field Neural Networks\nAuthors: Blake Bordelon, Cengiz Pehlevan\nDate: Thu Apr  6 23:11:49 2023\n\nWe analyze the dynamics of finite width effects in wide but finite feature learning neural networks. Starting from a dynamical mean field theory description of infinite width deep neural network kernel and prediction dynamics, we provide a characterization of the $O(1/\\sqrt{\\text{width}})$ fluctuations of the DMFT order parameters over random initializations of the network weights. Our results, while perturbative in width, unlike prior analyses, are non-perturbative in the strength of feature learning. In the lazy limit of network training, all kernels are random but static in time and the prediction variance has a universal form. However, in the rich, feature learning regime, the fluctuations of the kernels and predictions are dynamically coupled with a variance that can be computed self-consistently. In two layer networks, we show how feature learning can dynamically reduce the variance of the final tangent kernel and final network predictions. We also show how initialization variance can slow down online learning in wide but finite networks. In deeper networks, kernel variance can dramatically accumulate through subsequent layers at large feature learning strengths, but feature learning continues to improve the signal-to-noise ratio of the feature kernels. In discrete time, we demonstrate that large learning rate phenomena such as edge of stability effects can be well captured by infinite width dynamics and that initialization variance can decrease dynamically. For CNNs trained on CIFAR-10, we empirically find significant corrections to both the bias and variance of network dynamics due to finite width.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nFeature-Learning Networks Are Consistent Across Widths At Realistic Scales (https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.18411)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn40academia16\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.3 years ago; Title: Feature-Learning Networks Are Consistent Across Widths At Realistic ScalesAuthors: Nikhil Vyas, Alexander Atanasov, Blake Bordelon, Depen Morwani, Sabarish Sainathan, Cengiz Pehlevan ... We study the effect of width on the dynamics of feature-learning neural networks across a variety of architectures and datasets.\nTitle: Feature-Learning Networks Are Consistent Across Widths At Realistic Scales\nAuthors: Nikhil Vyas, Alexander Atanasov, Blake Bordelon, Depen Morwani, Sabarish Sainathan, Cengiz Pehlevan\nDate: Sun May 28 17:09:32 2023\n\nWe study the effect of width on the dynamics of feature-learning neural networks across a variety of architectures and datasets. Early in training, wide neural networks trained on online data have not only identical loss curves but also agree in their point-wise test predictions throughout training. For simple tasks such as CIFAR-5m this holds throughout training for networks of realistic widths. We also show that structural properties of the models, including internal representations, preactivation distributions, edge of stability phenomena, and large learning rate effects are consistent across large widths. This motivates the hypothesis that phenomena seen in realistic models can be captured by infinite-width, feature-learning limits. For harder tasks (such as ImageNet and language modeling), and later training times, finite-width deviations grow systematically. Two distinct effects cause these deviations across widths. First, the network output has initialization-dependent variance scaling inversely with width, which can be removed by ensembling networks. We observe, however, that ensembles of narrower networks perform worse than a single wide network. We call this the bias of narrower width. We conclude with a spectral perspective on the origin of this finite-width bias.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nA generalized neural tangent kernel (https://nndc.group/wp-content/uploads/2025/03/eilersmemmesheimergoedekeneurips24.pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn40search17\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 5 months ago; Gradient flow in the infinite-width limit ... reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces.\nA partial proof of a version of this theorem for the generalized NTK will be given later.\nOnly an auxiliary lemma remains to be proved.\nC.2.1\nGradient flow in the infinite-width limit\nGiven the results of the previous section, we can formulate an infinite width version of Equation\n(S10) by replacing the empirical with the analytic NTK. ## Ordinal 1996 message assistant commentary
I’m using the teaching and rigorous-math skills to keep the comparison precise. So far, this does not look like a new scaling principle: the width powers match μP. The substantive question is whether the proof strengthens “a limit after each fixed number of updates” into “one well-defined learning flow over a whole time interval.”

The\nL191@P2: “feature learning limits” mentioned above in our main\nL192@P2: experiments are µP limits. Figure to the right: We\nL193@P2: empirically verify our max learning rate predictions\nL194@P2: on relu MLP with 2 hidden layers, trained with square loss on CIFAR10. We plot learning rate vs\nL195@P2: accuracy in each subplot. Each curve represents MLP with a specific width. The right edge of each\nL196@P2: curve indicates the max learning rate. We see, indeed, max learning rate\nL198@P2: for SP scales like 1/width but is constant in µP.\nL199@P2: 1st forward pass\nL200@P2: 1st backward pass\nL201@P2: 2nd forward pass\nL202@P2: 2nd backward pass\nL203@P2: SGD Training Progress\nL204@P2: NN\nL205@P2: NNGP NTK Feature Learning Limit\nL206@P2: Take 𝑤𝑖𝑑𝑡ℎ → ∞ Limit via Tensor Programs\nL207@P2: Prior works This work\nL208@P2: Key Theoretical Idea: Tensor Programs In\nL209@P2: Section 7 and Appendix H.4, we describe the\nL210@P2: Tensor Programs technique for deriving (rigor\x02ously) the infinite-width training dynamics of\nL211@P2: any abc-parametrization. The main insight of\nL212@P2: this approach is:\nL213@P2: When width is large, every activation vec\x02tor has roughly iid coordinates, at any time\nL214@P2: during training. Using Tensor Programs,\nL215@P2: we can recursively calculate such coordi\x02nate distributions, and consequently un\x02derstand how the neural network function\nL216@P2: evolves.\nL217@P2: The Tensor Programs technique was developed\nL218@P2: in a series of papers [49–52] that proved the architectural universality of the Neural Network-Gaussian\nL219@P2: Process (NNGP) Correspondence and the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) limits and showed how to\nL220@P2: compute the corresponding infinite-width kernels. In the Figure above, the NNGP kernel can be\nL221@P2: thought of as the “limit” of the first forward pass of a randomly initialized model; the NTK can be\nL222@P2: similarly thought of as the “limit” of its first backward pass. The mechanics of calculating such limits\nL223@P2: is 1) to write down the relevant neural network computation (e.g. the first forward pass in the NNGP\nL224@P2: case) as a principled composition of matrix multiplication and coordinatewise nonlinearities, called\nL225@P2: a Tensor Program, and 2) to recursively calculate the distribution of coordinates of each vector via\nL226@P2: what’s called the Master Theorem. In this paper, we follow the exact same recipe, where in 1) we just\nL227@P2: write down the entire SGD training instead of only the first step. More generally,\nL228@P2: To derive the infinite-width limit of any neural computation (e.g. SGD training),\nL229@P2: 1) express it as a Tensor Program, and 2) mechanically apply the Master Theorem.\nL230@P2: For example, we easily recover the (discrete-time) 1-hidden-layer mean field limit (Theorem 6.1).\nL231@P2: It readily applies to practically any neural architecture (e.g. ResNet and Transformers)7as well as\nL232@P2: many common variants of SGD; however, in this paper, for pedagogical clarity, we only focus on\nL233@P2: multilayer perceptrons. The generality of our approach allows us to easily adapt to settings outside the\nL234@P2: traditional (CIFAR10-style) supervised classification, such as the Word2Vec and few-shot learning\nL235@P2: tasks in this paper, or reinforcement learning and image generation outside of our scope.\nL236@P2: 7\nL237@P2: e.g. by extending the example programs of [49, 51], which express only the first forward and backward\nL238@P2: passes, into the entire training computation.\nL239@P2-3: 3Our Contributions\nL240@P3: 1. Formulate a natural space of NN parametrizations (abc-parametrizations).\nL241@P3: 2. Prove Dynamical Dichotomy: Any nontrivial stable abc-parametrization yields either feature\nL242@P3: learning or kernel limits, but not both.\nL243@P3: 3. Show both NTK and standard parametrizations yield kernel limits and propose the Maximal\nL244@P3: Update Parametrization (µP) , which admits maximal feature learning in a suitable sense.\nL245@P3: 4. Use Tensor Programs to derive the infinite-width limit of µP and, more generally, the limit\nL246@P3: of any abc-parametrization. We verify our theory using extensive experiments.\nL247@P3: 5. Show the µP limit outperforms both NNGP/NTK baselines and finite networks on 1)\nL248@P3: Word2Vec and 2) Omniglot few-shot learning, trained via first-order MAML.\nL249@P3: Tensor Programs Series While this work is self-contained, it is positioned as the 4th paper in the\nL250@P3: series, following Yang [49, 51, 52]. We do not extend the Tensor Programs machinery further here,\nL251@P3: but instead extract the first major payoff of the foundation laid in the earlier works. In fact, this paper\nL252@P3: is the original motivation for this series; for a short history, see Appendix A.\nL253@P3: 2 Related Works\nL254@P3: Comparison with Mean Field Limits For 1-hidden-layer MLP, the mean field limit [11, 30, 43,\nL255@P3: 45] is equivalent to the µP limit modulo the symmetry of Eq. (5) (see Section 3.1). Several works also\nL256@P3: proposed different versions of mean field frameworks for deeper MLPs [5, 15, 34, 35, 46]. However,\nL257@P3: they did not consider the typical Gaussian N (0, 1/n) random initialization (or the appropriately\nL258@P3: rescaled version in their respective parametrizations)8, which has a Central-Limit effect as opposed\nL259@P3: to a Law-of-Large-Numbers effect. For example, [5, 35] can cover the case of N (0, 1/n2), instead\nL260@P3: of N (0, 1/n), initialization, which in fact causes the function to be stuck at initialization. See\nL261@P3: Appendix E for more explanations. Of these works, the mean field limit of [15] has the form\nL262@P3: most similar to what we derive here. There, as we do here, the coordinate distribution of each\nL263@P3: (pre)activation vector is tracked recursively. The main difference is, while [15] has an atypical\nL264@P3: initialization involving `2 regression, we consider the usual Gaussian N (0, 1/n) scheme. Such a\nL265@P3: (size n×n) Gaussian matrix in the middle of the network has a distinctly different effect, more similar\nL266@P3: to that of a Gaussian matrix in the usual NNGP/NTK calculation,9than the “mean field” matrices\nL267@P3: considered in [15] and previous works [5, 34, 35, 46], which has an “integral kernel” effect that is the\nL268@P3: straightforward generalization of matrices to function spaces. Nevertheless, discrete time versions of\nL269@P3: the 1-hidden-layer mean field limit and of many of the multilayer limits (such as [15, 35]) can be\nL270@P3: derived directly by writing the corresponding initialization and training inside a Tensor Program and\nL271@P3: applying the Master Theorem (Theorem 7.4).\nL272@P3: Discrete- vs Continuous-Time Gradient Descent At a high level, there are two natural limits\nL273@P3: of neural networks training dynamics: large-width and continuous-time. Most prior works on\nL274@P3: infinite-width limits of neural networks also took the continuous-time limit simultaneously, e.g.\nL275@P3: [11, 26, 30, 43, 45]. In contrast, here we only take the large width limit, so that gradient descent stays\nL276@P3: discrete-time. Then the results of these prior works can be recovered by taking another continuous\x02time limit. From a practical perspective, the continuous-time limit is often unnatural, e.g. 1) because\nL277@P3: the step size is usually as large as possible to speed up training, 2) because of the task (such as\nL278@P3: reinforcement learning), or 3) because of the importance of hyperparameters like batch size that are\nL279@P3: hidden away in such limits. On the theory side, taking the continuous-time limit can create issues\nL280@P3: with 1) well-posedness and 2) existence and uniqueness of the resulting ODE/PDE. While they can\nL281@P3: sometimes be proved to hold, they are artifacts of the continuous-time limit, as the corresponding\nL282@P3: questions for the discrete time evolution are trivial, and thus not relevant to the behavior of real\nL283@P3: networks.\nL284@P3: 8\nL285@P3: In fact, empirically we observe such Gaussian random initialization to be crucial to performance compared\nL286@P3: to the mean-field-style initialization in this literature.\nL287@P3: 9Actually, it is more similar to the Gaussian matrix in asymmetric message passing [6] in that care must be\nL288@P3: taken to keep track of correlation between W and W>.\nL289@P3-4: 4Technical Assumptions Earlier works on neural tangent or mean field limits (e.g. [11, 15, 26,\nL290@P4: 30, 35, 43, 45]) assume various forms of regularity conditions, such as 1) 0th, 1st, and/or 2nd\nL291@P4: order smoothness on the nonlinearity or other related functions, and 2) the support boundedness,\nL292@P4: subgaussianity, and/or PDF smoothness of initialization distributions. These are often either unnatural\nL293@P4: or difficult to check. In our work, the only assumption needed to rigorously obtain the infinite-width\nL294@P4: limit is that the nonlinearity φ has a polynomially bounded weak 2nd derivative and that the loss\nL295@P4: function has a continuous derivative w.r.t. the prediction (Assumption H.22). In particular, when\nL296@P4: we specialize to the 1-hidden-layer case and derive the discrete time version of the mean field limit,\nL297@P4: we cover the standard Gaussian initialization; in fact, we can allow any heavy-tailed initialization\nL298@P4: that can be written as the image of a Gaussian under a pseudo-Lipschitz function, which include\nL299@P4: nonsmooth PDFs and singular distributions.10 This generosity of technical assumptions is due to that\nL300@P4: of the Tensor Programs Master Theorems proven in [49, 51, 52].\nL301@P4: Training Time Many prior works (e.g. [4, 25, 30]) derived explicit time dependence of the con\x02vergence to infinite-width limit, so that a larger width can allow the network to stay close to the\nL302@P4: limit for longer. In this paper, our results only concern training time independent of width, since\nL303@P4: our primary objective is to investigate the limit itself and its feature learning capabilities. Moreover,\nL304@P4: recent evidence suggests that, given a fixed computational budget, it’s always better to train a larger\nL305@P4: model for a shorter amount of time [29], which validates the practical relevance of our limit mode.\nL306@P4: Nevertheless, it is possible to prove a quantitative version of the Tensor Programs Master Theorem,\nL307@P4: by which one can straightforwardly allow training time to increase with width.\nL308@P4: Classification of Parametrizations [10] pointed out that the weights move very little in the NTK\nL309@P4: limit, so that linearization approximately holds around the initial parameters, in contrast to the mean\nL310@P4: field limit (for 1-hidden-layer networks) where the weights move substantially. For this reason, they\nL311@P4: called the former “lazy training” and the latter “active training,” which are classified nonrigorously by\nL312@P4: a multiplicative scaling factor of the logit (similar to n\nL313@P4: −aL+1 in this paper). While these terms are not\nL314@P4: formally defined, they intuitively correspond to the kernel and feature learning regimes in our paper.\nL315@P4: From a different perspective, [31] observed that the NTK and mean field limit can be thought of as\nL316@P4: short and long time-scale regimes of the mean field evolution equations. Neither of the above works\nL317@P4: attempted to formally classify natural parametrizations of neural networks. In contrast, [48] studied a\nL318@P4: toy class of neural networks in the context of implicit regularization due to the scale α of initialization\nL319@P4: (which is closely related to logit multiplier of [10] noted above). They identified the α → ∞ limit (of\nL320@P4: the scale α, not of width) with the “kernel regime” and the α → 0 limit with what they call the “rich\nL321@P4: regime”. They showed that the former is implicitly minimizing an `2 risk while the latter, an `1 risk.\nL322@P4: They claim width allows the toy model to enter the kernel regime more naturally, but as we see in this\nL323@P4: work, both kernel and feature learning regimes are admissible in the large width limit of a standard\nL324@P4: MLP. Closer to our approach, [19] studied what amounts to a 2-dimensional subspace of the space of\nL325@P4: stable abc-parametrizations for L = 1. They proposed a notion of stability which is similar to the\nL326@P4: combination of stability and nontriviality in this paper. They characterized when the Neural Tangent\nL327@P4: Kernel, suitably generalized to any parametrization and playing a role similar to the feature kernel\nL328@P4: in this paper, evolves over time. However, to simplify the proofs, they assumed that the gradients\nL329@P4: for the different weight matrices are estimated using different inputs, a very unnatural condition.\nL330@P4: In contrast, here our results are for the usual SGD algorithm applied to MLPs of arbitrary depth.\nL331@P4: In all of the above works and most of existing literature, not much attention is paid to the feature\nL332@P4: learning capabilities of neural networks in the right parametrization, as opposed to our focus here. A\nL333@P4: notable exception is [12], which showed that the mean field limit, but not the NTK limit, can learn\nL334@P4: low dimension linear structure of the input distribution resulting in ambient-dimension-independent\nL335@P4: generalization bounds.\nL336@P4: Other Related Works [27] proposed a toy model to study how large learning rate can induce\nL337@P4: a neural network to move out of the kernel regime in Ω(log(width)) time. Since our dichotomy\nL338@P4: result only concerns training for O(1) time (which, as we argue above, is more practically relevant),\nL339@P4: there is no contradiction. [47] also noted that standard parametrization leads to unstable training\nL340@P4: dynamics. They then injected constants in the NTK parametrization, such as α/√n instead of 1/\nL341@P4: √\nL342@P4: n\nL343@P4: and tuned α in the resulting kernel. [2, 3] also observed the lack of feature learning in NNGP and\nL344@P4: NTK limits but, in contrast to taking the exact limit of SGD training as we do here, they proposed a\nL345@P4: deep kernel process as a way of loosely mimicking feature learning in finite-width networks. [17]\nL346@P4: 10We won’t expand further here, but it can be derived straightforwardly from the Master Theorem\nL347@P4: (Theorem 7.4).\nL348@P4-5: 5empirically observed that wider networks achieve better downstream performance with linear transfer\nL349@P5: learning, even though on the original pretraining task there can be little difference. We fix the input\nL350@P5: dimension d in this work, but one can also consider varying d with width n, e.g. [36, 38]. [28] proved\nL351@P5: a complexity separation between NTK and finite-width networks by showing the latter approximates\nL352@P5: a sort of infinite-width feature learning network. In the literature surrounding NTK, often there are\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2205.09653)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn43view1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: application/pdf; Number of pages: 55; Source: open({"ref_id":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2205.09653","lineno":null}); Total lines: 10619\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n (https://www.jmlr.org/papers/volume25/23-1225/23-1225.pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn43view2\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: application/pdf; Number of pages: 65; Source: open({"ref_id":"https://www.jmlr.org/papers/volume25/23-1225/23-1225.pdf","lineno":null}); Total lines: 9653\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nCommunications on Pure and Applied Mathematics |Wiley Online Library (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/cpa.22200)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn43view3\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; Content type: text/html; Source: open({"ref_id":"https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/cpa.22200","lineno":null}); Total lines: 1460\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "# Three inputs: a rigorous local-time joint width and gradient-flow limit\n\n## Result and scope\n\nFix three inputs \\(x_1,x_2,x_3\\in\\mathbb R^d\\), with\n\\[\n\\frac{\\|x_a\\|_2^2}{d}=1,\\qquad\nx_a\\ne x_b,\\quad x_a\\ne -x_b\\quad(a\\ne b),\\qquad\nG_{ab}=\\frac{x_a^\\top x_b}{d}.\n\\]\nBecause the norms agree, the conditions on \\(x_a,x_b\\) are exactly pairwise nonparallelity. The \\(3\\times3\\) matrix \\(G\\) is positive semidefinite with diagonal one. It may be singular. All input indices and input sums below range over \\(1,2,3\\).\nLet \\(y_1,y_2,y_3\\in\\{-1,1\\}\\). There is \\(T_0>0\\) such that the ordinary, layer-scaled full-batch GD specified below has a unique autonomous population limit on \\([0,T_0]\\), for every sequence of positive steps \\(\\eta_n\\to0\\). In particular this includes the earlier regime \\(\\eta_n\\sqrt n\\to0\\) and the original choice \\(n^{-2}\\). The proof works for all such triples and all eight label sign patterns. Moreover, the existence time \\(T_0\\) can be chosen uniformly over these triples and labels: its construction uses only three inputs, \\(|G_{ab}|\\le1\\), and the fixed activation and initialization scales. It does not depend on width, step size, input dimension, or a lower bound on a Gram eigenvalue.\n\nPredictions, residuals, loss, and all entries of all three kernel blocks converge in probability uniformly on this interval. The joint three-input hidden path laws converge with their second moments in the uniform path norm. Integrated squared hidden velocities converge. The joint empirical averages of any fixed finite collection of continuous, globally Lipschitz forward/adjoint probes, with bounded products when a product instruction is used, converge to their corresponding population expectations, uniformly in time. Unbounded first-layer backward fields and their quadratic contractions are included by the tail argument below.\n\nHere a fixed action probe is a finite calculation from the current fields and constants using fixed globally Lipschitz coordinate functions, linear combinations, and appropriately typed calls to \\(W^{(2)}\\) and its adjoint. A product instruction is allowed when both varying factors are uniformly bounded, for example after clipping. Final same-layer joint measurements can be continuous with at most quadratic growth. Probe length and functions do not grow with width or depend on the learning rate.\n\nOn a possibly smaller interval \\((0,T_*]\\), each input's preactivations move in both hidden layers, all three \\(3\\times3\\) kernel blocks are positive definite, the total kernel is nonconstant, loss strictly decreases, and \\(\\arctan\\) has positive best-affine-fit error on each hidden preactivation distribution.\n\n**Scope:** this is a local-time theorem. A convergence theorem on every finite horizon is not claimed for arbitrary input geometry. \\(T_0>0\\) is chosen before taking width and learning-rate limits and does not shrink in those limits. The strictly positive feature-learning coefficients and the possibly smaller activity interval \\(T_*\\) may depend on the fixed input geometry and labels.\n\nThe inputs are fixed while taking the width and learning-rate limits. A common existence interval across configurations is not a claim of a convergence rate uniform over configurations that themselves change with width.\n\n## The finite network and its exact updates\n\nThe dimensions are \\(W^{(1)}\\in\\mathbb R^{n\\times d}\\), \\(W^{(2)}\\in\\mathbb R^{n\\times n}\\), and \\(W^{(3)}\\in\\mathbb R^n\\). For input \\(a=1,2,3\\), use\n\\[\nz_a^{(1)}=\\frac{W^{(1)}x_a}{\\sqrt d},\\quad\nh_a^{(1)}=\\phi(z_a^{(1)}),\\quad\nz_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},\\quad\nh_a^{(2)}=\\phi(z_a^{(2)}),\\qquad \\phi=\\arctan,\n\\]\n\\[\nf_{n,a}=\\frac{(W^{(3)})^\\top h_a^{(2)}}n,\\qquad\nr_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,\\qquad L_n=\\sum_{a=1}^3r_{n,a}^2.\n\\]\nThe summed loss retains the earlier factors of two; using the average of the three losses divides the speed by three.\n\nAll initialization entries are independent:\n\\[\nW_{0,ij}^{(1)}\\sim N(0,1),\\quad\nW_{0,ji}^{(2)}\\sim N(0,1/n),\\quad\nW_{0,j}^{(3)}\\sim N(0,1/n^2).\n\\]\nConsequently each first-layer root triple is \\(N(0,G)\\), independently over neuron index \\(i\\). The three preactivations for one neuron may be correlated and may satisfy an exact linear relation. This is permitted.\n\nSet\n\\[\n\\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\\odot\\phi'(z_a^{(2)}),\\qquad\n\\delta_a^{(1)}=\\phi'(z_a^{(1)})\\odot(W^{(2)})^\\top\\delta_a^{(2)}.\n\\]\nThus \\(\\delta_a^{(\\ell)}=n\\,\\partial f_{n,a}/\\partial z_a^{(\\ell)}\\).\nIn the displayed rescaled parameterization the layer learning-rate multipliers are \\(n,1,n\\). The exact first-weight update is\n\\[\nW_{k+1}^{(1)}\n=W_k^{(1)}-\\frac{2\\eta_n}{\\sqrt d}\n\\sum_{b=1}^3r_{n,b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(1)}x_b^\\top.\n\\]\nMultiplication by \\(x_a/\\sqrt d\\) gives\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\nz_{a,k+1}^{(1)}\n&=z_{a,k}^{(1)}\n-2\\eta_n\\sum_bG_{ab}r_{n,b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(1)},\\\\\nW_{k+1}^{(2)}\n&=W_k^{(2)}-\\frac{2\\eta_n}{n}\n\\sum_b r_{n,b,k}\\delta_{b,k}^{(2)}(h_{b,k}^{(1)})^\\top,\\\\\nW_{k+1}^{(3)}\n&=W_k^{(3)}-2\\eta_n\\sum_b r_{n,b,k}h_{b,k}^{(2)} .\n\\end{aligned}\\tag{M1}\n\\]\nUse physical time \\(t=k\\eta_n\\), linearly interpolate these parameters, and recompute the forward pass at intermediate times.\n\nThe whole effect of input geometry on first-layer training is \\(G\\): training on input \\(b\\) moves input \\(a\\)'s preactivation with coefficient \\(G_{ab}\\). If \\(Gc=0\\), then \\(\\sum_a c_a z_a^{(1)}=0\\) initially and every update preserves this relation. No inverse of \\(G\\) is used.\n\nThe finite continuous flow has the kernel blocks\n\\[\nK^{(1)}_{n,ab}\n=G_{ab}\\frac{(\\delta_a^{(1)})^\\top\\delta_b^{(1)}}n,\\quad\nK^{(2)}_{n,ab}\n=\\frac{(h_a^{(1)})^\\top h_b^{(1)}}n\n \\frac{(\\delta_a^{(2)})^\\top\\delta_b^{(2)}}n,\\quad\nK^{(3)}_{n,ab}\n=\\frac{(h_a^{(2)})^\\top h_b^{(2)}}n .\n\\tag{M2}\n\\]\nDifferentiation gives \\(\\dot f_n=-2K_n r_n\\) and \\(\\dot L_n=-4r_n^\\top K_n r_n\\), where \\(K_n\\) is the sum of the blocks. Each block is positive semidefinite: it is the Gram matrix of the corresponding scaled parameter gradients. Individual residual magnitudes need not decrease; their squared sum does.\n\n## Why a new stability estimate is needed\n\nWith \\(F(s)=s+s^3/3\\), the componentwise transformation \\(X_a^{(1)}=F(Z_a^{(1)})\\) gives\n\\[\n\\dot X_a^{(1)}\n=-2\\sum_bG_{ab}r_b\n\\frac{1+(Z_a^{(1)})^2}{1+(Z_b^{(1)})^2}\n(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_b^{(2)}.\n\\tag{M3}\n\\]\nIf the inputs are mutually orthogonal, only diagonal terms occur and the ratios cancel. Otherwise nonzero cross-input terms remain and their ratios are unbounded. We therefore work with the original preactivations and prove a uniform tail estimate for the backward fields.\n\nThe exact limiting equations will be\n\\[\nH_a^{(1)}=\\phi(Z_a^{(1)}),\\quad\nZ_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}H_a^{(1)},\\quad\nH_a^{(2)}=\\phi(Z_a^{(2)}),\\quad\nf_a=\\mathbb E[W^{(3)}H_a^{(2)}],\\quad r_a=f_a-y_a,\n\\]\n\\[\n\\delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\\phi'(Z_a^{(2)}),\\qquad\n\\delta_a^{(1)}=\\phi'(Z_a^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_a^{(2)},\n\\]\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\dot Z_a^{(1)}&=-2\\sum_bG_{ab}r_b\\delta_b^{(1)},\\\\\n\\dot W^{(2)}&=-2\\sum_b r_b\\delta_b^{(2)}\\otimes H_b^{(1)},\\\\\n\\dot W^{(3)}&=-2\\sum_b r_bH_b^{(2)}.\n\\end{aligned}\\tag{M4}\n\\]\nHere \\((u\\otimes v)V=u\\,\\mathbb E[vV]\\). This definition displays the normalization that replaces the finite factor \\(1/n\\). First-layer and second-layer coordinates belong to separate populations. \\(W^{(2)}\\) acts from the former to the latter, and its adjoint acts in reverse. Every expectation pairs coordinates of the same population.\n\nThe initial root triple is \\(N(0,G)\\), the population readout is zero, and the initial action \\(W_0^{(2)}\\) is the joint forward-and-transpose limit of the initial Gaussian matrix, constructed next. The state consists of three first-layer fields and the shared matrix action and readout field. It has finitely many kinds of state objects, not finitely many scalar degrees of freedom.\n\n## The fixed-mesh construction and the new tail lemma\n\nWe use the following finite Gaussian fact. For a fixed finite calculation from iid jointly Gaussian coordinate tuples, independent of a Gaussian matrix of entry variance \\(1/n\\), with polynomially bounded coordinate functions and finitely many calls to that matrix and its transpose, every same-layer joint empirical average of a continuous polynomially bounded measurement has a deterministic limit. The limiting action is Gaussian randomness plus the responses forced by earlier opposite-direction calls. The precise response coefficients and their derivative convention are given and used in the appendix below. This is the specialization of Theorem 2.10 and Box 1 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685). Its hypotheses hold here: after fixing population scalar coefficients, each instruction is polynomially bounded, and the training instructions whose derivatives are used below are smooth with polynomially bounded derivatives, as functions of finitely many Gaussian variables. The initial tuple covariance may be singular; represent it as a fixed linear image of independent Gaussian roots. No claim of iid finite coordinates after matrix reuse is used.\n\nFor completeness, the underlying conditioning mechanism is as follows. If previous calls have revealed \\(W_0^{(2)}V=Y\\) and \\((W_0^{(2)})^\\top U=R\\), then the remaining Gaussian matrix is an independent Gaussian projected onto the directions orthogonal to the columns of \\(U\\) and \\(V\\). Its conditional mean obeys both recorded equations. A new product is the response fixed by these equations plus that projected Gaussian applied to the unexplored part of the new input. Gaussian integration by parts identifies the response coefficients. The finite Gaussian theorem makes this induction valid also with dependent query directions. We explicitly rely on this stated finite-program result, not on a claim that the infinite-time or growing-program-length theorem already follows from it.\n\nThese finite limits can be realized on two common population coordinate spaces. Here are the details relevant to the subsequent continuous-time argument. Take all the programs needed for rational meshes, their finite unions, and a countable closure under rational linear combinations, the coordinate operations in the proof, and bounded Lipschitz approximations. Their finite joint laws are consistent, so they can be realized jointly. Take the mean-square closure of their linear spans on each layer. Exact finite linear identities pass to these laws.\n\nA fixed sufficiently large \\(M\\) satisfies\n\\[\n\\mathbb P(\\|W_0^{(2)}\\|_{\\rm op}>M)\n\\le2\\,9^{2n}e^{-nM^2/8}\\longrightarrow0.\n\\tag{M5}\n\\]\nIndeed two \\(1/4\\)-nets of the unit sphere each have at most \\(9^n\\) points, and every fixed bilinear form of the matrix is \\(N(0,1/n)\\). This finite operator inequality and joint second-moment convergence imply\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[(W_0^{(2)}V)^2]\\le M^2\\mathbb E[V^2]\n\\]\nfor every generated \\(V\\). Thus the action is well-defined on equal-in-mean-square variables and extends to the closure. The same reasoning applies to the transpose. The exact finite identity\n\\[\n\\frac{u^\\top W_0^{(2)}v}{n}\n=\\frac{((W_0^{(2)})^\\top u)^\\top v}{n}\n\\]\npasses to the limit and identifies the two actions as adjoints. Bounded coordinate multipliers and their required products belong to these spaces by clipping and mean-square approximation. This constructs the common bounded-operator realization used below; it is not an additional assumption.\n\nDefine population Euler for (M4) in this common realization, starting from the stated initialization. Its rank expansion is\n\\[\nW_k^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}\n-2\\Delta\\sum_{s<k}\\sum_b\nr_{b,s}\\delta_{b,s}^{(2)}\\otimes H_{b,s}^{(1)}.\n\\tag{M6}\n\\]\nThus each fixed mesh is a finite Gaussian calculation with deterministic population residuals and contractions. Iterative construction is causal: the current fields determine their expectations and then the next fields. Its realization agrees with the finite Gaussian law, because applying the same rank expansion reduces each action to an initial-matrix call and those deterministic contractions.\n\nThe appendix proves the essential new estimate:\n\\[\n\\sup_{\\Delta:\\,\\Delta\\le T_0}\\ \n\\max_{a,k:\\,k\\Delta\\le T_0}\n\\mathbb E\\exp\\!\\left(\nc\\left|(W_k^{(2)})^*\\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\\right|^2\n\\right)\\le C .\n\\tag{M7}\n\\]\nHere \\(T_0,c,C>0\\) do not depend on the mesh. In particular the squared tail beyond \\(R\\) is at most \\(C e^{-c'R^2}\\), after reducing \\(c'\\).\n\nThe appendix is a derivation, not an assumed light-tail condition. It controls the accumulated deterministic response coefficients on both layers. The second-layer derivatives stay bounded; the first-layer derivatives have a Gaussian exponential bound. A short-time induction closes these bounds uniformly in the number of Euler steps.\n\n## Population Euler converges to a unique flow\n"}]

We obtain that, when the number of parameters diverges, the training dynamics converge (in a precise sense) to the dynamics obtained from a gradient descent on an infinitely wide deterministic linear NN.\nL133: Moreover, even if the weights remain random, we get their precise law along the training dynamics, and prove a quantitative convergence result of the linear predictor in terms of the number of parameters. We finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear NNs and show that the linear predictors of the NN converge at an exponential rate to the minimal $$\\ell _2$$-norm minimizer of the\xa0risk.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nCommunications on Pure and Applied Mathematics |Wiley Online Library (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/cpa.22200)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn44view1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; Content type: text/html; Source: find({"ref_id":"turn43view3","pattern":"title"}); Total lines: 1460\nNo matching text found for "title"--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n (https://www.jmlr.org/papers/volume25/23-1225/23-1225.pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn44view2\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: application/pdf; Number of pages: 65; Source: find({"ref_id":"turn43view2","pattern":"gradient flow"}); Total lines: 9653\nL257@P3: to the F1 space considered by Bach (2017a) if σ is 1-homogeneous.\nL258@P3: Favorable theoretical properties of the Barron space have been derived in prior literature,\nL259@P3: including approximation guarantees and Rademacher complexity bounds (Barron, 1993;\nL260@P3-4: 4Neural Hilbert Ladders\nL261@P4: Bach, 2017a; E et al., 2022). The Ansatz suggests that vectors\nin a TP become asymptotically independent as well as identically distributed both at initialization\nas well as during training. Leveraging this, the framework allows for a mechanistic tracking of\nthe behaviour of such vectors by assigning a random variable Zv to represent the (asymptotically\nidentical) distribution of the coordinates of the vector v. This principle underlies the development of\nthe key theoretical result of the Tensor Program machinery — the Master Theorem — which can\nbe viewed as the compositional, non-linear generalization of the law of large numbers. It allows the\ntheoretical computation of infinite width limits of different quantities such as (pre-)activations or the\noutput of a neural network.\nWhile the following proposition also holds for other structured SSMs such as MAMBA, for clarity,\nwe first introduce the simpler S4 model.\nThe S4 recurrent layer. The S4 recurrent layer y1:L = fS4(u1:L; w = {B, C}) can be viewed as a\ndiscretization of a continuous-time SSM given by\nd\ndtxt = Axt + But\nand\nyt = Re[Cxt]\n(1)\n3--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAlex Atanasov (https://abatanasov.com/)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn45search0\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last month; We perform an extensive empirical study on realistic networks in the "maximal update parameterization" (μP) of Greg Yang (introduced by other authors under the name "mean field parameterization"). ... We show that not only do loss curves of realistically wide networks converge to a limiting behavior corresponding to the infinite width limit, but that the individual network outputs, internal representations, and dynamical phenomena also behave nearly identically across large widths.\n### Maximal Update (μP) Networks Are Consistent Across Widths At Realistic Scales\n\nWork with Nikhil Vyas, Blake Bordelon, Depen Morwani, Sabarish Sainathan, and Cengiz Pehlevan. We perform an extensive empirical study on realistic networks in the "maximal update parameterization" (μP) of Greg Yang (introduced by other authors under the name "mean field parameterization"). In this parameterization, networks can learn features even at infinite width. We study networks across realistic datasets for vision nets on (CIFAR-5m, ImageNet) and transformer models on (Wikitext-103, C4). Wider networks are seen to perform better, consistent with prior works. We show that not only do loss curves of realistically wide networks converge to a limiting behavior corresponding to the infinite width limit, but that the individual network outputs, internal representations, and dynamical phenomena also behave nearly identically across large widths. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nControlled LLM Training On Spectral Sphere | PDF | Matrix (Mathematics) | Mathematical Optimization (https://www.scribd.com/document/989691438/2601-08393v1)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn45search1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 7 months ago; Crawled: 7 months ago; We use operator norm to characterize how the norm of activations changes ... There is a technique called Maximal Update Parameterization (μP). Proposed by Greg Yang et al. in 2022 (arXiv:2203.03466), it unifies the initialization of weights and the setting of learning rates when expanding the width of a network into a specific style. As a result, hyperparameters such as learning rates adjusted in small models can be transferred as-is to much larger models. Hyperparameter tuning is one of the most expensive trial-and-error processes in large-scale learning. This can now be completed through experiments on small models.\n\nWhy is such a thing possible? It is because this style is designed to correspond to the limit where features are learned when the width is pushed to infinity—exactly the mean-field limit. Because there is a theory of limits, we know the range within which it is safe to extrapolate. This is an example of theory directly reducing costs, and multiple operators are already using it in actual production.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n(PDF) Tensor Programs IVb: Adaptive Optimization in the Infinite-Width Limit (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/372889019_Tensor_Programs_IVb_Adaptive_Optimization_in_the_Infinite-Width_Limit)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn45search8\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.1 years ago; Crawled: last week; 2.5 Maximal Update ................................... ... by adding “nonlinear outer products” to Tensor Programs. ...
