Abstract\nL132: This paper studies the infinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks (NNs) initialized with random parameters. We obtain that, when the number of parameters diverges, the training dynamics converge (in a precise sense) to the dynamics obtained from a gradient descent on an infinitely wide deterministic linear NN.\nL133: Moreover, even if the weights remain random, we get their precise law along the training dynamics, and prove a quantitative convergence result of the linear predictor in terms of the number of parameters. We finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear NNs and show that the linear predictors of the NN converge at an exponential rate to the minimal $$\\ell _2$$-norm minimizer of the\xa0risk.\nL134: ## 1 INTRODUCTION\nL135: The description of the training dynamics of (artificial) neural networks (NNs) in the infinite-width limit, has in recent years shed light on several aspects of deep learning theory, such as (i) the existence of well-posed limits, which suggests to interpret practical large scale models as approximations of those limits, (ii) the importance of the choice of scalings/parametrization\ue200cite\ue20217†1\ue201 when passing to the limit—since several well-behaved but fundamentally different limits can be obtained, and (iii) the characterization of the long-term behavior of the dynamics—such as global convergence or algorithmic regularization—which in turn helps understanding the learning abilities of\xa0NNs.\nL136: These aspects are rather well understood for two-layer NNs, but the theory is lacunary for deeper NNs. A description of the infinite-width dynamics is available for the Neural Tangent (NTP) and Integrable (IP) parameterizations (discussed below), but both limits exhibit a form of degeneracy such as a lack of feature learning.\nL137: In\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ], the Maximal Update Parameterization ($$\\mu$$ P)—which is in a sense intermediate between (NTP) and (IP) in terms of scale—was introduced and shown to preserve feature learning in the limit for certain architectures, such as fully-connected NNs, which suggests that $$\\mu$$ P is a natural case of study. However, the theoretical understanding of this limit is so far very limited, because this limit involves large random matrices in an intricate way.\nL138: In particular, the following fundamental questions are still open:\nL139:   * (i)\nL140: \nL141: Is the infinite-width limit of Gradient Descent (GD) a GD trajectory in some infinite-dimensional space?\nL142: \nL143:   * (ii)\nL144: \nL145: Does it admit a well-posed continuous-time limit?\nL146: \nL147:   * (iii)\nL148: \nL149: Does it converge to minimizers? And when several minimizers exist, can we characterize which particular solution it selects?\nL150: In this paper, we study the infinite-width limit of deep linear\ue200cite\ue20219†2\ue201 NNs under $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$, and we answer positively to all these questions. Throughout the paper, we focus on the three-layer case, although our tools and analysis could be extended to more layers (the main conceptual gap happens when going from two to three layers). The last section\xa0shows, without technical details, how our three-layer results read in the case of deep NNs with an arbitrary number of layers.\nL151: Our analysis of linear NN is intended as a step towards understanding the dynamics in the general non-linear case, for which the three questions above are still\xa0unresolved.\nL152: ### 1.1 Related work and other limits\nL153: The first analysis of wide NNs may be traced back to\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20220†7\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20221†34\ue201 ]. The dynamics of wide NNs were first studied in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20222†2\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20223†14\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20224†17\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20225†28\ue201 ] for the NTP (or related linear dynamics) and in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20226†11\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20227†33\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20228†35\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20229†39\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20230†41\ue201 ] for non-linear dynamics in two-layer NNs under $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$, which is known as mean-field parameterization in this case (see Remark\xa0\ue200cite\ue20231†2.1\ue201 for a description of these various parameterizations).\nL154: The importance of the choice of parameterization when passing to the limit was first highlighted in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20232†12\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20233†32\ue201 ] and systematically studied in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20234†23\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ]. Parameterizations akin to $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$ were previously empirically studied in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20235†21\ue201 ] as a natural extension of the two-layer mean-field parameterization and a fix to the degeneracy of IP using large initial learning rates was proposed in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20236†26\ue201 ].\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2205.09653)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn46view1\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: application/pdf; Number of pages: 55; Source: open({"ref_id":"turn43view1","lineno":0}); Total lines: 10619\nL0@P0: Self-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory of Kernel\nL1@P0: Evolution in Wide Neural Networks\nL2@P0: Blake Bordelon & Cengiz Pehlevan\nL3@P0: John Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences, Center for Brain Science\nL4@P0: Harvard University\nL5@P0: Cambridge MA, 02138\nL6@P0: blake_bordelon@g.harvard.edu, cpehlevan@g.harvard.edu\nL7@P0: Abstract\nL8@P0: We analyze feature learning in infinite-width neural networks trained with gradient\nL9@P0: flow through a self-consistent dynamical field theory. We construct a collection\nL10@P0: of deterministic dynamical order parameters which are inner-product kernels for\nL11@P0: hidden unit activations and gradients in each layer at pairs of time points, providing\nL12@P0: a reduced description of network activity through training. These kernel order\nL13@P0: parameters collectively define the hidden layer activation distribution, the evolution\nL14@P0: of the neural tangent kernel, and consequently output predictions. We show that the\nL15@P0: field theory derivation recovers the recursive stochastic process of infinite-width\nL16@P0: feature learning networks obtained from Yang & Hu with Tensor Programs [1].\nL17@P0: For deep linear networks, these kernels satisfy a set of algebraic matrix equations.\nL18@P0: For nonlinear networks, we provide an alternating sampling procedure to self\x02consistently solve for the kernel order parameters. We provide comparisons of the\nL19@P0: self-consistent solution to various approximation schemes including the static NTK\nL20@P0: approximation, gradient independence assumption, and leading order perturbation\nL21@P0: theory, showing that each of these approximations can break down in regimes\nL22@P0: where general self-consistent solutions still provide an accurate description. Lastly,\nL23@P0: we provide experiments in more realistic settings which demonstrate that the loss\nL24@P0: and kernel dynamics of CNNs at fixed feature learning strength is preserved across\nL25@P0: different widths on a CIFAR classification task.\nL26@P0: 1 Introduction\nL27@P0: Deep learning has emerged as a successful paradigm for solving challenging machine learning and\nL28@P0: computational problems across a variety of domains [2, 3]. However, theoretical understanding\nL29@P0: of the training and generalization of modern deep learning methods lags behind current practice.\nL30@P0: Ideally, a theory of deep learning would be analytically tractable, efficiently computable, capable\nL31@P0: of predicting network performance and internal features that the network learns, and interpretable\nL32@P0: through a reduced description involving desirably initialization-independent quantities.\nL33@P0: Several recent theoretical advances have fruitfully considered the idealization of wide neural networks,\nL34@P0: where the number of hidden units in each layer is taken to be large. Under certain parameterization,\nL35@P0: Bayesian neural networks and gradient descent trained networks converge to gaussian processes\nL36@P0: (NNGPs) [4–6] and neural tangent kernel (NTK) machines [7–9] in their respective infinite-width\nL37@P0: limits. These limits provide both analytic tractability as well as detailed training and generalization\nL38@P0: analysis [10–17]. However, in this limit, with these parameterizations, data representations are fixed\nL39@P0: and do not adapt to data, termed the lazy regime of NN training, to contrast it from the rich regime\nL40@P0: where NNs significantly alter their internal features while fitting the data [18, 19]. The fact that the\nL41@P0: representation of data is fixed renders these kernel-based theories incapable of explaining feature\nL42@P0: 36th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2022).\nL43@P0-1: arXiv:2205.09653v3 [stat.ML] 4 Oct 2022learning, an ingredient which is crucial to the success of deep learning in practice [20, 21]. Thus,\nL44@P1: alternative theories capable of modeling feature learning dynamics are needed.\nL45@P1: Recently developed alternative parameterizations such as the mean field [22] and the µP [1] param\x02eterizations allow feature learning in infinite-width NNs trained with gradient descent. Using the\nL46@P1: Tensor Programs framework, Yang & Hu identified a stochastic process that describes the evolution\nL47@P1: of preactivation features in infinite-width µP NNs [1]. In this work, we study an equivalent parame\x02terization to µP with self-consistent dynamical mean field theory (DMFT) and recover the stochastic\nL48@P1: process description of infinite NNs using this alternative technique. In the same large width scaling,\nL49@P1: we include a scalar parameter γ0 that allows smooth interpolation between lazy and rich behavior\nL50@P1: [18]. We provide a new computational procedure to sample this stochastic process and demonstrate\nL51@P1: its predictive power for wide NNs.\nL52@P1: Our novel contributions in this paper are the following:\nL53@P1: 1. We develop a path integral formulation of gradient flow dynamics in infinite-width networks in the\nL54@P1: feature learning regime. Our parameterization includes a scalar parameter γ0 to allow interpolation\nL55@P1: between rich and lazy regimes and comparison to perturbative methods.\nL56@P1: 2. Using a stationary action argument, we identify a set of saddle point equations that the kernels\nL57@P1: satisfy at infinite-width, relating the stochastic processes that define hidden activation evolution to\nL58@P1: the kernels and vice versa. We show that our saddle point equations recover at γ0 = 1, from an\nL59@P1: alternative method, the same stochastic process obtained previously with Tensor Programs [1].\nL60@P1: 3. We develop a polynomial-time numerical procedure to solve the saddle point equations for deep\nL61@P1: networks. In numerical experiments, we demonstrate that solutions to these self-consistency\nL62@P1: equations are predictive of network training at a variety of feature learning strengths, widths\nL63@P1: and depths. We provide comparisons of our theory to various approximate methods, such as\nL64@P1: perturbation theory.\nL65@P1: 1.1 Related Works\nL66@P1: A natural extension to the lazy NTK/NNGP limit that allows the study of feature learning is to calculate\nL67@P1: finite width corrections to the infinite-width limit. Finite width corrections to Bayesian inference in\nL68@P1: wide networks have been obtained with various perturbative [23–29] and self-consistent techniques\nL69@P1: [30–33]. Training large neural networks has become increasingly expensive, making parameter-efficient finetuning methods like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) essential tools for adapting pre-trained models to specific tasks. ... This paper introduces Maximal-Update Adaptation (μA), a theoretical framework that provides explicit rules for scaling learning rates across different LoRA configurations and enables direct transfer of optimal learning rates from LoRA to full finetuning. ... The lack of principled guidance for these scaling relationships has made LoRA tuning more art than science.\nSubmitted 05 Feb 2026\n\nen\n\n# Learning Rate Scaling across LoRA Ranks and Transfer to Full Finetuning\n\nNC\n\nNan ChenSoledad VillarSoufiane Hayou\n\n## Abstract\n\nLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a standard tool for parameter-efficient finetuning of large models. While it induces a small memory footprint, its training dynamics can be surprisingly complex as they depend on several hyperparameters such as initialization, adapter rank, and learning rate. In particular, it is unclear how the optimal learning rate scales with adapter rank, which forces practitioners to re-tune the learning rate whenever the rank is changed. In this paper, we introduce Maximal-Update Adaptation ($\\mu$μ A), a theoretical framework that characterizes how the "optimal" learning rate should scale with model width and adapter rank to produce stable, non-vanishing feature updates under standard configurations. $\\mu$μ A is inspired from the Maximal-Update Parametrization ($\\mu$μ P) in pretraining. Our analysis leverages techniques from hyperparameter transfer and reveals that the optimal learning rate exhibits different scaling patterns depending on initialization and LoRA scaling factor. Specifically, we identify two regimes: one where the optimal learning rate remains roughly invariant across ranks, and another where it scales inversely with rank. We further identify a configuration that allows learning rate transfer from LoRA to full finetuning, drastically reducing the cost of learning rate tuning for full finetuning. Experiments across language, vision, vision--language, image generation, and reinforcement learning tasks validate our scaling rules and show that learning rates tuned on LoRA transfer reliably to full finetuning.\n\n\n## AI Overview\n\n## Understanding Learning Rate Scaling in LoRA\n\nTraining large neural networks has become increasingly expensive, making parameter-efficient finetuning methods like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) essential tools for adapting pre-trained models to specific tasks. However, practitioners face a persistent challenge: the optimal learning rate for LoRA changes unpredictably when key hyperparameters like adapter rank are modified, forcing costly re-tuning. This paper introduces Maximal-Update Adaptation (μA), a theoretical framework that provides explicit rules for scaling learning rates across different LoRA configurations and enables direct transfer of optimal learning rates from LoRA to full finetuning.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nnewton Changelog (https://data.safetycli.com/packages/pypi/newton/changelog)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn47search2\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; - Change `SolverMuJoCo` joint-limit `jnt_solref` conversion so Newton force-space `joint_limit_ke` / `joint_limit_kd` match the configured limit stiffness/damping at the joint limit instead of MuJoCo\'s acceleration-space default. ... - Auto-scale `ViewerGL` contact arrows, joint axes, and COM markers by `Viewer.scene_scale`; to approximate the previous fixed sizes after `set_model()`, set `viewer.renderer.arrow_length_scale = 0.1 / viewer.scene_scale`, `viewer.renderer.joint_scale = 0.1 / viewer.scene_scale`, and `viewer.renderer.com_scale = 0.1 / viewer.scene_scale`.\n(2959)\n- Change `SolverMuJoCo` joint-limit `jnt_solref` conversion so Newton force-space `joint_limit_ke` / `joint_limit_kd` match the configured limit stiffness/damping at the joint limit instead of MuJoCo\'s acceleration-space default. Within the NFD framework, the representational capacity in the deep-and-wide limit is characterized by the reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) induced by the Neural Network Gaussian Process (NNGP) kernel associated with the limiting SDE.\n\n# Neural Feature Dynamics in Deep Networks\n\nUpdated 25 December 2025\n\n  * Neural Feature Dynamics is a mathematical framework that characterizes the evolution of learned features and gradients in deep networks as their width and depth increase.\n  * It employs maximal-update parameterization and depth scaling to maintain O(1) feature evolution, enabling robust hyperparameter transfer and consistent scaling law predictions.\n  * NFD highlights failure modes such as internal collapse in multi-layer residual blocks and recommends depth-aware learning rate corrections to stabilize training.\n\nNeural Feature Dynamics (NFD) refers to the formal mathematical framework that characterizes the evolution of learned features in deep neural networks, particularly within the regime where both width and depth of the architecture become large simultaneously. NFD provides an analytic lens on end-to-end feature learning, scaling behaviors, and the validity of critical assumptions such as gradient-independence, thereby offering principled insight into when and why deep networks obey or deviate from classic scaling laws (Yao et al., 24 Dec 2025).\n\n## 1. Foundations: Parameterization and Feature Learning Regimes\n\nThe development of NFD is tightly linked to the maximal-update parameterization (MuP), which prescribes weight and learning-rate scaling so that feature evolution remains nontrivial in the infinite-width limit. For an $L$L-layer ResNet of width $n$n, the original MuP framework ensures that the feature activations and their updates remain $O(1)$O(1) as $n \\to \\infty$n→∞, enabling reliable hyperparameter transfer across widths (Noci et al., 2024).\n\nDepth-MuP generalizes this to depth by re-scaling the residual branch by a factor $T/L$T/L, where $T$T is a time-horizon parameter, leading to update dynamics for the hidden states: $$h_e = h_{e-1} + \\frac{T}{L} \\frac{1}{\\sqrt n} W_e \\phi(h_{e-1}).$$h e\u200b=h e−1\u200b+L T\u200bn\u200b1\u200bW e\u200bϕ(h e−1\u200b). This scaling is essential for maintaining meaningful feature evolution as $L \\to \\infty$L→∞ and, in single-layer residual blocks, supports empirical and theoretical consistency across varying network depths (Yao et al., 24 Dec 2025).\n\n## 2. Derivation of Neural Feature Dynamics in the Joint Limit\n\nNeural Feature Dynamics rigorously describes the evolution of both forward activations and backward gradients in the joint limit $n, L \\to \\infty$n,L→∞. The discrete-layer, discrete-step stochastic training dynamics converge to a coupled forward–backward McKean–Vlasov stochastic differential system: $$\\begin{aligned}\ndh_t^{(k)} &= -n_c \\sum_{i=0}^{k-1} \\mathcal L\'(f^{(i)}, y^{(i)})\\, \\mathbb E[\\phi(h_t^{(k)}) \\phi(h_t^{(i)})]\\, g_t^{(i)}\\,dt + dW_t^{(k)}, \\\ndg_t^{(k)} &= -n_c \\sum_{i=0}^{k-1} \\mathcal L\'(f^{(i)}, y^{(i)})\\, \\mathbb E[g_t^{(k)} g_t^{(i)}]\\, \\phi(h_t^{(i)})\\,dt + d\\widetilde W_t^{(k)}.\n\\end{aligned}$$d h t(k)\u200b\u200b=−n c\u200bi=0∑k−1\u200bL′(f(i),y(i))E[ϕ(h t(k)\u200b)ϕ(h t(i)\u200b)]g t(i)\u200bd t+d W t(k)\u200b,\xa0d g t(k)\u200b\u200b=−n c\u200bi=0∑k−1\u200bL′(f(i),y(i))E[g t(k)\u200bg t(i)\u200b]ϕ(h t(i)\u200b)d t+d W t(k)\u200b.\u200b Here, $n$n 0 and $n$n 1 denote the continuum-depth features and their backpropagated gradients, while the stochastic terms are governed by Brownian motions with covariances matching neural kernel and gradient statistics.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDokumenttyp - MADOC (https://madoc.bib.uni-mannheim.de/view/types/research%3D5Fpaper.default.html)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn47search4\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: yesterday; Rossi, Elena ; Kötz, Jennifer ; Goatin, Paola ; Göttlich, Simone ORCID: 0000-0002-8512-4525 (2019) Well-posedness of a non-local model for material flow on conveyor belts.\nMannheim [Arbeitspapier]\nGöttlich, Simone ORCID: 0000-0002-8512-4525 ; Hante, Falk M. ; Potschka, Andreas ; Schewe, Lars (2019) Penalty alternating direction methods for mixed-integer optimal control with combinatorial constraints. Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a foundational algorithm for large-scale statistical learning and stochastic optimization. ... In this paper, we develop an efficient, model-agnostic methodology for constructing confidence regions from SGD trajectories that applies in both finite- and infinite-variance regimes.The procedure is based on a joint weak convergence result for the Polyak-Ruppert averaged estimator and an empirical second-moment normalizer constructed from stochastic gradients along the SGD trajectory.This joint limit yields a self-normalized statistic in which the leading tail-dependent scaling terms cancel.\nThe procedure is based on a joint weak convergence result for the Polyak-Ruppert averaged estimator and an empirical second-moment normalizer constructed from stochastic gradients along the SGD trajectory. This joint limit yields a self-normalized statistic in which the leading tail-dependent scaling terms cancel. We then use a subsampling calibration scheme to estimate the relevant critical values, avoiding explicit estimation of tail indices, slowly varying functions, or stable-law parameters. The resulting confidence regions are straightforward to implement and are asymptotically valid under both the finite- and infinite-second-moment regimes. \ue200cite\ue20216†Learn more.\ue201 L125: \nL126: [Button: Copy URL]\nL127: \nL128: Share a link\nL129: \nL130: Share on\nL131: ## Abstract\nL132: This paper studies the infinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks (NNs) initialized with random parameters. We obtain that, when the number of parameters diverges, the training dynamics converge (in a precise sense) to the dynamics obtained from a gradient descent on an infinitely wide deterministic linear NN.\nL133: Moreover, even if the weights remain random, we get their precise law along the training dynamics, and prove a quantitative convergence result of the linear predictor in terms of the number of parameters. We finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear NNs and show that the linear predictors of the NN converge at an exponential rate to the minimal $$\\ell _2$$-norm minimizer of the\xa0risk.\nL134: ## 1 INTRODUCTION\nL135: The description of the training dynamics of (artificial) neural networks (NNs) in the infinite-width limit, has in recent years shed light on several aspects of deep learning theory, such as (i) the existence of well-posed limits, which suggests to interpret practical large scale models as approximations of those limits, (ii) the importance of the choice of scalings/parametrization\ue200cite\ue20217†1\ue201 when passing to the limit—since several well-behaved but fundamentally different limits can be obtained, and (iii) the characterization of the long-term behavior of the dynamics—such as global convergence or algorithmic regularization—which in turn helps understanding the learning abilities of\xa0NNs.\nL136: These aspects are rather well understood for two-layer NNs, but the theory is lacunary for deeper NNs. A description of the infinite-width dynamics is available for the Neural Tangent (NTP) and Integrable (IP) parameterizations (discussed below), but both limits exhibit a form of degeneracy such as a lack of feature learning.\nL137: In\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ], the Maximal Update Parameterization ($$\\mu$$ P)—which is in a sense intermediate between (NTP) and (IP) in terms of scale—was introduced and shown to preserve feature learning in the limit for certain architectures, such as fully-connected NNs, which suggests that $$\\mu$$ P is a natural case of study. However, the theoretical understanding of this limit is so far very limited, because this limit involves large random matrices in an intricate way.\nL138: In particular, the following fundamental questions are still open:\nL139:   * (i)\nL140: \nL141: Is the infinite-width limit of Gradient Descent (GD) a GD trajectory in some infinite-dimensional space?\nL142: \nL143:   * (ii)\nL144: \nL145: Does it admit a well-posed continuous-time limit?\nL146: \nL147:   * (iii)\nL148: \nL149: Does it converge to minimizers? And when several minimizers exist, can we characterize which particular solution it selects?\nL150: In this paper, we study the infinite-width limit of deep linear\ue200cite\ue20219†2\ue201 NNs under $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$, and we answer positively to all these questions. Throughout the paper, we focus on the three-layer case, although our tools and analysis could be extended to more layers (the main conceptual gap happens when going from two to three layers). The last section\xa0shows, without technical details, how our three-layer results read in the case of deep NNs with an arbitrary number of layers.\nL151: Our analysis of linear NN is intended as a step towards understanding the dynamics in the general non-linear case, for which the three questions above are still\xa0unresolved.\nL152: ### 1.1 Related work and other limits\nL153: The first analysis of wide NNs may be traced back to\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20220†7\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20221†34\ue201 ]. The dynamics of wide NNs were first studied in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20222†2\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20223†14\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20224†17\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20225†28\ue201 ] for the NTP (or related linear dynamics) and in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20226†11\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20227†33\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20228†35\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20229†39\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20230†41\ue201 ] for non-linear dynamics in two-layer NNs under $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$, which is known as mean-field parameterization in this case (see Remark\xa0\ue200cite\ue20231†2.1\ue201 for a description of these various parameterizations).\nL154: The importance of the choice of parameterization when passing to the limit was first highlighted in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20232†12\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20233†32\ue201 ] and systematically studied in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20234†23\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ]. Parameterizations akin to $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$ were previously empirically studied in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20235†21\ue201 ] as a natural extension of the two-layer mean-field parameterization and a fix to the degeneracy of IP using large initial learning rates was proposed in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20236†26\ue201 ].\nL155: Our work has strong connections to\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ], which shows, essentially, that all the random vectors that are generated when running a finite number of GD steps on a (non-linear) NN converge jointly in law to a family of objects characterized by an abstract algorithm.\nL156: Because of the intricate dependency that arises between random matrices and random vectors, this limit “algorithm” is, unfortunately, more complex than its finite-width counterpart and hard to study beyond a few GD steps (in particular, it is non Markovian, i.e.,\xa0the state of the infinite width system at time $$t$$ is not enough to determine the state at time $$t+1$$).\nL157: One of our contributions is, for the particular case of linear NNs, to exhibit a simple and theoretically tractable structure in this limit.\nL158: From a technical viewpoint,\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ] relies on the technique of Gaussian conditioning, which originated in the field of statistical physics to describe TAP equations\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20237†8\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20238†9\ue201 ], while we use the method of moments which is another classical technique of random matrix theory that allows to easily obtain universality (i.e., our results apply for non-Gaussian initializations as well; note that the universality of the technique of\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ] was proved recently in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20239†24\ue201 ]) and rates that are quantitative in the width.\nL159: The random matrix statements of our work (in particular Proposition\xa0\ue200cite\ue20240†3.3\ue201 ) have thus their counterpart in the language of\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ]; by proposing an independent proof with different techniques, our purpose is to make the analysis self-contained as well as to shed a different light on the objects appearing in the limit. See also\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20241†27\ue201 ] for more links between random matrix theory and NN\xa0theory.\nL160: Another closely related work is\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20242†10\ue201 ], which introduces a closed system of equations\xa0describing the dynamics of infinite width (non-linear) NNs. These equations\xa0can be written in continuous-time as well, thus giving an answer the question (ii). The aforementioned work, however, which relies on tools from dynamical mean-field theory, is derived at a formal level with no explicit control of the error terms.\nL161: For the linear case,\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20242†10\ue201 ] writes a more specific system of equations\xa0that describes the same dynamics as ours, but our descriptions are of different nature: as in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20218†45\ue201 ], the system derived in\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20242†10\ue201 ] is non Markovian. In contrast, our description is a gradient flow dynamics—thus Markovian—and all the complexity that arises from the correlated random matrices is encoded in the initial state of the dynamics.\nL162: The simplicity of our limit system allows us in particular to study the large time behavior of the dynamics to answer question (iii).\nL163: Finally, there is a rich literature on the training dynamics of linear NNs.\nL164: Some works show that the optimization landscape is benign\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20243†6\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20244†15\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20245†19\ue201 ] (the latter studies the NTP and thus a dynamics that becomes linear in the large width case), other works study settings where the dynamics display a “saddle to saddle” behavior\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20246†22\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20247†29\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20248†31\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20249†40\ue201 ], and finally, a line of works studies the implicit bias of GD\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20250†4\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20251†30\ue201 ]; that is, which solution is chosen when the problem is underdetermined.\nL165: A common assumption in this literature is that the matrices are balanced at initialization, that is, $$W_{\\ell } W_\\ell ^\\top =W_{\\ell +1}^\\top W_{\\ell +1}$$ where $$W_{\\ell }$$ and $$W_{\\ell +1}$$ are the initial matrices in two consecutive layers of the NN. This assumption leads to simple dynamics\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20252†3\ue201 ], which remain tractable even in the infinite depth limit\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20253†13\ue201 ]. While the assumption covers the case of orthogonal initialization, it is not satisfied for standard i.i.d.\nL166: initializations, where $$W_{\\ell } W_\\ell ^\\top$$ and $$W_{\\ell +1}^\\top W_{\\ell +1}$$ are independent random matrices. The simplification that results from the “balancedness” assumption can be seen in our analysis from the fact that in Proposition\xa0\ue200cite\ue20240†3.3\ue201 , most of the terms in the right hand side would vanish, leading to a much simpler\xa0recursion.\nL167: Our analysis in the last section\xa0borrows ideas from\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20251†30\ue201 ] which shows a min-$$\\ell _2$$ implicit bias for linear NNs with the logistic loss. Note that linear NN do not always exhibit this type of implicit bias: there are subtle results for architectures that are not fully connected\xa0[\ue200cite\ue20254†25\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20255†37\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20256†38\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20257†44\ue201 ].\nL168: ### 1.2 Organization of the paper\nL169: In Section\xa0\ue200cite\ue20258†2\ue201 , we present our main results and illustrate them with numerical experiments. Section\xa0\ue200cite\ue20259†3\ue201 studies the structure of iterated products of large random matrices with random vectors, and we show that they can be expanded in a basis of random vectors. These objects are the building blocks of the GD iterations and these results are exploited in Section\xa0\ue200cite\ue20260†4\ue201 , which contains the proof of the infinite-width limit. In Section\xa0\ue200cite\ue20261†5\ue201 we study properties of the limit system.\nL170: Finally, in Section\xa0\ue200cite\ue20262†6\ue201 we describe the analogous results for multi-layer\xa0NNs.\nL171: ## 2 PRESENTATION OF THE MAIN RESULTS\nL172: This section\xa0presents a rigorous discussion of linear NNs under $$\\mu {\\rm {P}}$$ of width $$m$$, in the limit as $$m\\rightarrow \\infty$$. In the case of two-layer NNs, the analogous problem has been qualitatively understood in [\ue200cite\ue20226†11\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20227†33\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20228†35\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20229†39\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20230†41\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20263†43\ue201 ] (see also the reviews [\ue200cite\ue20264†5\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20265†18\ue201 , \ue200cite\ue20266†20\ue201 ]).\nL173: The first striking special feature of the two layers case is that there is a natural choice of the parametrization—which mathematically is represented by a suitable factor of $$m$$ in front of the output weights—that allows the parameters to remain nondegenerate and deterministic in the limit $$m\\rightarrow \\infty$$.\nL174: Under this parametrization, two-layer NNs can be interpreted as a Wasserstein gradient flow for the weights (also in the limit), and hence the problem as $$m\\rightarrow \\infty$$ is also a solution of a Wasserstein gradient flow (and in particular it can be written as a family of parabolic equations).\nL175: For NNs of more than two layers, several aspects of the previous analysis change. Firstly, as discussed in the introduction, it is not possible to find a natural parametrization (that is, a consistent rescaling of the three layers of weights) such that one expects them to remain nondegenerate or deterministic in the limit $$m\\rightarrow \\infty$$.\nL176: In fact, as we will also see a posteriori, with the right choice of parametrization outlined in subsections\xa0\ue200cite\ue20267†2.1\ue201 and \ue200cite\ue20268†2.2\ue201 below, the evolution of the entries of the intermediate layer is negligible with respect to their initialization size, but these small variations change significantly the output.\nL177: Due to this issue with parametrizations, it is essential in our analysis to consider randomized initial data, and to expect such random effect to survive in our limit system with some averaging\xa0effects.\nL178: Our limit system is expressed in a basis of independent, identically distributed gaussian random variables. In turn, its coefficients are obtained by solving an infinitely wide linear NN, which in the continuous-time limit can be represented as an explicit collection of\xa0ODEs.\nL179: ### 2.1 Setting\nL180: \nL181: Let $$\\tilde{h}^m$$ be a single output three-layer linear NN with input $$x\\in \\mathbb {R}^d$$, width $$m\\in \\mathbb {N}$$, and weights $$\\tilde{\\bm{U}}^m\\in \\mathbb {R}^{m\\times d}$$, $$\\tilde{\\bm{W}}^m \\in \\mathbb {R}^{m\\times m}$$, and $$\\tilde{\\bm{V}}^m \\in \\mathbb {R}^{m}$$:\nL182: $$$$\\begin{equation*} y = \\tilde{h}^m{\\left(x, \\tilde{\\bm{U}}^m, \\tilde{\\bm{W}}^m, \\tilde{\\bm{V}}^m\\right)} = \\sum _{i = 1}^m \\tilde{\\bm{V}}^m_i \\sum _{j = 1}^m \\tilde{\\bm{W}}^m_{ij} \\sum _{\\ell = 1}^d \\tilde{\\bm{U}}^m_{j\\ell } x_\\ell = \\langle \\tilde{\\bm{V}}^m, \\tilde{\\bm{W}}^m \\tilde{\\bm{U}}^m x\\rangle. \\end{equation*}$$$$\nL183: Given a smooth loss function $$\\mathcal {L}:\\mathbb {R}\\times \\mathbb {R}\\rightarrow \\mathbb {R}$$, we study the behavior of GD starting from a random initialization on the expected loss $$\\tilde{F}$$ defined as\nL184: \nL185: $$$$\\begin{equation*} \\tilde{F}^m{\\left(\\tilde{\\bm{U}}^m, \\tilde{\\bm{W}}^m, \\tilde{\\bm{V}}^m\\right)}:=\\int _{\\mathbb {R}^d\\times \\mathbb {R}}\\mathcal {L}(\\tilde{h}^m(x), y)\\, d\\rho (x, y). \\end{equation*}$$$$\n\nInfinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks (https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.16980)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn49academia12\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.8 years ago; This paper studies the infinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks initialized with random parameters. ... We obtain that, when the number of neurons diverges, the training dynamics converge (in a precise sense) to the dynamics obtained from a gradient descent on an infinitely wide deterministic linear neural network. Moreover, even if the weights remain random, we get their precise law along the training dynamics, and prove a quantitative convergence result of the linear predictor in terms of the number of neurons. We finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear neural networks and show that the linear predictors of the neural network converge at an exponential rate to the minimal $\\ell_2$-norm minimizer of the risk.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nINFINITE-WIDTH LIMIT OF (https://people.math.ethz.ch/~afigalli/papers-pdf/Infinite-width-limit-of-deep-linear-neural-networks.pdf)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn49search13\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.8 years ago; We finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear neural networks and show that the linear predictors of the neural network converge at an exponential rate to the minimal ℓ2-norm minimizer of the risk. ... The description of the training dynamics of (artificial) neural networks (NNs) in the infinite-width limit, has in recent years shed light on several aspects of deep learning theory, such as (i) the existence of well-posed limits, which suggests to interpret practical large scale models as approximations of those limits, (ii) the importance of the choice of scalings/parametrization1 when passing to the limit — since several well-behaved but fundamentally different limits can be obtained, and (iii) the characterization of the long-term behavior of the dynamics — such as global convergence or algorithmic regularization — which in turn helps understanding the learning abilities of neural networks.\nINFINITE-WIDTH LIMIT OF  \nDEEP LINEAR NEURAL NETWORKS\n\nLÉNAÏC CHIZAT, MARIA COLOMBO, XAVIER FERNÁNDEZ-REAL,  \nAND ALESSIO FIGALLI\n\n**Abstract.** This paper studies the infinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks initialized with random parameters. We obtain that, when the number of neurons diverges, the training dynamics converge (in a precise sense) to the dynamics obtained from a gradient descent on an infinitely wide deterministic linear neural network. Moreover, even if the weights remain random, we get their precise law along the training dynamics, and prove a quantitative convergence result of the linear predictor in terms of the number of neurons.  \nWe finally study the continuous-time limit obtained for infinitely wide linear neural networks and show that the linear predictors of the neural network converge at an exponential rate to the minimal ℓ2-norm minimizer of the risk.\n\n1. We systematically compute generalization properties of linear, nonlinear, and deep nonlinear networks for kernel matrices with heterogeneous entries.\nHere, we build upon this limit and provide a field theoretic formalism that covers the generalization properties of infinitely wide networks. We systematically compute generalization properties of linear, nonlinear, and deep nonlinear networks for kernel matrices with heterogeneous entries. In contrast to currently employed spectral methods, we derive the generalization properties from the statistical properties of the input, elucidating the interplay of input dimensionality, size of the training dataset, and variability of the data. We show that data variability leads to a non-Gaussian action reminiscent of a {\\phi }^{3}+{\\phi }^{4} theory. Using our formalism on a synthetic task and on MNIST, we obtain a homogeneous kernel matrix approximation for the learning curve as well as corrections due to data variability that allow the estimation of the generalization properties and exact results for the bounds of the learning curves in the case of infinitely many training data points.\n\n  * Image: Figure 1\n  * Image: Figure 2\n  * Image: Figure 3\n  * Image: Figure 4\n  * Image: Figure 5\n  * Image: Figure 6\n  * Image: Figure 7\n[Button: See 3 more figures]\n\n[Button: Close]\n\n[Button: Prev][Button: Next]\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDeep Learning Theory Lectures Overview | PDF | Mathematical Analysis | Applied Mathematics (https://www.scribd.com/document/975676141/Univ-Luxembourg-Lectures-June-2024-Notes)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn49search6\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.3 years ago; Crawled: 8 months ago; suppose we now consider a deep linear network with output dimension NL+1 = 1. ... 3 Analysis of Deep Neural Networks at Init## 3.3 GP Limit at Infinite Width and Finite Depth\ncentered Gaussian process with normalized Euclidean covariance\n\x10 \x11 1\nlim Cov z (L+1) (x), z (L+1) (x′ ) = x, x′ ,\nN1 ,...,NL →∞ N0\nwhich is actually independent of L. In this asymptotic limit, both the initialization and training dynamics of deep networks often simplify dramatically, revealing universal behaviors such as Gaussian process (GP) priors, deterministic training kernels, and phase diagrams of scaling regimes. ## Ordinal 2056 agent_message /root/three_input_limit

The precise theorem-level assessment is:

**What is stronger than fixed-step convergence.** A fixed-\(K\) statement concerns finitely many matrix calls and coordinate operations. Our theorem follows approximately \(T/\eta_n\) updates, which diverges, and proves uniform physical-time convergence to a unique solution of explicit current-state differential equations. It additionally controls the stated path laws, kernels and hidden velocities and verifies strict feature movement.

The elementary distinction is

\[
e_n(k)=\min\{k/n,1\}.
\]

For every fixed \(k\), \(e_n(k)\to0\). But with \(\eta_n=1/n\),

\[
e_n(\lfloor T/\eta_n\rfloor)\to\min\{T,1\},
\]

not zero. This is a schematic illustration, not our error estimate.

**Both iterated limits.** Joint convergence alone does not logically ensure either inner limit exists. For example,

\[
a(n,\eta)=\eta\sin n+n^{-1}\sin(1/\eta)
\]

tends to zero jointly as \(n\to\infty,\eta\to0\), whereas both respective inner limits fail.

In our particular model, however, both orders follow locally from short additional arguments using ingredients already established:

- **GF first:** for fixed \(n\), the finite-dimensional smooth GD dynamics converge to their finite-width flow. Compare that flow to the oracle by setting the fine-step defect \(\eta_n\) to zero in the existing stability argument. Then send width to infinity.
- **Width first:** for fixed \(\eta\), compare actual GD to its oracle on the same finite grid. The grid-point comparison has sampling and initialization errors but no fine/coarse mesh mismatch. The finite-program theorem therefore gives population Euler at that \(\eta\). Its convergence to population flow is precisely the established Euler Cauchy argument.

Thus both iterated limits give the same answer on the proved local interval, in the empirical/action/path sense—not as a literal limit of differently sized matrices. The original joint conclusion itself should not be presented as the sole logical reason.

**What autonomy adds.** The current state is a fixed list: three first-layer fields, the shared \(W^{(2)}\) action, and the readout field. Both the forward and adjoint actions are retained. This state determines subsequent evolution, with uniqueness; one does not need an ever-growing list of previous matrix queries.

That does **not** mean finitely many scalar degrees of freedom. The fields and operator can encode accumulated training information. Loss alone, or separate marginal neuron laws, are not asserted to be Markovian. Restartability is established along the constructed admissible states during the local interval, not globally for every arbitrary operator-valued initialization.

**What is not established.** There is no classification or necessity theorem for scaling, no uniquely permissible scaling, no universality over activations or initial laws, no global-time theorem for arbitrary geometry, and no guarantee for a growing number of inputs.

The existing relative learning powers remain \(n:1:n\) in the specified parameterization. Taking \(\eta_n=n^{-p}\), \(p>0\), refines the common clock; it does not discover different relative layer exponents. The initialization remains special, particularly the zero limiting readout.

Finally, this analysis identifies a stronger guarantee than the *premise of fixed-step convergence*. It does not establish historical novelty relative to all TP/μP literature, and it should not be presented as originating feature learning.

## Ordinal 2060 message assistant commentary
I found an important qualification: later tensor-program work already represents the infinite-width weights as operators with closed current-state updates. So that representation is not a new contribution either. The remaining distinction is the control needed to pass to continuous time and prove that finite-width GD approaches the same flow—not the discovery of feature learning or of an operator-valued state.

Payload:
Conclusion: the result does not force different width exponents from μP. It supplies a particular joint-limit justification; neither a new scaling classification nor literature-wide novelty follows.

The strongest newly verified comparison is TP IVb:

- Its §2.6.3, Definition 2.6.7, Propositions 2.6.8–2.6.9 and footnote 24 already construct a common \(L^2\) space, bounded initial-matrix operators, and adjoints. Theorem 2.7.1 then gives closed deep-μP state updates using these operators and feature/backpropagation vectors. Theorem 2.9.25 generalizes this to representable architectures. Consequently, the Hilbert-space/operator presentation itself should not be presented as new. [TP IVb, pp. 22–24](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814#page=22), [pp. 34–35](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814#page=34).
- These are discrete, fixed-step-index convergence statements. Section 1.1 holds training time constant while taking width infinite. Section 2.4.1 offers continuous-time intuition, not the requested joint μP limit. “Uniformized” in §4.6 means standardizing instruction types, not uniform estimates for growing training length. [TP IVb, p. 7](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814#page=7), [p. 16](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814#page=16), [p. 73](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814#page=73).

“Same axioms” is inaccurate in the important uniqueness comparison. TP IV’s Theorem E.5 requires maximal updates in every layer **and maximal initialization of the readout**. The latter requires the initial readout acting on the learned feature change to remain order one. Our vanishing population readout removes that contribution. Thus all-layer nontrivial learning does not invoke that uniqueness theorem with identical hypotheses. Its classification also has activation-specific assumptions, unlike our restricted arctan theorem. [TP IV supplement, §E.3](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf#page=7), [§I.1](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf#page=15).

Nevertheless, zero initialization is explicitly accommodated in modern μP conventions: TP V’s discussion immediately below Tables 8–9 allows zero initialization variance, and Appendix D.2 recommends zero output initialization in some settings. This supports calling ours a vanishing-readout/zero-readout μP-type variant; it does not prove its joint limit. [TP V, p. 23](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466#page=23), [p. 27](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.03466#page=27).

The extra obligation is clear: at physical time \(t=k\eta_n\), \(\eta_n\to0\) entails \(k\to\infty\). Fixed-program convergence does not alone provide the required mesh-uniform stability, tightness, or limit interchange. Conversely, observing no movement at fixed \(k\) when \(\eta_n\to0\) is just observing physical time tending to zero—not a contradiction with μP feature learning.

Continuous-time feature-learning limits themselves already have precedents. Bordelon–Pehlevan derives μP-equivalent gradient-flow DMFT, while explicitly acknowledging heuristic/non-rigorous steps. Chizat–Colombo–Fernández-Real–Figalli rigorously studies a deep-linear μP width limit and its subsequent continuous-time dynamics; those hypotheses and limit order differ from ours. [Bordelon–Pehlevan, §2 and Discussion](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10541946), [Chizat et al., Theorems 2.2–2.3 and §5](https://people.math.ethz.ch/~afigalli/papers-pdf/Infinite-width-limit-of-deep-linear-neural-networks.pdf).

Finally, gradient flow is not synonymous with the NTK regime. An evolving tangent kernel and order-one feature changes are consistent with μP. The NTK distinction is frozen-feature/fixed-kernel dynamics, not discrete versus continuous time. [TP IV, Theorems 3.5–3.7](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c.pdf).

Safe framing: “a rigorous local joint width–step limit for this nonlinear, vanishing-readout model, compatible with μP update scaling.” Establishing novelty beyond that requires a broader theorem-by-theorem comparison.

## Ordinal 2089 message assistant final_answer
No—not in the sense of forcing a new scaling beyond μP. The result is better understood as **a stronger dynamical justification for a particular μP-type model**, rather than a new scaling principle.

And after checking the later tensor-program papers, I would narrow the novelty claim further: even the representation using current fields and weight operators already exists there.

### The scaling is not the new message

In our notation, the three layer learning rates are
\[
\eta_n(n\kappa_1,\;\kappa_2,\;n\kappa_3),
\]
and one step represents physical time \(\eta_n\). Consequently, the learning rates **per unit of physical time** are
\[
\frac{\eta_n(n\kappa_1,\;\kappa_2,\;n\kappa_3)}{\eta_n}
=
(n\kappa_1,\;\kappa_2,\;n\kappa_3).
\]

Those are the same relative width powers as μP. Sending the common factor \(\eta_n\) to zero refines the time discretization; it does not change the relative layer scaling.

For example, choosing \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) makes the actual learning rates proportional to
\[
(n^{-1},\;n^{-2},\;n^{-1}).
\]
That looks different from \((n,1,n)\) until we remember that we also perform \(n^2\) updates per unit of physical time.

Our smaller readout initialization is a separate choice. We have **not** proved that the joint limit forces it. Also, “the same axioms as μP” needs care: μP’s strict maximality characterization includes maximal readout initialization, whereas our population readout starts at zero. The literature already accommodates limits obtained by suppressing initialization or learning rates; this is not a counterexample to its classification. [Tensor Programs IVb, Remarks 2.7.5 and 2.8.21](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814).

More conceptually, gradient flow and the NTK regime are different distinctions. Gradient flow means continuous optimization time. NTK means, in this comparison, a particular infinite-width regime with frozen features and a fixed prediction kernel. Continuous-time training can be feature-learning or lazy. μP identifies a feature-learning scaling; it is not merely a correction to NTK dynamics. [Tensor Programs IV](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2011.14522).

### What the extra mathematical obligation actually is

The relevant distinction is between these two guarantees:

“After any fixed number of updates, the finite network approaches an infinite-width network.”

“Over an entire fixed physical-time interval, increasingly wide networks with increasingly small steps approach one well-defined continuous learning process.”

The second does not follow just by replacing differences with derivatives in the first. To reach time \(T\), we need approximately
\[
k=\frac{T}{\eta_n}
\]
updates, and this number diverges.

A simple illustration: an error \(k/n\) vanishes as \(n\to\infty\) for every fixed \(k\). But if \(\eta_n=1/n\), reaching physical time \(T\) requires approximately \(nT\) updates, and that error becomes approximately \(T\), not zero. This is only an illustrative error sequence, but it exposes the missing implication.

Our proof supplies the additional control needed in the specified model: errors remain controlled as the number of updates grows, the population discretizations approach a unique continuous evolution, and finite-width GD approaches that same evolution. For example,
\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
|L_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t)-L(t)|
\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability},
\]
together with the stronger feature, kernel, and matrix-action conclusions we discussed.

This distinction was explicitly recognized in the original μP paper: it takes the width limit while retaining discrete time, and separately identifies existence and uniqueness of continuous-time equations as additional issues. [Tensor Programs IV, “Discrete- vs Continuous-Time Gradient Descent”](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2011.14522#page=4).

However, we should not inflate the other ingredients into new discoveries. Tensor Programs IVb already constructs initial weight operators, identifies their transpose with the corresponding adjoint, and writes closed updates for current weights and features. So “we can retain \(W^{(2)}\) and describe an autonomous infinite-width network” is not, by itself, new relative to the TP literature. [Tensor Programs IVb, §2.6.3 and Theorem 2.7.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2308.01814#page=22).

The remaining substantive work is proving that these continuous-time equations actually work: a solution exists, it is unique in the relevant class, and the finite systems converge to it over an interval—not merely that the equations can be written down.

### What broad message can we reasonably attach to it?

The strongest defensible message is:

> In this nonlinear deep-network example, the feature-learning width limit remains a genuine learning process when optimization time becomes continuous. Neither the continuum limit nor the removal of discrete steps forces the hidden layers to freeze.

That is a meaningful mathematical foundation. It allows us to treat the limiting network as a dynamical system whose properties can be investigated, rather than only as the output of a finite-step probabilistic calculation.

But this is **not a newly discovered phenomenon at the level of μP’s feature-learning principle**. Continuous-time μP-equivalent feature-learning equations already appear in Bordelon–Pehlevan’s dynamical mean-field work. [Self-Consistent Dynamical Field Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.09653). Rigorous infinite-width dynamics and a subsequent continuous-time limit were also established for deep **linear** networks by Chizat, Colombo, Fernández-Real, and Figalli. [Infinite-width limit of deep linear neural networks](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/cpa.22200).

The potentially distinctive contribution of our result is therefore the **particular rigorous nonlinear joint-limit theorem**, including its explicit nontriviality guarantees—not continuous-time feature learning in general. I have not established that this exact theorem is literature-first.

There is also a substantial difference in breadth. μP provides a classification and a scaling prescription across broad families of networks. Our current result concerns two hidden layers, arctan, a special readout initialization, three fixed inputs, and a short time interval. It establishes a sufficient construction, not a characterization of all possible constructions.

To reach a comparably broad message, we would need something further: a general criterion explaining when feature-learning width limits admit a well-defined continuous flow, or a consequence about learning that the existing descriptions did not reveal. For instance, proving that the evolving representations enable a particular generalization advantage would say more than proving that they move. Likewise, proving a new scaling is *necessary* would say more than showing one scaling works.

So I would frame the present result as:

**A rigorous local continuous-time foundation for a restricted nonlinear μP-type model—not a replacement for μP, not a new scaling law, and not yet a new general theory of what deep networks learn.**

## Ordinal 2096 message user 
can you generalize the non-local GF population limit result to a wide class (as wide as possible from the current arctan case) ? 


## Ordinal 2099 message assistant commentary
I’ll treat “non-local” as existence and convergence on every fixed finite time interval, not just a short initial interval. I’ll check which parts of the arctan argument extend to other activations, and whether the multiple-input setting creates a separate obstruction.

The final section proves strict activity and nonlinearity.\n\n## 1. Exact finite-width equations\n\nPut\n\\[\nC=nc,\\qquad \\langle u,v\\rangle_n=n^{-1}u^\\top v,\n\\qquad (u\\otimes_n v)z=u\\langle v,z\\rangle_n,\n\\]\nand write\n\\[\nq(x)=\\phi'(x)=\\frac1{1+x^2},\\qquad\nD=C\\odot q(S),\\qquad P=B^\\top D,qquad e=f_n-1.     \\tag{1.1}\n\\]\nThen \\(f_n=\\langle C,G\\rangle_n\\).  Direct differentiation gives\n\\[\n\\frac{\\partial f_n}{\\partial A_i}=\\frac1nq(A_i)P_i,\n\\qquad\n\\frac{\\partial f_n}{\\partial B_{ji}}=\\frac1nD_jH_i,\n\\qquad\n\\frac{\\partial f_n}{\\partial c_j}=G_j.             \\tag{1.2}\n\\]\nMultiplying the loss gradients by (T3) proves that the associated finite-width gradient flow is exactly\n\\[\n\\dot A=-2e\\,q(A)\\odot P,qquad\n\\dot B=-2e\\,D\\otimes_nH,qquad\n\\dot C=-2e\\,G.                                     \\tag{1.3}\n\\]\nThe three scaled tangent-kernel blocks are\n\\[\nK^A_n=\\|q(A)P\\|_n^2,qquad\nK^B_n=\\|H\\|_n^2\\|D\\|_n^2,qquad\nK^C_n=\\|G\\|_n^2,                                  \\tag{1.4}\n\\]\nwhere \\(\\|v\\|_n^2=\\langle v,v\\rangle_n\\).  Thus, for \\(K_n=K_n^A+K_n^B+K_n^C\\),\n\\[\n\\dot f_n=-2eK_n,qquad \\dot L_n=-4e^2K_n.          \\tag{1.5}\n\\]\nIn particular, \\(|e(t)|\\le |e(0)|\\) along the flow.\n\nDifferentiating \\(S=BH\\) will also be useful:\n\\[\n\\dot S=-2e\\left\\{\\|H\\|_n^2D+B[q(A)^2\\odot P]\\right\\}. \\tag{1.6}\n\\]\n\n## 2. The regularizing coordinate and dimension-free flow estimates\n\nDefine\n\\[\nF(a)=a+\\frac{a^3}{3},\\qquad X=F(A),\\qquad J=F^{-1},\n\\qquad h(x)=\\arctan J(x).                           \\tag{2.1}\n\\]\nSince \\(F'(a)=q(a)^{-1}\\), equation (1.3) becomes\n\\[\n\\boxed{\n\\begin{aligned}\nA&=J(X),& H&=h(X),& S&=BH,\\\\\nG&=\\arctan S,&D&=Cq(S),&e&=\\langle C,G\\rangle_n-1,\\\\\n\\dot X&=-2eB^\\top D,&\n\\dot B&=-2eD\\otimes_nH,&\n\\dot C&=-2eG.\n\\end{aligned}}                                    \\tag{2.2}\n\\]\nThis is exact, not a linearization.  Also\n\\[\nJ'(x)=q(J(x)),\\qquad h'(x)=q(J(x))^2,               \\tag{2.3}\n\\]\nso \\(J,h,q\\circ J\\) are globally Lipschitz; \\(|H|,|G|\\le a_0:=\\pi/2\\).\n\nFor two states define\n\\[\nd_n(\\theta,\\widetilde\\theta)\n=\\|X-\\widetilde X\\|_n+\n \\|B-\\widetilde B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}+\n \\|C-\\widetilde C\\|_n.                            \\tag{2.4}\n\\]\nOn a set on which \\(\\|B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}},\\|\\widetilde B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\\le M_B\\) and \\(\\|C\\|_\\infty,\\|\\widetilde C\\|_\\infty\\le M_C\\), the vector field in (2.2) is Lipschitz in (2.4), with a constant independent of \\(n\\).  Indeed,\n\\[\n\\|H-\\widetilde H\\|_n\\le\\|X-\\widetilde X\\|_n,     \\tag{2.5}\n\\]\n\\[\n\\|S-\\widetilde S\\|_n\n\\le a_0\\|B-\\widetilde B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\n  +M_B\\|X-\\widetilde X\\|_n,                       \\tag{2.6}\n\\]\nand\n\\[\n\\|D-\\widetilde D\\|_n\n\\le \\|C-\\widetilde C\\|_n\n +M_C\\operatorname{Lip}(q)\\|S-\\widetilde S\\|_n.  \\tag{2.7}\n\\]\nFurthermore\n\\[\n|e-\\widetilde e|\n\\le a_0\\|C-\\widetilde C\\|_n\n +\\|\\widetilde C\\|_n\\|S-\\widetilde S\\|_n,        \\tag{2.8}\n\\]\n\\[\n\\|B^\\top D-\\widetilde B^\\top\\widetilde D\\|_n\n\\le \\|B-\\widetilde B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\\|D\\|_n\n +M_B\\|D-\\widetilde D\\|_n.                        \\tag{2.9}\n\\]\nFinally \\(\\|u\\otimes_nv\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}=\\|u\\|_n\\|v\\|_n\\).  Substitution of (2.5)--(2.9) into (2.2) gives\n\\[\n\\|V_n(\\theta)-V_n(\\widetilde\\theta)\\|_{d_n}\n\\le L(M_B,M_C,\\|C\\|_n,\\|\\widetilde C\\|_n)d_n(\\theta,\\widetilde\\theta). \\tag{2.10}\n\\]\n\nThe required bounded set is invariant on each compact interval, uniformly in width on a high-probability initialization event.  From (1.5), \\(|e(t)|\\le |e(0)|\\), and hence\n\\[\n\\|C(t)\\|_\\infty\\le\\|C(0)\\|_\\infty+2a_0|e(0)|t.  \\tag{2.11}\n\\]\nEquation (2.2) then bounds \\(\\|B(t)\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\\), followed by \\(\\|X(t)\\|_n\\).  Explicitly,\n\\[\n\\|\\dot B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\n\\le2a_0|e(0)|\\|C\\|_n,\n\\quad\n\\|\\dot X\\|_n\n\\le2|e(0)|\\|B\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\\|C\\|_n.             \\tag{2.12}\n\\]\nNow \\(\\|C_n(0)\\|_n=O_{\\mathbb P}(n^{-1})\\), \\(\\|C_n(0)\\|_\\infty=o_{\\mathbb P}(1)\\), and \\(\\|X_n(0)\\|_n=O_{\\mathbb P}(1)\\). On every \\([0,T]\\), explicit Euler with mesh \\(\\delta\\), polygonally interpolated, satisfies\n\\[\n\\sup_{t\\le T}d_n(\\theta_n^\\delta(t),\\theta_n(t))\n\\le C_T\\delta                                             \\tag{2.14}\n\\]\non an event of probability tending to one.  To verify (2.14), the local defect is at most \\(\\frac12L_TM_T\\delta^2\\), where \\(M_T\\) bounds the vector field; the recurrence\n\\(r_{k+1}\\le(1+L_T\\delta)r_k+\\frac12L_TM_T\\delta^2\\) and discrete Gronwall give the assertion.  A stopped bootstrap keeps the Euler iterates in the slightly enlarged bounded set.\n\n## 3. The only nonclassical external theorem\n\nWe use the following exact specialization of Greg Yang, *Tensor Programs III: Neural Matrix Laws*, arXiv:2009.10685v3, Setup 2.2 and Theorem 2.10.\n\n**Fixed-program reused-Gaussian theorem.**  Fix a finite program whose\ninitial matrix has iid \\(N(0,1/n)\\) entries, independent of all initial\nvectors, whose initial vector coordinates are iid copies of a fixed jointly\nGaussian vector (the covariance may be singular), and whose instructions\nare coordinatewise polynomially bounded maps and finitely many applications\nof \\(B_0\\) and \\(B_0^\\top\\).  For every fixed finite list of program vectors\nand every polynomially bounded test \\(\\psi\\),\n\\[\n\\frac1n\\sum_{i=1}^n\\psi(Z_{n,i}^1,\\ldots,Z_{n,i}^r)\n\\longrightarrow\n\\mathbb E\\psi(Z^1,\\ldots,Z^r)\\quad\\text{almost surely}. \\tag{3.1}\n\\]\nThe limiting variables retain every reused-transpose response term; a transpose call is not replaced by an independent multiplication.\n\nThe full primary proof was checked.  Its Gaussian-conditioning step conditions the matrix simultaneously on all preceding \\(BY\\) and \\(B^\\top V\\) calls.  A simultaneous induction proves empirical-moment convergence and constructs a full-support core set.  At singular covariance, rank stability and zero stability show that a zero limiting innovation is eventually an exact finite-width linear dependence; a positive innovation is a high-rank projected Gaussian for which the proof establishes the required strong law.  This closes the singular-covariance case without assuming rank stability.  The theorem is only for one fixed finite parameterless program.\n\nWe do **not** use the paper's Appendix A.1 conditional almost-sure strengthening or Appendix A.2 mean-convergence proof.  Independent inspection found that the former is false for arbitrary varying conditioning sigma-fields and that the printed proof of the latter omits a uniform-integrability step.  Neither is needed here.  Nor do we import the paper's operator-norm citation; (2.13) was proved directly.\n\n## 4. One canonical action space and its autonomous flow\n\nThe continuum state must live on fixed spaces before a time integral or a\nrestart map can be discussed.  We construct those spaces once, from the\ninitial Gaussian action, rather than constructing unrelated GNS spaces at\nindividual times.\n\n### 4.1  Simultaneous cyclic realization of the initial action\n\nUse two sorts, right and left.  Let \\(\\mathscr P_R,\\mathscr P_L\\) be the\ncountable formal probe grammar generated by the right Gaussian coordinate\n\\(A_0\\), the left zero coordinate, rational linear combinations, formal\napplications of \\({\\bf B}_0,{\\bf B}_0^*\\), and coordinate maps from the\nfollowing countable library:\n\n* the exact maps \\(F,J,h,\\arctan,q\\),\n  \\((c,s)\\mapsto c q(s)\\), and\n  \\((a,p)\\mapsto q(a)^rp\\), \\(r=1,2\\);\n* clipping at every positive integer level; and\n* a countable algebra of bounded Lipschitz cylinder functions with rational\n  data which generates every finite-dimensional Borel sigma-field.\n\nThe grammar is closed again after every new coordinate map or matrix call.\nIt is countable, and every finite part is one fixed finite \\(\\mathrm{NETSOR}^T\\)\nprogram.  Apply (3.1) to every finite part and intersect the resulting\ncountably many probability-one events.  The limiting same-side finite joint\nlaws are consistent.  Kolmogorov extension therefore gives probability\nspaces \\((\\Omega_R,\\mu_R)\\), \\((\\Omega_L,\\mu_L)\\) carrying all formal\nright and left coordinates.  Because the bounded Lipschitz cylinder algebra\nis present, their spans are dense in\n\\[\n\\mathcal H_R=L^2(\\Omega_R,\\mu_R),\\qquad\n\\mathcal H_L=L^2(\\Omega_L,\\mu_L).                  \\tag{4.1}\n\\]\nRational coefficients make the grammar countable; multiplication by an\narbitrary fixed real is obtained in the \\(L^2\\) closure by rational\napproximation.  A particular finite tensor program may equivalently use\nthat real directly as part of its fixed coordinate map.\n\nFor a formal right probe \\(v\\), define \\(B_0v\\) to be the coordinate named\nby \\({\\bf B}_0v\\), and similarly on the left.  On the product coupling of\nall widths, (2.13) and Borel--Cantelli imply\n\\(\\|B_{0,n}\\|_{\\rm op}\\le M_0\\) eventually almost surely.  Passing to the\nlimiting squared norms gives\n\\[\n\\|B_0v\\|_{\\mathcal H_L}\\le M_0\\|v\\|_{\\mathcal H_R}. \\tag{4.2}\n\\]\nThus zero-norm relations are respected and \\(B_0\\) extends uniquely to all\nof \\(\\mathcal H_R\\).  Passing to the limit in\n\\(\\langle u,B_{0,n}v\\rangle_n=\\langle B_{0,n}^\\top u,v\\rangle_n\\)\nproves that the named transpose action is its Hilbert adjoint.  The\nZHat/ZDot rule in (3.1) is what determines these actions; in particular the\nadjoint is not an independent Gaussian operator.\n\nPut \\(X_0=F(A_0)\\in\\mathcal H_R\\), \\(C_0=0\\in\\mathcal H_L\\). ## Ordinal 2169 message assistant commentary
There is a useful extension: the global one-input argument does not actually need arctan’s special formula, or even monotonicity. It can use a scalar auxiliary flow that works for a broad class of smooth activations.

The input geometry is a separate issue. The same construction works for any fixed number of orthogonal inputs, but it does not yet turn the arbitrary-angle three-input theorem into an all-time result.

The assertion is uniform over a compact \\(W_2\\)-family.\n\n**Proof.**  \\(W_2\\) convergence is weak convergence plus uniform integrability of \\(a^2+p^2\\).  On \\(|p|\\le M\\), truncate further to a compact set and approximate \\(\\Psi\\) by bounded continuous functions. Exact GD versus the finite-width flow\n\nIt remains to replace the auxiliary Euler schemes by the exact GD specified in the theorem.  Let\n\\[\nR=-2eB^*D.                                          \\tag{7.1}\n\\]\nOne raw GD step is \\(A^+=A+\\eta q(A)R\\); the \\(B,C\\) updates are exactly the Euler updates of (2.2). With \\(\\eta=\\eta_n=n^{-2}\\), (7.5) tends to zero.\n\nWe linearly interpolate the parameter iterates and recompute all network\nnodes from those interpolated parameters. Then\n\\[\nV_{A,k}=-2e_kq(A_k)P_k,\\qquad\nV_{B,k}=-2e_kD_k\\otimes_nH_k,                     \\tag{7.6}\n\\]\nand, at \\(t=k\\eta+s\\), \\(0\\le s\\le\\eta\\),\n\\[\n\\partial_tS(t)=V_{B,k}\\arctan(A_k+sV_{A,k})\n+(B_k+sV_{B,k})q(A_k+sV_{A,k})V_{A,k}.            \\tag{7.7}\n\\]\nThe bounded-state estimates give\n\\[\n\\sup_{k\\eta\\le T}\\{\\|V_{A,k}\\|_n+\n\\|V_{B,k}\\|_{\\rm op}+\\|\\partial_tS\\|_n\\}\\le C_T  \\tag{7.8}\n\\]\non the stopped event; (7.5) closes the stop.\n\nIt remains to pass the squared slopes, where a fourth-moment shortcut would\nbe invalid.  Let \\(\\widehat\\mu_{n,k}\\) be the empirical law of\n\\((A_k^{\\rm GD},P_k^{\\rm GD})\\).  Equations (7.5), (6.4), and (6.9) give\n\\[\n\\max_{k\\eta_n\\le T}\nW_2(\\widehat\\mu_{n,k},\\mu_{k\\eta_n})\\longrightarrow0. \\tag{7.9}\n\\]\nThus the uniform square-tail estimate (6.11) also holds at all GD nodes.\nSince \\(q'\\) is bounded, on \\(|P_k|\\le M\\),\n\\[\n\\|[q(A_k+sV_{A,k})-q(A_k)]V_{A,k}\\|_n\n\\le C_T\\eta M^2,                                  \\tag{7.10}\n\\]\nwhile on the complement both terms are bounded pointwise by\n\\(C_T|P_k|\\). The fixed finite Gaussian theorem applies jointly to all its nodes and proves convergence of all the needed empirical contractions and second moments.\n\nThere is a useful distinction between oracle nodes and an actual finite network state. Construct proxy parameter arrays using its first-layer nodes and readout, and the matrix\n\\[\nW_{k,\\mathrm{proxy}}^{(2)}\n=W_0^{(2)}-\\frac{2\\Delta}{n}\n\\sum_{s<k,b}r_{b,s}\\delta_{b,s,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}\n(h_{b,s,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\\top.\n\\tag{M12}\n\\]\nApplying this proxy matrix to a current oracle activation differs from its prescribed oracle preactivation only by sums of empirical-minus-population contractions. For example each forward discrepancy has the form\n\\[\n-2\\Delta r_{b,s}\\delta_{b,s,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}\n\\left(\n\\frac{(h_{b,s,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\\top\nh_{a,k,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)}}{n}\n-\\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]\n\\right).\n\\tag{M13}\n\\]\nThe transpose discrepancy has the corresponding contraction of two \\(\\delta^{(2)}\\) fields. At a fixed mesh there are finitely many such terms. Their scalar errors tend to zero, and their vector norms stay bounded. The actual network quantities recomputed from the proxy arrays therefore differ from the oracle nodes by \\(o_{\\mathbb P}(1)\\) in root mean square. Bounded readout and the operator bound justify each subsequent forward/backward comparison. In particular its recomputed vector field differs from its prescribed oracle grid velocity by \\(o_{\\mathbb P}(1)\\).\n\nFor each fixed threshold \\(R\\), the oracle's empirical squared tails converge to their population counterparts. One may use continuous upper cutoffs, equal to one beyond \\(R\\) and zero below \\(R-1\\), to avoid discontinuities. The fixed finite Gaussian theorem applies to these quadratic-growth measurements. Consequently the root-mean-square tail of the recomputed proxy backward field has limit superior at most \\(C e^{-cR^2}\\), after modifying constants. This is proved for the reference oracle, not assumed for actual GD.\n\nLinearly interpolate the proxy parameters. Their metric speed is bounded in probability, with limiting bound independent of the mesh, by (M4) and the node consistency just proved. Their between-grid vector-field defect is bounded by (M10), plus a fixed-mesh \\(o_{\\mathbb P}(1)\\). Compare the actual GD interpolant to this proxy. Actual GD's derivative is its vector field at the preceding fine grid point; its state changes by \\(O(\\eta_n)\\) inside a fine step. Apply (M9) with the proxy as reference. No tail bound for the actual GD is needed. Gronwall gives\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\sup_{t\\le T}d_n(\\theta_n^{\\mathrm{GD}}(t),\\theta_n^{\\mathrm{proxy},\\Delta}(t))\n\\le C e^{C(1+R)T}\n\\bigg(\n&\\frac{\\|W_{n,0}^{(3)}\\|_2}{\\sqrt n}\n+(1+R)(\\eta_n+\\Delta)\\\\\n&+e^{-cR^2}+o_{\\mathbb P}(1)\n\\bigg).\n\\end{aligned}\\tag{M14}\n\\]\nThe \\(o_{\\mathbb P}(1)\\) here is at fixed \\(R,\\Delta\\). Bounds on the actual readout supremum are obtained from its update, not from root-mean-square closeness.\n\nThe initialization discrepancy vanishes, and \\(\\eta_n\\to0\\). First take \\(n\\to\\infty\\) at fixed \\(R,\\Delta\\); next take \\(\\Delta\\to0\\); finally take \\(R\\to\\infty\\). Together with (M11), the fixed-mesh oracle empirical convergence, and the triangle inequality for observables, this proves the claimed joint limit. Thus this local proof needs no width-dependent learning-rate restriction beyond \\(\\eta_n\\to0\\).\n\nFor the first kernel block, use (M9) once more to transfer the first-layer \\(\\delta^{(1)}\\) fields in mean square; the other blocks follow directly from the bounded/Lipschitz maps. Products and contractions then converge by Cauchy–Schwarz. For hidden velocities,\n\\[\n\\dot z_a^{(2)}\n=\\dot W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)}\n+W^{(2)}\\bigl[\\phi'(z_a^{(1)})\\odot\\dot z_a^{(1)}\\bigr].\n\\]\nThe reference first-layer velocity is a finite linear combination of bounded multiples of the backward fields, hence has the same tail control. Applying the same comparison proves convergence of both hidden velocities in integrated mean square and hence of their integrated squared norms.\n\nTo obtain path-law convergence, first use the finite Gaussian theorem on any fixed finite time grid. Between two times separated by at most \\(\\varepsilon\\), any absolutely continuous coordinate path satisfies\n\\[\n\\sup_{s,t\\ \\mathrm{in\\ one\\ grid\\ cell}}|z(t)-z(s)|^2\n\\le\\varepsilon\\int_{\\mathrm{cell}}|\\dot z(u)|^2\\,du.\n\\]\nThe proved mean-energy bounds therefore approximate empirical and population path laws, in squared uniform-path distance, by their piecewise linear grid reconstructions with error at most \\(C\\varepsilon\\). Letting the grid become fine gives the joint three-input path-law assertion. The same finite-grid approximation and (M9) give uniform-in-time convergence of the stated fixed action probes.\n\n## Strict feature learning without assuming that G is invertible\n\nWe have proved existence, uniqueness, and convergence without needing \\(G\\) positive definite. For strict feature learning, two additional arguments replace the two-input proof's full-support and exchange-symmetry shortcuts.\n\nWrite\n\\[\nZ_{0,a}^{(1)}=\\frac{w^\\top x_a}{\\sqrt d},\\qquad w\\sim N(0,I_d),\\qquad\nQ_{ab}=\\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].\n\\]\nWe first prove \\(Q\\) positive definite even if \\(G\\) has rank two. If \\(c^\\top Qc=0\\), then\n\\[\n\\sum_{a=1}^3c_a\\arctan(w^\\top x_a/\\sqrt d)=0\n\\]\nalmost surely. Continuity and the full support of \\(w\\) make this identity hold for every \\(w\\in\\mathbb R^d\\).\n\nChoose \\(v\\in\\mathbb R^d\\) outside the finitely many hyperplanes\n\\[\nv^\\top x_a=0,\\qquad v^\\top(x_a-x_b)=0,\\qquad\nv^\\top(x_a+x_b)=0.\n\\]\nEach is a proper hyperplane because the inputs are nonzero and no two equal up to sign. A finite union of proper hyperplanes cannot cover \\(\\mathbb R^d\\). Hence the numbers \\(b_a=v^\\top x_a/\\sqrt d\\) are nonzero and their squares are distinct. Restrict the identity to \\(w=tv\\). The coefficients of \\(t,t^3,t^5\\) in\n\\(\\arctan(tb)=tb-(tb)^3/3+(tb)^5/5+O(t^7)\\) give\n\\[\n\\sum_a c_a b_a(b_a^2)^k=0,\\qquad k=0,1,2.\n\\]\nThe determinant of the matrix with columns \\((1,b_a^2,b_a^4)^\\top\\) is\n\\(\\prod_{a<b}(b_b^2-b_a^2)\\ne0\\). Thus \\(c_a b_a=0\\) for every \\(a\\), hence \\(c=0\\). This proves \\(Q\\succ0\\).\n\nThe first forward calls now give a full-support Gaussian triple\n\\[\n(Z_{0,1}^{(2)},Z_{0,2}^{(2)},Z_{0,3}^{(2)})\\sim N(0,Q).\n\\]\nDefine the repeated coefficients\n\\[\nS=\\sum_a y_aH_{0,a}^{(2)},\\quad\nU_a=S\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(2)}),\\quad\nP_a=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,\\quad\nB_a=\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,\n\\]\n\\[\nV_{ab}=\\mathbb E[U_aU_b],\\qquad D_{ab}=\\mathbb E[B_aB_b].\n\\tag{M15}\n\\]\nConditional on the last two second-layer coordinates, \\(S\\) is a strictly monotone function of the first coordinate. That first coordinate has a continuous conditional Gaussian density, so \\(\\mathbb P(S=0)=0\\). If \\(\\sum_ac_aU_a=0\\), division by \\(S\\), full support, and continuity imply\n\\(\\sum_ac_a\\phi'(s_a)=0\\) for every \\(s\\in\\mathbb R^3\\). Varying one coordinate while fixing the other two forces each \\(c_a=0\\). Therefore \\(V\\succ0\\).\n\nThe simultaneous initial transpose responses satisfy\n\\[\nP_a=\\sum_{c=1}^3H_{0,c}^{(1)}\n\\left[\nQ^{-1}\n\\begin{pmatrix}\n\\mathbb E[Z_{0,1}^{(2)}U_a]\\\\\n\\mathbb E[Z_{0,2}^{(2)}U_a]\\\\\n\\mathbb E[Z_{0,3}^{(2)}U_a]\n\\end{pmatrix}\n\\right]_c+\\Gamma_a,\n\\tag{M16}\n\\]\nwhere \\(\\Gamma\\sim N(0,V)\\) is independent of the first-layer roots. This follows by conditioning the initial Gaussian matrix on its three forward calls. Its fresh transpose part has source covariance \\(V\\); the finite-rank projection onto the initial forward directions disappears in normalized mean square. Equivalently, Gaussian integration by parts gives this instance of the appendix response rule.\n\nSince every \\(\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})>0\\), the conditional covariance of \\((B_1,B_2,B_3)\\), given the first-layer roots, is positive definite. Hence \\(D\\succ0\\).\n\nDefine the first- and second-layer displacement coefficients by\n\\[\nT_a=\\sum_bG_{ab}y_bB_b,\\qquad\nR_a=\\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b+\nW_0^{(2)}[\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a].\n\\tag{M17}\n\\]\nEach \\(T_a\\) has strictly positive conditional variance. In fact its coefficient of \\(\\Gamma_a\\) in (M16) is \\(G_{aa}y_a\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})\\ne0\\), and \\(V\\) is positive definite. Thus \\(\\mathbb E[T_a^2]>0\\).\n\nTo prove every \\(R_a\\) nonzero we cannot use input-exchange symmetry: a general triple has none. Instead examine the next forward call. Denote its input, which recurs in this calculation, by\n\\[\nJ_a=\\phi'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a.\n\\]\nLet \\(\\alpha_a\\in\\mathbb R^3\\) solve\n\\[\n\\sum_c Q_{bc}\\alpha_{a,c}=\\mathbb E[H_{0,b}^{(1)}J_a],\n\\qquad b=1,2,3,\n\\]\nand subtract the component explained by the previous forward inputs:\n\\[\nJ_a^\\perp=J_a-\\sum_c\\alpha_{a,c}H_{0,c}^{(1)},\\qquad\n\\sigma_a^2=\\mathbb E[(J_a^\\perp)^2].\n"}]

## Ordinal 2204 agent_message /root/two_input_full_audit_b 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
