Payload:
I found no directly applicable black-box theorem covering all three requirements together: all-layer-trained nonlinear μP, our Gaussian middle initialization and finite dataset, and arbitrary joint \(\eta_n\to0\) convergence on every finite horizon. This bounded search does not establish that no such theorem exists.

The local DMFT source is a different model. `/tmp/dmft2602src` contains Nishiyama–Imaizumi, *High-Dimensional Limit of Stochastic Gradient Flow via Dynamical Mean-Field Theory*, arXiv:2602.06320v2.

- Section 2 fixes network width \(m\) and sends sample size \(n\) and input dimension \(d\) proportionally to infinity. Its random matrix is a fixed design matrix \(X\), not a trained middle-layer matrix.
- Assumptions 2.1–2.2 require independent uniformly sub-Gaussian design entries of variance \(1/d\), initialization/noise empirical convergence in every Wasserstein order, and globally Lipschitz drift functions with specified Lipschitz derivatives.
- Theorem 3.1 is global for noiseless flow \(\tau=0\), or when \(\ell\) is affine in its prediction argument; general stochastic nonlinear dynamics receive only a local theorem. Theorem 3.2 gives finite-dimensional empirical \(W_2\) convergence.
- Section 2 explicitly does **not** establish matching high-dimensional SGD and SGF limits. Thus this is not a joint-step theorem for our model. [Primary paper, §§2–3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.06320#page=4).

Closest relevant nonlinear μP result: Chen–Vanden-Eijnden–Bruna, *A Functional-Space Mean-Field Theory of Partially-Trained Three-Layer Neural Networks*, JMLR 27(52), 2026.

Its Theorem 7 covers the \(\alpha=1/2\) μP scaling, Gaussian middle initialization, finite datasets, and every fixed finite time. Assumptions 1–3 require continuous first activation, bounded second activation with bounded Lipschitz derivative, Gaussian middle weights, compactly supported symmetric output initialization, and sub-Gaussian first-layer initialization. But **the first layer is frozen**, and §2 explicitly restricts analysis to gradient flow. It therefore does not supply our \(\kappa_1>0\) or actual-GD joint limit. [§2, Assumptions 1–3, Theorem 7](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2210.16286#page=6).

Other exclusions:

- Nguyen–Pham’s Theorem 3.1 provides global multilayer MF well-posedness with sub-Gaussian initialization and structural forward/backward regularity. Its fully connected example uses \(1/n\) middle-layer averaging with order-one stored weights. Expressing our Gaussian \(1/\sqrt n\)-scale effective middle weights in that convention makes stored initialization scale as \(\sqrt n\), violating its uniform initialization control. [Example 2.1](https://ems.press/content/serial-article-files/29198#page=10), [Theorem 3.1](https://ems.press/content/serial-article-files/29198#page=18).
- Chen–Yang–Zhao–Gu’s 2025 “Global Convergence…” paper studies discrete μP training. Corollary 4.6 assumes weights become exactly stationary after a finite iteration; it is not a global GF well-posedness theorem. Its claimed first-layer preactivation independence also cannot apply to our singular-\(G\) triples: input linear relations persist under every \(W^1(t)\). [Theorem 4.5 and Corollary 4.6](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.09565#page=5).

For the proposed extension, the main mathematical warning is:

Loss dissipation bounds finite-time displacement in the training metric,
\[
\int_0^T\!\left(
n^{-1}\|\dot W^1\|_F^2+
\|\dot W^2\|_F^2+
n^{-1}\|\dot W^3\|_2^2
\right)dt\le L(0).
\]
This controls the learned middle-operator increment, but not the troublesome multiplication
\[
[\phi'(Z^1)-\phi'(Z^{1,\mathrm{ref}})]B^{1,\mathrm{ref}}.
\]
A bounded \(L^2\) operator need not send bounded functions to bounded functions. Therefore RMS bounds alone do not establish global reference stability or justify repeatedly restarting the local theorem. A finite-horizon tail/regularity continuation estimate remains necessary.

Finally, “general smooth activation” is too broad even at initialization: \(\phi(z)=e^{z^4}\) gives infinite Gaussian second moments. Bounded smooth activations are plausible extension candidates, but strict activity, nonlinear affine-fit error, and Gram positivity require separate nondegeneracy assumptions; they do not follow merely from well-posedness.

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 2.1 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': ' (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685v3)\n\ue200cite\ue202turn76view0\ue201 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; Content type: application/pdf; Number of pages: 87; Source: open({"ref_id":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685v3","lineno":null}); Total lines: 13312\nL0@P0: Tensor Programs III: Neural Matrix Laws\nL1@P0: Greg Yang\nL2@P0: Microsoft Research AI\nL3@P0: gregyang@microsoft.com\nL4@P0: Abstract\nL5@P0: In a neural network (NN), weight matrices linearly transform inputs into preac\x02tivations that are then transformed nonlinearly into activations. A typical NN\nL6@P0: interleaves multitudes of such linear and nonlinear transforms to express complex\nL7@P0: functions. Thus, the (pre-)activations depend on the weights in an intricate manner.\nL8@P0: We show that, surprisingly, (pre-)activations of a randomly initialized NN become\nL9@P0: independent from the weights as the NN’s widths tend to infinity, in the sense of\nL10@P0: asymptotic freeness in random matrix theory. We call this the Free Independence\nL11@P0: Principle (FIP), which has these consequences: 1) It rigorously justifies the calcula\x02tion of asymptotic Jacobian singular value distribution of an NN in Pennington et al.\nL12@P0: [36, 37], essential for training ultra-deep NNs [48]. 2) It gives a new justification of\nL13@P0: gradient independence assumption used for calculating the Neural Tangent Kernel\nL14@P0: of a neural network. FIP and these results hold for any neural architecture. We\nL15@P0: show FIP by proving a Master Theorem for any Tensor Program, as introduced\nL16@P0: in Yang [50, 51], generalizing the Master Theorems proved in those works. As\nL17@P0: warmup demonstrations of this new Master Theorem, we give new proofs of the\nL18@P0: semicircle and Marchenko-Pastur laws, which benchmarks our framework against\nL19@P0: these fundamental mathematical results.\nL20@P0: 1 Introduction\nL21@P0: A neural network (NN), at a high level, is an interleaved composition of linear and nonlinear\nL22@P0: transformations. Its weights are the matrices involved in the linear transformations. The image\nL23@P0: (resp. input) of nonlinear transformations are called activations (resp. preactivations). Thus, a priori,\nL24@P0: (pre-)activations depend in complicated ways on the weights. We show that, surprisingly, the (pre-\nL25@P0: )activations become roughly independent from the weights in the sense of random matrix theory,\nL26@P0: when the NN is randomly initialized and as its width tends to infinity. More formally, we prove that\nL27@P0: the weights are asymptotically free from the diagonal matrices whose diagonals are the images of\nL28@P0: preactivation vectors under any bounded coordinatewise function.1 This result holds for any neural\nL29@P0: architecture (i.e. architectural universality)2. We call this result the Free Independence Principle\nL30@P0: (FIP). A major application of FIP is in rigorously justifying a prevalent free independence assumption\nL31@P0: in the calculation of NN Jacobian singular value distribution [25, 36, 37, 43, 48], as we overview\nL32@P0: below.\nL33@P0: Besides FIP, we also prove several other interesting results. In the next few subsections, we discuss\nL34@P0: the context required to state them. A summary of all of our contributions appears at the end of this\nL35@P0: introduction, and the reader in a hurry may feel free to skip directly to it.\nL36@P0: 1Note, however, that we make no claim about trained weights, just random weights.\nL37@P0: 2\nL38@P0: In this work, architecture refers to the network topology along with the random initialization of parameters,\nL39@P0: as specified by a NETSOR> program (Definition 2.1).\nL40@P0-1: arXiv:2009.10685v3 [cs.NE] 8 May 20211.1 Random Matrix Theory in Deep Learning\nL41@P1: Random matrix theory (RMT) has a successful history of being applied in deep learning [25, 36,\nL42@P1: 37, 43, 48]. For example, RMT has been used to calculate the Jacobian3singular value distribution\nL43@P1: of a wide neural network, which is an important indicator of its architectural soundness: If this\nL44@P1: distribution becomes very diffuse as the network gets deeper (i.e. more layers), then an error signal\nL45@P1: (i.e. the gradient) will be badly distorted as it is backpropagated. On the other hand, if the distribution\nL46@P1: concentrates around 1 even when the network gets deeper, then the error signal is largely preserved,\nL47@P1: and all layers of the networks receive adequate signal to improve. This idea is known as dynamical\nL48@P1: isometry [36] and has been successfully applied in practice to train ultra-deep networks (as deep as\nL49@P1: 10,000 layers! [48]).\nL50@P1: This calculation of the NN Jacobian singular values distribution can be seen as a vast nonlinear\nL51@P1: generalization of the classical problem of calculating the singular value distribution for an iid random\nL52@P1: matrix. The solution of this latter problem is famously given by (the square root of) the Marchenko\x02Pastur distribution. A related classical random matrix law is the semicircle law, which says that the\nL53@P1: eigenvalue distribution of the sum A + A> of a random real iid matrix A and its transpose A> is\nL54@P1: asympotically shaped like a semicircle. Like the utility of NN Jacobian singular values, Marchenko\x02Pastur and the semicircle law have both been impactful in science and engineering and are considered\nL55@P1: two fundamental laws of random matrix theory. In fact, the semicircle law was first motivated by a\nL56@P1: study of the distribution of energy levels of an atom [21].\nL57@P1: Up until recently, the study of the NN Jacobian singular value distribution lacked a rigorous foundation.\nL58@P1: The first calculation of it assumed (free) independence (between the weights and the preactivations)\nL59@P1: that was only empirically checked but not proved. It was unknown how widespread this phenomenon\nL60@P1: actually was. One purpose of this work is filling in that hole: FIP reveals the architectural universality\nL61@P1: of this free independence (i.e. the neural network can be as complex as any modern manifestations\nL62@P1: and the principle still holds).\nL63@P1: 1.2 New Approach to Random Matrix Theory\nL64@P1: Another purpose of this work is to illustrate a new way to go about random matrix theory, one that\nL65@P1: allows us to methodically attack the nonlinear problems of deep neural networks. In contrast, classical\nL66@P1: methods of random matrix theory typically heavily rely on the linearity of classical random matrix\nL67@P1: ensembles. The expansion technique is one such method. We review how it’s used in linear problems\nL68@P1: and show it is ineffective for (nonlinear) neural networks problems.\nL69@P1: The expansion technique of classical RMT For example, a typical quantity to calculate is the\nL70@P1: expectation of the trace of some matrix power tr(A>A)\nL71@P1: k\nL72@P1: for some iid random matrix A. One\nL73@P1: common way to proceed is expanding this expression in terms of monomials of the entries of A,\nL74@P1: and then counting the different kinds of monomials that arise: such as monomials multilinear in the\nL75@P1: entries of A, or monomials where every entry appears quadratically, etc. Given this expansion, the\nL76@P1: trace expectation then follows easily.\nL77@P1: The expansion technique is ineffective in neural network problems Now, in the (nonlinear)\nL78@P1: case of neural networks, the matrix A is usually something more complicated. Take, for example,\nL79@P1: A = D2BD1B for some iid matrix B and diagonal matrices Dithat depend nonlinearly on B. This\nL80@P1: dependence can, for example, take the following form: for some iid vector v and φ = tanh (to\nL81@P1: be applied coordinatewise), we define u1 = φ(Bv), u2 = φ(Bu1) and set Dito have diagonal ui.\nL82@P1: Such ensemble A commonly appears as the Jacobian of a neural network. Inspired by the classical\nL83@P1: expansion technique described above, one may attack this problem by expanding tr(A>A)\nL84@P1: k\nL85@P1: in terms\nL86@P1: of entries of A or in terms of entries of B. Because the entries of A are correlated in complicated\nL87@P1: ways, it will be hard to use the expansion in A. On the other hand, we cannot even expand tr(A>A)\nL88@P1: k\nL89@P1: cleanly in B because of the nonlinear dependence of Di on B (particularly because of φ)\nL90@P1: 4\nL91@P1: .\nL92@P1: 3Here we are concerned with the input-output Jacobian of a neural network on a fix input. Some works\nL93@P1: consider the Jacobian with respect to the parameters, where the Jacobian has dimension #parameters × #data\nL94@P1: points (e.g. [35]). This is a related but distinct case from the input-output Jacobian, which is what we focus on\nL95@P1: in this work.\nL96@P1: 4We can Taylor expand Di in terms of entries of B but that gets ugly really fast.\nL97@P1-2: 2Thus the usual expansion technique runs into a wall very quickly.\nL98@P2: Our proposed method We express the trace tr(A>A)\nL99@P2: k\nL100@P2: as an expectation Ev v\nL101@P2: >(A>A)kv\nL102@P2: where v is a standard Gaussian vector5. Then we inductively analyze the vectors\nL103@P2: Av, A>Av, AA>Av,(A>A)\nL104@P2: 2v, . . . ,(A>A)kv, in a way we will soon describe below. Finally, this\nL105@P2: analysis of (A>A)\nL106@P2: kv will allow us to calculate Ev v>(A>A)kv.\nL107@P2: It turns out that the vectors v, Av, A>Av, AA>Av,(A>A)\nL108@P2: 2v, . . . ,(A>A)kv will\nL109@P2: 1. all have approximately iid coordinates in the large dimension limit, in a suitable sense, i.e.\nL110@P2: {(Av)α}α are approximately iid, and similarly for {(A>Av)α}α, {((A>A)\nL111@P2: 2v)α}α, etc,\nL112@P2: where α denotes coordinate index;\nL113@P2: 2. but the sequence of coordinates vα,(Av)α,(A>Av)α, . . . ,((A>A)\nL114@P2: kv)α are correlated for\nL115@P2: each fixed index α, in the same way across all α.\nL116@P2: By analyze, we mean to calculate such correlations. Then Ev v\nL117@P2: >(A>A)kv is given by the correlation\nL118@P2: between vα and ((A>A)\nL119@P2: kv)α.\nL120@P2: Example Let us use the classical example of iid A to make these two points more concrete.\nL121@P2: If v ∈ R\nL122@P2: n is a standard Gaussian vector, and A ∈ Rn×n, Aαβ ∼ N (0, 1/n) is iid and independent\nL123@P2: from v, then it’s not hard to see Av has coordinates which tend to iid Gaussians via some kind\nL124@P2: of central limit argument (illustraing point (1)). Next, we will soon see intuitively that A>Av is\nL125@P2: asymptotically the sum of v and a random Gaussian vector with iid coordinates, independent from v\nL126@P2: (illustrating point (1)). Thus A>Av is coordinatewise correlated with v, illustrating point (2).\nL127@P2: To get this intuition, write each coordinate\nL128@P2: (A\nL129@P2: >Av)α =\nL130@P2: X\nL131@P2: β,γ\nL132@P2: AβαAβγvγ =\nL133@P2: X\nL134@P2: β\nL135@P2: A\nL136@P2: 2\nL137@P2: βαvα +\nL138@P2: X\nL139@P2: β;γ6=α\nL140@P2: AβαAβγvγ, β, γ ∈ [n]. (1)\nL141@P2: Because each Aβα has variance 1/n, the first sum will converge via law of large numbers to vα.\nL142@P2: In the second sum, one can note that each summand is uncorrelated with others (and higher order\nL143@P2: correlations drop off rapidly with n), so one may expect the second sum to converge to a Gaussian\nL144@P2: through some central limit behavior. We can calculate that the second sum P\nL145@P2: β;γ6=α AβαAβγvγ for\nL146@P2: different αs will be uncorrelated, so their limits for different α should be independent Gaussians.\nL147@P2: Likewise, it should be asymptotically independent from v for the same reason.\nL148@P2: This finishes our example and illustrates the overarching philosophy of our proposed\nL149@P2: method. But it is still tedious if we have to manually derive the correlation between\nL150@P2: vα,(Av)α,(A>Av)α, . . . ,((A>A)\nL151@P2: kv)α. In addition, it’s not entirely clear at this point that this\nL152@P2: method can handle the nonlinear ensemble example A = D2BD1B given above. This is where the\nL153@P2: Tensor Programs framework is crucial, which we overview now.\nL154@P2: 1.3 The Tensor Programs Framework\nL155@P2: The Tensor Programs framework can be thought of as a clean, scalable, and rigorous packaging of\nL156@P2: the intuition explained in the last section. We first demonstrate how one can apply this framework\nL157@P2: to compute the correlations between vα,(Av)α,(A>Av)α, . . . ,((A>A)\nL158@P2: kv)α. Then we describe in\nL159@P2: more general terms what the framework is about.\nL160@P2: Calculating Ev v\nL161@P2: >(A>A)kv with Tensor Programs The framework associates a random variable\nL162@P2: Z\nL163@P2: u ∈ R to each u ∈ {v, Av, A>Av, . . . ,(A>A)kv}, representing the distribution of coordinates of\nL164@P2: u in the large n limit. It also comes with a set of symbolic rules to derive Z\nL165@P2: Av given Zv\nL166@P2: , Z\nL167@P2: A\nL168@P2: >Av\nL169@P2: given Z\nL170@P2: Av and Zv\nL171@P2: , and so on. For example, consistent with the intuition laid out in Section 1.2, Z\nL172@P2: v\nL173@P2: and Z\nL174@P2: Av are defined as independent standard Gaussians (since v and Av are asymptotically standard\nL175@P2: Gaussian vectors, independent from each other), and Z\nL176@P2: A\nL177@P2: >Av = Z\nL178@P2: v + S for a standard Gaussian\nL179@P2: S ∈ R independent from Z\nL180@P2: v\nL181@P2: (and Z\nL182@P2: Av). Finally, we can calculate limn→∞ Ev v>(A>A)kv =\nL183@P2: EZ\nL184@P2: vZ(A\nL185@P2: >A)k\nL186@P2: v\nL187@P2: .\nL188@P2: 5\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "# Global-in-time population limit for one input or finitely many mutually orthogonal inputs\n\nThis theorem is a genuine all-finite-time extension of the one-input argument. It does not establish the same conclusion for arbitrary correlated inputs. A scalar-flow coordinate, rather than an inverse integral, permits activation derivatives that vanish or change sign and removes unnecessary transformed-initialization moment assumptions.\n\n## Statement\n\nFix a finite number m of inputs x_a in R^d with x_a^T x_b/d=1 if a=b and zero otherwise. Fix labels y_a in R and positive layer constants kappa_1,kappa_2,kappa_3. The first-layer activation phi_1 and second-layer activation phi_2 may differ. Assume:\n\n1. phi_1 is continuously differentiable; phi_1' is bounded and globally Lipschitz. The function phi_1 itself need not be bounded, monotone, or odd, and its derivative need not be positive.\n2. phi_2 is bounded and continuously differentiable; phi_2' is bounded and globally Lipschitz.\n\nUse the network and exact updates\n\n    z_a^{(1)}=W^{(1)}x_a/sqrt(d),\n    h_a^{(1)}=phi_1(z_a^{(1)}),\n    z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},\n    h_a^{(2)}=phi_2(z_a^{(2)}),\n    f_{n,a}=(W^{(3)})^T h_a^{(2)}/n,\n    r_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,\n    L_n=sum_a r_{n,a}^2,\n\n    delta_a^{(2)}=W^{(3)} elementwise-times phi_2'(z_a^{(2)}),\n    delta_a^{(1)}=phi_1'(z_a^{(1)}) elementwise-times (W^{(2)})^T delta_a^{(2)},\n\n    z_{a,k+1}^{(1)}=z_{a,k}^{(1)}-2 kappa_1 eta_n r_{n,a,k} delta_{a,k}^{(1)},\n    W_{k+1}^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}-(2 kappa_2 eta_n/n) sum_a r_{n,a,k} delta_{a,k}^{(2)}(h_{a,k}^{(1)})^T,\n    W_{k+1}^{(3)}=W_k^{(3)}-2 kappa_3 eta_n sum_a r_{n,a,k}h_{a,k}^{(2)}.\n\nThe first update uses orthogonality: there are no cross-input first-layer gradient terms. Initialize W^{(1)} with independent N(0,sigma_1^2) entries and W^{(2)} with independent N(0,sigma_2^2/n) entries, independently of each other. The rescaled readout can have the previous independent N(0,sigma_3^2/n^2) initialization, for any fixed finite sigma_1,sigma_2,sigma_3. More generally the readout initialization may be independent with its coordinate supremum converging to zero in probability. The limiting readout is zero.\n\nFor every fixed T<infinity there is a unique autonomous population gradient flow on [0,T]. If eta_n>0 and eta_n sqrt(n) tends to zero, the physical-time GD trajectories t=k eta_n converge to this flow on [0,T]. The conclusion includes predictions, summed loss, all kernel-block entries, same-layer joint hidden path laws with their second moments, and all the fixed globally Lipschitz forward/adjoint measurements in the previously proved theorem. The unbounded backward-field quadratic measurements and integrated squared hidden speeds are included by the same uniform second-moment tail argument.\n\nThis is a sufficient learning-rate condition, not a claim of necessity. No positivity or nonlinearity conclusion is asserted for every activation in this class: constants and affine first activations are deliberately included. Strict feature-learning statements require separate nondegeneracy hypotheses.\n\n## The scalar-flow coordinate\n\nLet J(s,z) be the scalar ODE flow\n\n    partial_s J(s,z)=phi_1'(J(s,z)),    J(0,z)=z,    s in R.\n\nSince phi_1' is globally Lipschitz and bounded, this ODE has a unique global solution in both time directions. Write M_1=sup|phi_1'| and L_1=Lip(phi_1'). Integration gives\n\n    |J(s,z)-J(t,z)| <= M_1 |s-t|,\n    |J(s,z)| <= |z|+M_1|s|.\n\nThe flow identity J(s,J(t,z))=J(s+t,z) follows because both sides solve the same scalar ODE with the same value at s=0. Continuous dependence on the initial value follows on each bounded s interval from\n\n    |J(s,z)-J(s,w)| <= exp(L_1|s|)|z-w|.\n\nTherefore J is jointly continuous. Also\n\n    |phi_1(J(s,z))-phi_1(J(t,z))| <= M_1^2 |s-t|.\n\nFor each input introduce a transformed displacement X_a with X_a(0)=0, retaining the fixed Gaussian root Z_{0,a}^{(1)}, and define\n\n    Z_a^{(1)}(t)=J(X_a(t),Z_{0,a}^{(1)}).\n\nThe transformed evolution is\n\n    dot X_a=-2 kappa_1 r_a (W^{(2)})^*delta_a^{(2)},\n    dot W^{(2)}=-2 kappa_2 sum_a r_a delta_a^{(2)} tensor H_a^{(1)},\n    dot W^{(3)}=-2 kappa_3 sum_a r_a H_a^{(2)},\n\nwhere all derived fields use the stated network equations and (u tensor v)V=u E[vV]. The ordinary first-layer equation follows by the scalar chain rule.\n\nConversely, any mean-square differentiable solution of the ordinary first-layer equation obeys this representation. For almost every population coordinate its backward coefficient is integrable in time, by Cauchy-Schwarz and Fubini. The scalar equation dot z(t)=b(t)phi_1'(z(t)) with integrable b has the unique solution J(integral_0^t b(s)ds,z(0)): differentiating verifies it, and the scalar Lipschitz inequality with integrable coefficient proves uniqueness. Thus the coordinate construction does not change the original gradient flow or discard other possible solutions.\n\nWhen phi_1'>0, one may instead use F'(z)=1/phi_1'(z). Then J(s,z)=F^{-1}(F(z)+s). The displacement formulation does not require E[F(Z_0)^2]<infinity. In particular, the rapid growth of the inverse-derivative integral for erf creates no restriction on its Gaussian initialization variance.\n\n## Global existence and uniqueness\n\nWork on the same two population coordinate spaces and bounded initial Gaussian matrix action constructed in the prior finite-program proof. Use the state distance\n\n    sum_a sqrt(E|X_a-X_a_tilde|^2)\n      + ||W^{(2)}-W_tilde^{(2)}||_op\n      + sqrt(E|W^{(3)}-W_tilde^{(3)}|^2),\n\nwith the same fixed first-layer roots on the two sides. The finite-width version replaces each square-mean norm by its ordinary Euclidean norm divided by sqrt(n).\n\nOn sets where the X norms, matrix operator norms, and readout coordinate suprema are bounded, the transformed vector field is Lipschitz in this distance, uniformly in width. Here are the potentially relevant factors. The first activation difference is bounded by M_1^2 times the X difference, while its own mean-square norm is bounded by\n\n    |phi_1(0)|+M_1 sqrt(E|Z_{0,a}^{(1)}|^2)+M_1^2 sqrt(E|X_a|^2).\n\nForward matrix differences follow by adding and subtracting one factor and using the operator norm. The second-layer backward difference obeys\n\n    ||delta_a^{(2)}-delta_a_tilde^{(2)}||_L2\n       <= ||phi_2'||_infty ||W^{(3)}-W_tilde^{(3)}||_L2\n          + ||W_tilde^{(3)}||_infty Lip(phi_2') ||Z_a^{(2)}-Z_a_tilde^{(2)}||_L2.\n\nIts adjoint response is Lipschitz by the same operator estimate. The readout is Lipschitz because phi_2 is bounded and Lipschitz. Rank-one differences satisfy\n\n    ||u tensor v-u_tilde tensor v_tilde||_op\n       <= ||u-u_tilde||_L2 ||v||_L2+||u_tilde||_L2 ||v-v_tilde||_L2.\n\nThese inequalities prove local existence and uniqueness by contracting the integrated vector field on a sufficiently short time interval. A common readout supremum bound defines a closed complete set under the displayed metric and is preserved by its integral equation on a sufficiently short interval.\n\nThe chain rule and adjoint identity give\n\n    dot f=-2 K r,    dot L=-4 r^T K r <=0,\n\nwhere the physical kernel is K=kappa_1 K^{(1)}+kappa_2 K^{(2)}+kappa_3 K^{(3)}, and\n\n    K^{(1)}_ab=1_{a=b} E[delta_a^{(1)}delta_b^{(1)}],\n    K^{(2)}_ab=E[H_a^{(1)}H_b^{(1)}] E[delta_a^{(2)}delta_b^{(2)}],\n    K^{(3)}_ab=E[H_a^{(2)}H_b^{(2)}].\n\nEach is a Gram matrix of the corresponding parameter gradients. Thus ||r(t)||_2<=||r(0)||_2. Let B_2=sup|phi_2|. The readout equation gives the pointwise bound\n\n    ||W^{(3)}(t)||_infty <= ||W^{(3)}(0)||_infty\n           +2 kappa_3 B_2 sqrt(m)||r(0)||_2 t.\n\nOn any prescribed finite horizon T, this bounds every delta_a^{(2)} in mean square and in coordinate supremum. The other equations consequently give\n\n    ||dot X_a||_L2 <= C_T ||W^{(2)}||_op,\n    ||dot W^{(2)}||_op <= C_T (1+sum_a ||X_a||_L2).\n\nThe fixed root norms are absorbed into C_T. Adding these inequalities and integrating gives a scalar linear Gronwall bound on the X norms and the matrix operator norm throughout [0,T]. Hence no finite-time blow-up or loss of the bounded-state conditions can occur. The locally unique solution extends to every finite horizon.\n\nUniqueness for the raw state follows from the exact J representation already proved. In particular one can restart at the current raw first-layer fields, rather than storing their earlier clock or their initialization. The ordinary equations use only current fields, the current matrix action, and its adjoint. The same current-generated-space argument as in the prior proof identifies equal current action laws with equal future action laws. The flow is autonomous, not merely a chosen interpolation of limiting observations.\n\n## The finite-width and mesh argument\n\nOne small extension of the prior elementary finite Gaussian lemma is needed. Its coordinate maps may now depend on fixed initial root coordinates, be jointly continuous and of at most linear growth, and be globally Lipschitz in their varying program arguments with a constant independent of the fixed root value. The maps J(X,Z_0) and phi_1(J(X,Z_0)) have exactly these properties.\n\nTo verify this extension, retain the initial roots among every same-layer joint tuple. In the Gaussian conditioning induction, joint empirical convergence with second moments passes through every continuous map of at most linear growth. This can be checked by truncating the input tuple to a large ball, where the map is uniformly continuous, and using the convergent second-moment tails outside that ball. In the independent-small-Gaussian perturbation used to remove singular matrix-query Grams, the root coordinates are unchanged. Therefore the perturbation stability uses only the uniform Lipschitz constants in the varying program arguments, exactly as in the prior proof. Gaussian matrix operator bounds and the deterministic finite-program recurrence then remove that perturbation. These two observations prove the needed extended lemma, rather than assuming a theorem for arbitrarily long programs.\n\nAll oracle products defining delta^{(2)} have a deterministically bounded readout factor on a fixed mesh; clipping that factor outside its proven bound makes the coordinate instruction globally Lipschitz without changing the oracle. Unbounded first activations occur only in globally Lipschitz coordinate maps or scalar linear combinations and matrix calls. Thus they do not introduce a product of two unbounded varying coordinate factors.\n\nThe population transformed Euler method has O_T(Delta) state error. This follows from the bounded vector field and its bounded Lipschitz constant on the larger stopped set, the O(Delta^2) local defect, and the recurrence e_{k+1}<=(1+C_T Delta)e_k+C_T Delta^2. The readout supremum stopping bound is controlled separately through its update, exactly as in the prior proof. The estimates also hold uniformly in width for finite transformed Euler.\n\nFor each fixed Delta, expand the trained middle matrix as its initialization plus the finite sum of rank-one updates. Supply the oracle with the population residuals and all population contractions in those expansions. The extended finite Gaussian lemma proves joint empirical convergence of every oracle node and contraction. The difference between recomputing the proxy matrix action and the prescribed oracle node is a finite sum of vectors multiplied by empirical-minus-population contractions. Its normalized norm vanishes. The same Lipschitz state estimate transfers this fixed-mesh limit from the oracle to the actual transformed finite Euler method. Taking width to infinity first at fixed Delta and then Delta to zero gives the transformed finite-flow population limit on every fixed [0,T].\n\n## Exact raw GD also converges\n\nThere is a useful direct scalar estimate that does not require phi_1' to be nonzero. In this paragraph only, write A=phi_1', let L=Lip(A), and consider a raw update\n\n    z^+=z+eta A(z)b.\n\nIf L eta |b|<=1/2 and A(z) is nonzero, every point between z and z^+ lies in the same component of {A!=0}, because\n\n    |A(z+s eta A(z)b)-A(z)| <= L eta |A(z)b| <= |A(z)|/2,\n    0<=s<=1.\n\nDefine its exact scalar-flow clock increment by\n\n    Delta X = integral_z^{z^+} du/A(u).\n\nThen z^+=J(Delta X,z). Substituting u=z+s eta A(z)b gives\n\n    |Delta X-eta b|\n       <= eta |b| integral_0^1\n             |A(z)/A(z+s eta A(z)b)-1| ds\n       <= L eta^2 b^2.\n\nIf A(z)=0, the raw step is fixed; set Delta X=eta b. Since J(s,z)=z for every s at an equilibrium, the same exact representation and error bound hold. If L=0 the derivative is constant and the transformation is exact, without a step restriction.\n\nApply this coordinatewise with\n\n    b_a=-2 kappa_1 r_{n,a}(W^{(2)})^T delta_a^{(2)}.\n\nThe raw finite trajectories themselves have dimension-independent bounds on every [0,T]. Indeed bounded phi_2 gives a discrete Gronwall bound on the readout supremum directly from its update and |r_{n,a}|<=|y_a|+B_2||W^{(3)}||_infty. This bounds delta^{(2)}. The first raw update then has normalized size at most C_T eta ||W^{(2)}||_op, while the matrix update has operator size at most C_T eta(1+sum_a ||z_a^{(1)}||_2/sqrt(n)). Adding these bounds gives another discrete linear Gronwall estimate. Initial root RMS and initial matrix operator norm are bounded with probability tending to one. Consequently, on such events,\n\n    ||b_a||_2/sqrt(n)<=C_T,    ||b_a||_infty<=C_T sqrt(n).\n\nThus eta_n sqrt(n)->0 guarantees the scalar step condition throughout the interval for all sufficiently large n. Lift every raw trajectory recursively by the exact increments Delta X. The scalar flow identity ensures z_{a,k}^{(1)}=J(X_{a,k},z_{a,0}^{(1)}) exactly at every step. Its transformed first update differs from Euler by a vector whose normalized norm is at most\n\n    L eta_n^2 (sum_i b_{a,i}^4/n)^{1/2}\n       <= L eta_n^2 ||b_a||_infty ||b_a||_2/sqrt(n)\n       <= C_T eta_n^2 sqrt(n).\n\nThe other two parameter updates already have the transformed Euler form. Summing their stable error recurrence, including the ordinary O_T(eta_n^2) flow defect, yields\n\n    sup_{t<=T} d_n(lifted GD(t), finite flow(t))\n       <= C_T(eta_n+eta_n sqrt(n)).\n\nAt interpolation times, comparing the scalar flow segment with the linear raw parameter segment gives the same vanishing bound (and the between-grid movement is O_T(eta_n) in normalized size). This proves the claimed raw GD bridge with no hidden condition involving the magnitude of the initial scalar transform.\n\nCombining this estimate, the finite-mesh oracle limit, and the two O_T(Delta) mesh errors proves the joint width/step conclusion. The limiting paths have compact time-indexed families of backward fields in mean square. Their uniformly vanishing squared tails permit clipping the remaining phi_1'(Z) times backward-field products, passing bounded measurements through the already proved limit, and removing clipping. This is the prior proof's argument for kernel entries and integrated squared speeds; it requires neither a sub-Gaussian assumption nor a global-in-time Gaussian-response estimate.\n\n## Covered examples and exact limitations\n\nAs either activation, arctan, tanh, logistic sigmoid, erf with any fixed nonzero input scaling, and softsign z/(1+|z|) satisfy the stated bounded C^{1,1} assumptions. Softsign need not be twice continuously differentiable: its first derivative 1/(1+|z|)^2 is continuous, bounded, and globally Lipschitz, which is sufficient. The smooth bounded rational saturation z/sqrt(1+z^2) also qualifies. Sine and cosine qualify despite derivative zeros and sign changes.\n\nFor the first activation only, the class additionally includes affine functions, softplus, GELU, and SiLU: their first derivatives are bounded and globally Lipschitz, although the functions themselves are unbounded. ReLU, leaky ReLU, hard tanh, and other derivative-jump activations are not covered by this theorem. No assertion about their limits is being made.\n\nFor a first-layer erf activation, using the unshifted F(Z_0) as an L2 state would needlessly exclude some ordinary Gaussian initializations because F grows rapidly. The scalar-flow/displacement construction above avoids that issue entirely.\n\nFor nonorthogonal inputs, the first-layer equation is instead\n\n    dot Z_a^{(1)}=-2 kappa_1 sum_b G_ab r_b phi_1'(Z_b^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*delta_b^{(2)}.\n\nIts a-th equation is not phi_1'(Z_a^{(1)}) times a scalar backward coefficient. The scalar coordinate proof therefore does not apply. The theorem above must not be used to promote the separately established arbitrary-angle local result to a global-time result.\n"}]

## Ordinal 2253 agent_message /root/three_input_limit 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/three_input_limit
Payload:
I could not rigorously extend the arbitrary-nonorthogonal-input theorem to every finite horizon. The exact audit and a useful conditional theorem are in [GLOBAL_MULTIINPUT_EXTENSION_ATTEMPT.md](/tmp/GLOBAL_MULTIINPUT_EXTENSION_ATTEMPT.md).

The missing estimate can be weaker than the current Gaussian-tail lemma: for every finite \(T\), it suffices to prove

\[
\sup_{\Delta}\max_{a,k:\,k\Delta\le T}
\mathbb E\exp\!\left(c_T
\left|(W_k^{(2)})^*\delta_{a,k}^{(2)}\right|
\right)<\infty
\]

for some \(c_T>0\).

Conditional on this, I proved the whole all-finite-horizon extension: divide \([0,T]\) into finitely many intervals short enough that the exponential tail beats the localized Gronwall amplification. Population Euler becomes Cauchy, yielding existence and uniqueness; the same oracle argument gives actual-GD convergence for every \(\eta_n\to0\).

What is already global—bounded readout, matrix operator norm, residuals and average squared speeds—does **not** prove that exponential estimate. The troublesome product remains

\[
[\phi'(Z)-\phi'(\widetilde Z)]\,\widetilde P.
\]

The local response induction explicitly needs a small-time coefficient to close. Restarting it is not automatic: the new fields are correlated with the original matrix through their entire history, not independent initialization roots. A tail bound at one endpoint plus a small \(L^2\) change does not preserve exponential tails.

The file also gives an explicit reused-Gaussian-matrix example showing why bounded operator norm and bounded query amplitudes alone cannot supply uniform exponential moments.

I checked a related global DMFT primary theorem, [Nishiyama–Imaizumi, arXiv:2602.06320](https://arxiv.org/html/2602.06320v1). Its globally Lipschitz \(h(\theta)+X^\top\ell(X\theta)\) framework does not directly cover our multiplicative first-layer gate and trained middle matrix.

Therefore, the safe claim is: **arbitrary-input local theorem, plus a precise sufficient global moment criterion that remains unproved here**. No global arbitrary-angle theorem should be claimed from the present estimates.

Completed [GLOBAL_ACTIVATION_TRANSFORM_EXTENSION.md](/tmp/GLOBAL_ACTIVATION_TRANSFORM_EXTENSION.md).

The all-finite-time theorem covers one input or any fixed number of mutually orthogonal inputs, with:

- \(\phi_1\in C^1\), bounded globally Lipschitz derivative; \(\phi_1\) itself may be unbounded, nonmonotone, or have derivative zeros.
- Bounded \(\phi_2\in C^1\) with bounded globally Lipschitz derivative.
- Exact GD whenever \(\eta_n\sqrt n\to0\).
- Any vanishing Gaussian readout standard deviation \(\sigma_3n^{-\beta}\), \(\beta>0\), or a fixed bounded iid nonvanishing readout law.

The key coordinate is the scalar flow \(J_s=\phi_1'(J)\). An exact effective-clock calculation proves the raw-GD defect even across activation classes with derivative zeros/sign changes.

The file includes the fixed-program theorem’s verified hypotheses, global existence and autonomy, the GD bridge, explicit limitations for correlated inputs and unbounded readout initialization, and full strict short-time feature-learning conclusions under zero limiting readout, nonaffine \(\phi_1\), and nonconstant \(\phi_2\).

Covered examples include tanh, sigmoid, erf, softsign, rational saturation, sine, and cosine in either layer; softplus, GELU, and SiLU additionally qualify in the first layer.

## Ordinal 2303 agent_message /root/three_input_limit 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/three_input_limit
Payload:
**PASS**, assuming \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\kappa_1,\kappa_2,\kappa_3>0\) and that the flow with the required mean-square continuity has already been established.

Your argument for \(V\succ0\) correctly handles flat spots. From \(c^\top Vc=0\), continuity and Gaussian full support give

\[
S(s)\sum_a c_a\phi_2'(s_a)=0
\]

everywhere. At a zero of \(S\), either every derivative vanishes or a nonzero derivative of \(S\) provides nearby points with \(S\ne0\). Thus \(\sum_a c_a\phi_2'(s_a)=0\) everywhere. Since bounded nonconstant \(\phi_2\) cannot have constant derivative, varying one coordinate proves every \(c_a=0\).

The later nondegeneracy steps also check out. In particular,

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(P\mid\text{first-layer roots})=\sigma_2^2V.
\]

Writing \(q_a=\phi_1'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})\), continuity and nonconstancy of \(\phi_1\) imply \(\mathbb E q_a^2,\mathbb E q_a^4>0\). Hence

\[
\mathbb E T_a^2\ge \sigma_2^2V_{aa}\mathbb E q_a^2>0.
\]

For \(J_a=y_aq_a^2P_a\),

\[
\operatorname{dist}_{L^2}
\!\left(J_a,\operatorname{span}\{H_{0,b}^{(1)}\}_b\right)^2
\ge \sigma_2^2V_{aa}\mathbb E q_a^4>0.
\]

The next forward call therefore has a genuinely fresh Gaussian component. The learned-matrix contribution is measurable in the old second-layer variables and cannot cancel it.

The middle leading rank operator is nonzero because

\[
\left\|\sum_a y_aU_a\otimes H_{0,a}^{(1)}\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
=y^\top(Q\circ V)y>0.
\]

Finally, defining \(D_{ab}=\mathbb E[q_aP_aq_bP_b]\), the weighted kernel expansion is

\[
y^\top K(t)y
=
\kappa_3\mathbb E S^2+
8\kappa_3^2
\left[
\kappa_1\sum_aD_{aa}
+\kappa_2y^\top(Q\circ V)y
\right]t^2+o(t^2),
\]

with strictly positive coefficient.

The best-affine-error claims are valid: Gaussian full support and continuity exclude an exact affine representation unless the activation itself is affine. Their positivity persists by mean-square continuity.

No monotonicity, analyticity, or assertion \(S\ne0\) almost surely is needed. These are nondegeneracy conclusions; they do not independently establish existence for this broader activation class.

Hence no finite-time blow-up or loss of the bounded-state conditions can occur. The flow is autonomous, not merely a chosen interpolation of limiting observations.\n\n## The finite-width and mesh argument\n\nUse the fixed-program specialization of Setup 2.2 and Theorem 2.10 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685), also stated in Section 3 of the audited proof. For a fixed finite calculation with iid jointly Gaussian initial coordinate tuples, independent Gaussian matrices of variance sigma_2^2/n, and polynomially bounded coordinate instructions, all fixed same-layer polynomially bounded empirical measurements converge to the prescribed population expectations, retaining reused-transpose responses. This invocation concerns fixed programs only, not calculations whose length grows with width.\n\nIts hypotheses hold here. The m initial preactivations form a Gaussian tuple, and an additional independent Gaussian root can generate a bounded readout law. The maps J(X,Z_0) and phi_1(J(X,Z_0)) are continuous and linearly bounded. The second activation, its derivative, and the first derivative are bounded. Products defining delta^{(2)} have polynomial growth. Each fixed-mesh rank expansion makes only finitely many initial-matrix calls in either direction. All residuals and contractions in the oracle are supplied deterministic population numbers. Vanishing readout initialization perturbations are transferred separately by the dimension-independent stability estimate; they are not passed to a fixed-program theorem as width-dependent functions.\n\nAll oracle products defining delta^{(2)} have a deterministically bounded readout factor on a fixed mesh; clipping that factor outside its proven bound makes the coordinate instruction globally Lipschitz without changing the oracle. For state comparisons the Gaussian roots stay fixed, so J and the first activation are uniformly Lipschitz in their varying clock arguments; no joint global Lipschitz bound in the frozen root is asserted or needed. Thus the oracle identification and the state stability use exactly their respective, different hypotheses.\n\nThe population transformed Euler method has O_T(Delta) state error. This follows from the bounded vector field and its bounded Lipschitz constant on the larger stopped set, the O(Delta^2) local defect, and the recurrence e_{k+1}<=(1+C_T Delta)e_k+C_T Delta^2. The readout supremum stopping bound is controlled separately through its update, exactly as in the prior proof. The estimates also hold uniformly in width for finite transformed Euler.\n\nFor each fixed Delta, expand the trained middle matrix as its initialization plus the finite sum of rank-one updates. Supply the oracle with the population residuals and all population contractions in those expansions. The stated fixed-program theorem proves joint empirical convergence of every oracle node and contraction. The difference between recomputing the proxy matrix action and the prescribed oracle node is a finite sum of vectors multiplied by empirical-minus-population contractions. Its normalized norm vanishes. The same Lipschitz state estimate transfers this fixed-mesh limit from the oracle to the actual transformed finite Euler method. In this paragraph only, write A=phi_1', let L=Lip(A), and consider a raw update\n\n    z^+=z+eta A(z)b.\n\nIf L eta |b|<=1/2 and A(z) is nonzero, every point between z and z^+ lies in the same component of {A!=0}, because\n\n    |A(z+s eta A(z)b)-A(z)| <= L eta |A(z)b| <= |A(z)|/2,\n    0<=s<=1.\n\nDefine its exact scalar-flow clock increment by\n\n    Delta X = integral_z^{z^+} du/A(u).\n\nThen z^+=J(Delta X,z). Substituting u=z+s eta A(z)b gives\n\n    |Delta X-eta b|\n       <= eta |b| integral_0^1\n             |A(z)/A(z+s eta A(z)b)-1| ds\n       <= L eta^2 b^2.\n\nIf A(z)=0, the raw step is fixed; set Delta X=eta b. Since J(s,z)=z for every s at an equilibrium, the same exact representation and error bound hold. If L=0 the derivative is constant and the transformation is exact, without a step restriction.\n\nApply this coordinatewise with\n\n    b_a=-2 kappa_1 r_{n,a}(W^{(2)})^T delta_a^{(2)}.\n\nThe raw finite trajectories themselves have dimension-independent bounds on every [0,T]. Indeed bounded phi_2 gives a discrete Gronwall bound on the readout supremum directly from its update and |r_{n,a}|<=|y_a|+B_2||W^{(3)}||_infty. This bounds delta^{(2)}. The first raw update then has normalized size at most C_T eta ||W^{(2)}||_op, while the matrix update has operator size at most C_T eta(1+sum_a ||z_a^{(1)}||_2/sqrt(n)). Adding these bounds gives another discrete linear Gronwall estimate. Initial root RMS and initial matrix operator norm are bounded with probability tending to one. Consequently, on such events,\n\n    ||b_a||_2/sqrt(n)<=C_T,    ||b_a||_infty<=C_T sqrt(n).\n\nThus eta_n sqrt(n)->0 guarantees the scalar step condition throughout the interval for all sufficiently large n. Lift every raw trajectory recursively by the exact increments Delta X. The scalar flow identity ensures z_{a,k}^{(1)}=J(X_{a,k},z_{a,0}^{(1)}) exactly at every step. Its transformed first update differs from Euler by a vector whose normalized norm is at most\n\n    L eta_n^2 (sum_i b_{a,i}^4/n)^{1/2}\n       <= L eta_n^2 ||b_a||_infty ||b_a||_2/sqrt(n)\n       <= C_T eta_n^2 sqrt(n).\n\nThe other two parameter updates already have the transformed Euler form. Summing their stable error recurrence, including the ordinary O_T(eta_n^2) flow defect, yields\n\n    sup_{t<=T} d_n(lifted GD(t), finite flow(t))\n       <= C_T(eta_n+eta_n sqrt(n)).\n\nAt interpolation times, comparing the scalar flow segment with the linear raw parameter segment gives the same vanishing bound (and the between-grid movement is O_T(eta_n) in normalized size). This proves the claimed raw GD bridge with no hidden condition involving the magnitude of the initial scalar transform.\n\nCombining this estimate, the finite-mesh oracle limit, and the two O_T(Delta) mesh errors proves the joint width/step conclusion. The limiting paths have compact time-indexed families of backward fields in mean square. Their uniformly vanishing squared tails permit clipping the remaining phi_1'(Z) times backward-field products, passing bounded measurements through the already proved limit, and removing clipping. This is the prior proof's argument for kernel entries and integrated squared speeds; it requires neither a sub-Gaussian assumption nor a global-in-time Gaussian-response estimate.\n\n## Covered examples and exact limitations\n\nAs either activation, arctan, tanh, logistic sigmoid, erf with any fixed nonzero input scaling, and softsign z/(1+|z|) satisfy the stated bounded C^{1,1} assumptions. Softsign need not be twice continuously differentiable: its first derivative 1/(1+|z|)^2 is continuous, bounded, and globally Lipschitz, which is sufficient. The smooth bounded rational saturation z/sqrt(1+z^2) also qualifies. Sine and cosine qualify despite derivative zeros and sign changes.\n\nFor the first activation only, the class additionally includes affine functions, softplus, GELU, and SiLU: their first derivatives are bounded and globally Lipschitz, although the functions themselves are unbounded. ReLU, leaky ReLU, hard tanh, and other derivative-jump activations are not covered by this theorem. No assertion about their limits is being made.\n\nFor a first-layer erf activation, using the unshifted F(Z_0) as an L2 state would needlessly exclude some ordinary Gaussian initializations because F grows rapidly. The scalar-flow/displacement construction above avoids that issue entirely.\n\nFor nonorthogonal inputs, the first-layer equation is instead\n\n    dot Z_a^{(1)}=-2 kappa_1 sum_b G_ab r_b phi_1'(Z_b^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*delta_b^{(2)}.\n\nIts a-th equation is not phi_1'(Z_a^{(1)}) times a scalar backward coefficient. The scalar coordinate proof therefore does not apply. The theorem above must not be used to promote the separately established arbitrary-angle local result to a global-time result.\n\n## Strict feature learning on a fixed short interval\n\nThe global existence theorem can additionally retain all the earlier strict short-time feature-learning conclusions. Assume sigma_1,sigma_2>0, all kappa_l>0, labels y_a in {-1,1}, zero limiting readout, phi_1 is not affine, and phi_2 is not constant. Keep the activation regularity assumptions of the theorem. Neither derivative needs a fixed sign, and either may vanish on intervals. The conclusions below are short-time conclusions along the globally existing solution, not assertions that hidden speeds stay positive forever.\n\nThe orthogonal first-layer roots are independent N(0,sigma_1^2). Set\n\n    Q_ab=E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].\n\nWriting mu=E[phi_1(sigma_1 g)] and v=Var(phi_1(sigma_1 g)), independence gives Q=v I+mu^2 11^T. The variance v is positive because the continuous nonconstant phi_1 cannot be constant on the full support of a nondegenerate Gaussian. Hence Q is positive definite. The initial second-layer tuple Y_a=Z_{0,a}^{(2)} is Gaussian with covariance sigma_2^2 Q and full support.\n\nUse the recurring quantities\n\n    S=sum_a y_a phi_2(Y_a),\n    U_a=S phi_2'(Y_a),\n    P_a=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,\n    B_a=phi_1'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,\n    V_ab=E[U_aU_b],    D_ab=E[B_aB_b],\n    T_a=y_a B_a.\n\nThe matrix V is positive definite even if phi_2 has flat parts. Suppose sum_a c_a U_a=0 almost surely. Continuity and Gaussian full support give the identity\n\n    (sum_a y_a phi_2(s_a))(sum_a c_a phi_2'(s_a))=0\n\nfor every s in R^m. At any point where the first factor is nonzero, the second is zero. At a point where the first is zero but at least one phi_2'(s_a) is nonzero, its corresponding partial derivative y_a phi_2'(s_a) is nonzero; nearby points therefore have nonzero first factor, and continuity again makes the second zero. If every phi_2'(s_a) is zero, the second is zero directly. Thus sum_a c_a phi_2'(s_a)=0 everywhere. The derivative phi_2' is nonconstant, since a bounded function with constant derivative would be constant. Varying one s_a at a time forces each c_a=0. This proves V positive definite without division by S on its possible positive-probability zero set.\n\nThe first reused transpose response is\n\n    P_a=sum_c H_{0,c}^{(1)}[Q^{-1}E(Y U_a)]_c+Gamma_a,\n    Gamma~N(0,sigma_2^2 V),\n\nwhere Gamma is independent of the first-layer roots. Consequently for every nonzero deterministic c,\n\n    E[(sum_a c_a B_a)^2]\n       >= sigma_2^2 lambda_min(V) sum_a c_a^2 E[phi_1'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})^2] > 0.\n\nThe last inequality holds because a continuously differentiable nonconstant phi_1 has a nonzero derivative on some interval, which has positive Gaussian probability. Thus D is positive definite even with derivative zeros and sign changes. In particular each T_a is nonzero in mean square.\n\nDefine\n\n    M_a=sum_b y_b Q_ab U_b,\n    A_a=phi_1'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}) T_a\n       =y_a phi_1'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})^2 P_a,\n    R_a^kappa=kappa_2 M_a+kappa_1 W_0^{(2)}A_a.\n\nThe next initial-matrix forward call has a strictly positive fresh Gaussian part. To see this, subtract from A_a its mean-square projection onto span{H_{0,b}^{(1)}} and denote the remainder A_a^perp. ## Ordinal 2340 agent_message /root/two_input_full_audit_b 
Message Type: FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: /root
Sender: /root/two_input_full_audit_b
Payload:
PASS for the theorem through initialization, global existence, fixed-mesh identification, and raw-GD convergence, with the previously established common-operator/oracle framework understood.

The important checks pass:

- \(J\) works for derivatives that vanish or change sign. Scalar uniqueness with an integrable time-dependent coefficient gives the exact raw-flow representation.
- Unbounded \(\phi_1\), including GELU, causes no missing \(L^\infty\) requirement. Its \(L^2\) norm controls every relevant rank-one and matrix-action estimate. The necessary supremum bound is on the readout, and the proof supplies it.
- Bounded nonvanishing readout initialization is admissible; an unbounded order-one Gaussian readout is correctly excluded.
- Theorem 2.10 of TP III admits polynomially bounded instructions. Thus \(J\)’s linear growth suffices; its potentially exponential sensitivity to its frozen root is irrelevant to the fixed-root stability argument. The deterministic-coefficient oracle avoids an unsupported pseudo-Lipschitz invocation.
- The direct discrete Gronwall bounds are valid without GD loss dissipation. The signed clock formula and its \(L_1\eta^2b^2\) error are correct, giving the stated sufficient condition \(\eta_n\sqrt n\to0\).

Two small proof completions would improve the written argument:

1. Explicitly bound the lifted GD clocks:
   \[
   \sup_{k\eta\le T}\|X_k\|_{\mathrm{RMS}}
   \le C_T(1+\eta\sqrt n).
   \]
   This follows by summing the clock increments. Raw preactivation bounds alone would not control \(X\) when \(\phi_1'\) vanishes.

2. For the claimed \(P_2\) hidden-path laws, state the time-discretization estimate
   \[
   \frac1n\sum_i\sup_t|Z_i(t)-Z_i(\pi_h t)|^2
   \le h\int_0^T\|\dot Z(t)\|_{\mathrm{RMS}}^2\,dt.
   \]
   Together with uniform hidden-velocity bounds and fixed-grid convergence, this supplies the stronger path-space assertion, not merely \(\sup_t\) RMS convergence.

Keep the conclusions at second moments/quadratic observables unless additional higher-moment estimates are proved. Also retain the shared-matrix joint root law: orthogonality does not make second-layer initial preactivations independent when \(\mathbb E\phi_1(\sigma_1G)\ne0.

The first-layer and second-layer populations are separate.\n\nThe initial \\(W_0^{(2)}\\) is the bounded forward-and-adjoint action obtained from the joint limits of finite Gaussian matrix calculations, as described below. It is **not** an ordinary continuum matrix or integral kernel with iid Gaussian entries. The notation records its action on generated population fields, together with its adjoint.\n\nThe scalar chain rule converts (6) into the ordinary first-layer equation\n\\[\n\\dot Z_a^{(1)}=-2\\kappa_1 r_a\\delta_a^{(1)}.\n\\tag{7}\n\\]\nConversely, any solution of (7) in the bounded state class used below has representation (5). Indeed, Cauchy–Schwarz and Fubini make its backward coefficient integrable in time at almost every coordinate. For an integrable scalar coefficient \\(b(t)\\), the equation\n\\[\n\\dot z(t)=b(t)(\\phi^{(1)})'(z(t))\n\\]\nhas the solution\n\\[\nz(t)=J\\!\\left(\\int_0^t b(s)\\,ds,z(0)\\right).\n\\]\nDifferentiation verifies the equation, and the Lipschitz difference inequality with integrable coefficient \\(|b(t)|L_1\\) proves uniqueness. Thus the scalar coordinate changes neither the original gradient flow nor its possible solutions.\n\nWhen \\((\\phi^{(1)})'>0\\), one can use\n\\[\nF'(z)=\\frac1{(\\phi^{(1)})'(z)},\n\\qquad\nJ(s,z)=F^{-1}(F(z)+s).\n\\]\nBut the displacement formulation does not require\n\\(\\mathbb E[F(Z_0^{(1)})^2]<\\infty\\). The rapid growth of this integral for an erf activation therefore places no restriction on its Gaussian initialization variance.\n\n## Global existence, uniqueness, and autonomy\n\nFor a population variable \\(U\\), write\n\\[\n\\|U\\|_{L^2}:=\\sqrt{\\mathbb E[U^2]}.\n\\]\nThe operator norm is its largest amplification of this root-mean-square size. Compare two states with the same fixed first-layer roots using\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\nd={}&\n\\sum_{a=1}^m\n\\|X_a^{(1)}-\\widetilde X_a^{(1)}\\|_{L^2}\\\\\n&+\\|W^{(2)}-\\widetilde W^{(2)}\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\n+\\|W^{(3)}-\\widetilde W^{(3)}\\|_{L^2}.\n\\end{aligned} \\tag{8}\n\\]\nAt finite width replace every population \\(L^2\\) norm by the ordinary Euclidean norm divided by \\(\\sqrt n\\); denote this distance by \\(d_n\\).\n\nOn sets with bounded clock \\(L^2\\) norms, matrix operator norms, and readout suprema, the transformed vector field is Lipschitz in (8), with a constant independent of width. The key bounds are as follows. Equation (4) controls first-activation differences, and\n\\[\n\\|H_a^{(1)}\\|_{L^2}\n\\le\n|\\phi^{(1)}(0)|\n+M_1\\|Z_{0,a}^{(1)}\\|_{L^2}\n+M_1^2\\|X_a^{(1)}\\|_{L^2}.\n\\tag{9}\n\\]\nAdding and subtracting one factor bounds the forward matrix difference. The second-layer backward difference satisfies\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\|\\delta_a^{(2)}-\\widetilde\\delta_a^{(2)}\\|_{L^2}\n\\le{}&\n\\|(\\phi^{(2)})'\\|_\\infty\n\\|W^{(3)}-\\widetilde W^{(3)}\\|_{L^2}\\\\\n&+\n\\|\\widetilde W^{(3)}\\|_\\infty\n\\operatorname{Lip}((\\phi^{(2)})')\n\\|Z_a^{(2)}-\\widetilde Z_a^{(2)}\\|_{L^2}.\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{10}\n\\]\nIts adjoint response is Lipschitz by the operator bound. The output uses boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of \\(\\phi^{(2)}\\). Finally,\n\\[\n\\|u\\otimes v-\\widetilde u\\otimes\\widetilde v\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\n\\le\n\\|u-\\widetilde u\\|_{L^2}\\|v\\|_{L^2}\n+\n\\|\\widetilde u\\|_{L^2}\\|v-\\widetilde v\\|_{L^2}.\n\\]\nThese estimates prove local existence and uniqueness by contracting the integrated equations on a short interval. A common readout supremum bound defines a closed complete set under (8), and its integral update preserves that bound after the interval is made short enough.\n\nThe chain rule and the adjoint identity give\n\\[\n\\dot f=-2Kr,\n\\qquad\n\\dot L=-4r^\\top Kr\\le0,\n\\tag{11}\n\\]\nwhere\n\\[\nK=\\kappa_1K^{(1)}+\\kappa_2K^{(2)}+\\kappa_3K^{(3)}\n\\]\nand\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\nK_{ab}^{(1)}\n&=\\mathbf1_{\\{a=b\\}}\\,\n\\mathbb E[\\delta_a^{(1)}\\delta_b^{(1)}],\\\\\nK_{ab}^{(2)}\n&=\\mathbb E[H_a^{(1)}H_b^{(1)}]\\,\n  \\mathbb E[\\delta_a^{(2)}\\delta_b^{(2)}],\\\\\nK_{ab}^{(3)}\n&=\\mathbb E[H_a^{(2)}H_b^{(2)}].\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{12}\n\\]\nEach block is the Gram matrix of the corresponding parameter gradients. Thus \\(\\|r(t)\\|_2\\le\\|r(0)\\|_2\\).\n\nLet \\(B_2=\\|\\phi^{(2)}\\|_\\infty\\). Equations (6) and (9) then give\n\\[\n\\|\\dot X_a^{(1)}\\|_{L^2}\n\\le C_T\\|W^{(2)}\\|_{\\mathrm{op}},\n\\qquad\n\\|\\dot W^{(2)}\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\n\\le C_T\\left(1+\\sum_a\\|X_a^{(1)}\\|_{L^2}\\right).\n\\tag{14}\n\\]\nThe fixed initial root norms are absorbed into \\(C_T\\). Adding and integrating these inequalities gives a linear Gronwall bound on the clock norms and matrix operator norm throughout \\([0,T]\\). No finite-time blow-up or loss of the bounded-state conditions is possible. The solution therefore extends uniquely to every finite horizon.\n\nThe original state is autonomous as well. At a restart time, take the current \\(Z_a^{(1)}\\) as the new roots in (5), set the new clocks to zero, and repeat the same argument. The original equations require only current fields and the current matrix action and adjoint. More formally, the closed spaces generated from current fields by the bounded coordinate operations and these actions contain the future integral construction. Equal current joint action laws give an isometry of these spaces that preserves the equations. Uniqueness then gives equal future action laws. Earlier clock values and the original initialization are not additional information needed for the future.\n\n## Identifying the population action and passing to the width limit\n\nWe use the fixed-program specialization of Setup 2.2 and Theorem 2.10 of [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685). For a fixed finite calculation with iid jointly Gaussian initial coordinate tuples, independent Gaussian matrices of variance \\(\\sigma_2^2/n\\), and polynomially bounded coordinate instructions, fixed same-layer polynomially bounded empirical measurements converge to their prescribed population expectations. The limit retains the responses from reused transpose calls. This theorem is applied only to fixed programs, never to a program whose length grows with width.\n\nIts hypotheses hold here. The initial preactivations form a Gaussian tuple; an independent Gaussian root can generate a bounded readout law. Equations (3)–(4) make \\(J(X,Z_0)\\) and \\(\\phi^{(1)}(J(X,Z_0))\\) linearly bounded. The other activation and derivative maps are bounded. The backward products have polynomial growth. At fixed mesh, the rank expansion makes finitely many matrix and transpose calls, with deterministic population residuals and contractions. Vanishing initialization perturbations are transferred separately by stability, not inserted as width-dependent coordinate functions.\n\nFor clarity, the common initial action is constructed before solving the flow. Collect the finite calculations for rational meshes, their finite unions, and a countable closure under the coordinate operations and bounded continuous measurements used here. The fixed-program theorem gives consistent finite joint laws, realized on one coordinate space for each layer. Exact finite linear identities pass to these laws. A Gaussian matrix operator bound implies, with a fixed sufficiently large \\(M\\),\n\\[\n\\|W_0^{(2)}V\\|_{L^2}\n\\le M\\|V\\|_{L^2}\n\\]\nfor every generated \\(V\\); the same holds for the transpose action. For example the finite bound follows from two \\(1/4\\)-nets and a union bound,\n\\[\n\\mathbb P\\!\\left(\\|W_0^{(2)}\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}>M\\right)\n\\le\n2\\,9^{2n}\n\\exp\\!\\left(-\\frac{nM^2}{8\\sigma_2^2}\\right)\n\\longrightarrow0\n\\]\nwhen \\(\\sigma_2>0\\); the action is zero when \\(\\sigma_2=0\\).\nHence the actions are well-defined on variables equal in mean square and extend to the closures of the generated spans. The finite transpose identity passes to\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[U\\,W_0^{(2)}V]\n=\n\\mathbb E[((W_0^{(2)})^*U)V].\n\\tag{15}\n\\]\nThis identifies the forward and backward actions as adjoints on the same two fixed spaces. It is the initial object used in (6).\n\nFor a proof mesh \\(\\Delta\\), Euler applied to (6) has error \\(O_T(\\Delta)\\), uniformly in width and also in the population space. The bounded vector field and its Lipschitz constant give a one-step error \\(C_T\\Delta^2\\), so\n\\[\ne_{k+1}\\le(1+C_T\\Delta)e_k+C_T\\Delta^2.\n\\]\nSummation gives the stated error. A first-exit argument keeps the clock and matrix inside slightly larger bounds; the readout supremum is controlled separately by its update, as in (13).\n\nAt fixed \\(\\Delta\\), expand the trained matrix as its initialization plus finitely many rank-one updates. Construct the oracle using the population residuals and every population contraction in those expansions. The fixed-program theorem identifies all its joint node laws and quadratic contractions. Applying its proxy matrix to an oracle vector differs from the prescribed node by finitely many terms of the form\n\\[\n-2\\kappa_2\\Delta r_{b,s}\\,\n\\delta_{b,s,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(2)}\n\\left[\n\\frac{\n(h_{b,s,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)})^\\top\nh_{a,k,\\mathrm{oracle}}^{(1)}\n}{n}\n-\n\\mathbb E[H_{b,s}^{(1)}H_{a,k}^{(1)}]\n\\right].\n\\tag{16}\n\\]\nEach scalar discrepancy vanishes and each vector has bounded root-mean-square norm. The corresponding transpose discrepancies use contractions of two second-layer backward fields. Thus recomputed proxy quantities approach the oracle nodes.\n\nThe state estimate (8) transfers this identification to finite Euler with empirical feedback. All factors involving the readout can be clipped beyond its proven supremum bound without changing any oracle value. For comparisons the roots stay fixed: \\(J\\) and its first activation are uniformly Lipschitz in their changing clock arguments. A joint global Lipschitz bound in the frozen root is neither asserted nor needed.\n\nConsequently, taking \\(n\\to\\infty\\) at fixed \\(\\Delta\\), and then \\(\\Delta\\to0\\), proves the population limit of the finite continuous flow on every \\([0,T]\\).\n\n## The bridge from exact GD\n\nA scalar estimate makes the discrete argument work even when the first derivative vanishes or changes sign. In the following calculation only, write\n\\[\nA=(\\phi^{(1)})',\n\\qquad L=\\operatorname{Lip}(A),\n\\qquad\nz^+=z+\\eta A(z)b.\n\\]\nIf \\(L\\eta|b|\\le1/2\\) and \\(A(z)\\ne0\\), then for \\(0\\le s\\le1\\),\n\\[\n|A(z+s\\eta A(z)b)-A(z)|\n\\le L\\eta|A(z)b|\n\\le \\frac{|A(z)|}{2}.\n\\]\nThe whole segment stays in the same interval where \\(A\\ne0\\). Define its exact scalar-flow clock increment by\n\\[\n\\Delta X=\\int_z^{z^+}\\frac{du}{A(u)}.\n\\]\nThen \\(z^+=J(\\Delta X,z)\\). Substitution in the integral gives\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n|\\Delta X-\\eta b|\n&\\le\n\\eta|b|\\int_0^1\n\\left|\n\\frac{A(z)}{A(z+s\\eta A(z)b)}-1\n\\right|\\,ds\\\\\n&\\le L\\eta^2b^2.\n\\end{aligned} \\tag{17}\n\\]\nIf \\(A(z)=0\\), the raw step fixes \\(z\\). Set \\(\\Delta X=\\eta b\\); the same exact representation holds because \\(J(s,z)=z\\) at an equilibrium. If \\(L=0\\), the derivative is constant and there is no coordinate defect or step restriction.\n\nApply (17) to each coordinate with\n\\[\nb_a\n"}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': "=\n-2\\kappa_1r_{n,a}\n(W^{(2)})^\\top\\delta_a^{(2)}.\n\\tag{18}\n\\]\nThe raw GD trajectories themselves have width-independent bounds on \\([0,T]\\). First,\n\\[\n|r_{n,a}|\n\\le |y_a|+B_2\\|W^{(3)}\\|_\\infty\n\\]\nand the readout update give a discrete Gronwall bound on its supremum. This bounds \\(\\delta_a^{(2)}\\). The first raw update then has root-mean-square increment at most \\(C_T\\eta_n\\|W^{(2)}\\|_{\\mathrm{op}}\\), while the matrix operator increment is at most\n\\[\nC_T\\eta_n\n\\left(\n1+\\sum_a\\frac{\\|z_a^{(1)}\\|_2}{\\sqrt n}\n\\right).\n\\]\nAdding these bounds gives a second discrete linear Gronwall estimate. The initial root RMS and matrix operator norm are bounded with probability tending to one, and the allowed readout initializations have the requisite high-probability uniform supremum bound. On these events,\n\\[\n\\frac{\\|b_a\\|_2}{\\sqrt n}\\le C_T,\n\\qquad\n\\|b_a\\|_\\infty\\le C_T\\sqrt n.\n\\tag{19}\n\\]\nThus \\(\\eta_n\\sqrt n\\to0\\) enforces the scalar step condition throughout the interval.\n\nLift the raw iterates recursively by these exact clock increments, starting from \\(x_{a,0}^{(1)}=0\\). The scalar flow identity ensures\n\\[\nz_{a,k}^{(1)}\n=\nJ(x_{a,k}^{(1)},z_{a,0}^{(1)})\n\\]\nexactly at every step.
