This proves the raw GD bridge without a moment assumption on an unshifted scalar transform.\n\nCombining (22), the fixed-mesh oracle limit, and the two \\(O_T(\\Delta)\\) mesh errors proves the joint width/step conclusion.\n\nFor the remaining quadratic measurements, the backward fields\n\\[\n(W^{(2)}(t))^*\\delta_a^{(2)}(t)\n\\]\nform a compact time-indexed family in \\(L^2\\). Their squared tails therefore vanish uniformly as the clipping threshold grows. Clip before multiplying by \\((\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_a^{(1)})\\), pass the resulting bounded Lipschitz measurements through the proved limit, and remove clipping. Fixed-grid second-moment convergence and state continuity give the corresponding finite empirical tail control. This supplies kernel entries and squared hidden velocities without assuming sub-Gaussian tails.\n\nFor completeness, uniform velocity-energy bounds also supply the path-law assertion. For a time grid of spacing \\(h\\), let \\(\\pi_h t\\) be its preceding grid point. Cauchy–Schwarz on each coordinate gives\n\\[\n\\frac1n\\sum_i\n\\sup_{t\\le T}\n|z_i(t)-z_i(\\pi_h t)|^2\n\\le\nh\\int_0^T\n\\frac{\\|\\dot z(t)\\|_2^2}{n}\\,dt.\n\\tag{23}\n\\]\nThe population counterpart replaces the empirical average by expectation. The parameter bounds above and the bounded activation derivatives bound these integrals uniformly for each hidden layer. Piecewise-linear reconstruction from the same grid satisfies an analogous estimate with a fixed additional factor. At fixed grid size the joint laws converge with second moments; letting \\(h\\to0\\) gives convergence of the hidden path laws with their second moments in the uniform path norm. The clipped backward-field argument likewise identifies the integrated squared speeds.\n\n## Examples and the input-geometry boundary\n\nEither layer may use arctan, tanh, logistic sigmoid, erf with fixed nonzero input scaling, softsign \\(z/(1+|z|)\\), or the smooth saturation \\(z/\\sqrt{1+z^2}\\). Sine and cosine also qualify, despite derivative zeros and sign changes. Softsign is not twice continuously differentiable at zero, but\n\\[\n\\frac{d}{dz}\\frac{z}{1+|z|}\n=\n\\frac1{(1+|z|)^2}\n\\]\nis continuous, bounded, and globally Lipschitz, which is enough.\n\nIn the first layer only, affine functions, softplus, GELU, and SiLU also qualify: their derivatives are bounded and globally Lipschitz although the activations are unbounded. ReLU, leaky ReLU, hard tanh, and derivative-jump activations are not covered by this theorem.\n\nFor a general input Gram matrix\n\\[\nG_{ab}=\\frac{x_a^\\top x_b}{d},\n\\]\nthe first-layer equation becomes\n\\[\n\\dot Z_a^{(1)}\n=\n-2\\kappa_1\\sum_b\nG_{ab}r_b\n(\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_b^{(1)})\n(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_b^{(2)}.\n\\tag{24}\n\\]\nWith a nonlinear first activation, the right side is not the \\(a\\)-th activation derivative times one scalar backward coefficient. The scalar-coordinate proof therefore does not give a global-time arbitrary-angle theorem.\n\nThere is a verified exception. If\n\\[\n\\phi^{(1)}(z)=cz+d_0,\n\\]\nthen\n\\[\n\\dot Z_a^{(1)}\n=\n-2\\kappa_1c\\sum_b\nG_{ab}r_b(W^{(2)})^*\\delta_b^{(2)}.\n\\tag{25}\n\\]\nThe troublesome multiplication by a varying first-layer derivative has disappeared. In the raw state\n\\((Z_1^{(1)},\\ldots,Z_m^{(1)},W^{(2)},W^{(3)})\\),\nthe vector field is Lipschitz on the same bounded sets. The bounds (13)–(14) hold with raw first-layer norms and constants depending on the fixed \\(G\\). The fixed-program initialization allows the possibly singular Gaussian covariance \\(\\sigma_1^2G\\). The same oracle and mesh proof therefore gives a global population limit for **any fixed finite normalized input configuration**, including singular Gram matrices.\n\nIn this affine case, raw GD is already ordinary Euler for the Lipschitz raw vector field, so the sufficient step condition improves to \\(\\eta_n\\to0\\). All the preceding readout restrictions remain. This exception does not give a nonlinear first layer: its best affine-fit error is exactly zero.\n\n## Strict feature learning on a fixed short interval\n\nThe global theorem retains the earlier strict short-time conclusions under additional assumptions: take \\(\\sigma_1,\\sigma_2>0\\), all \\(\\kappa_\\ell>0\\), labels \\(y_a\\in\\{-1,1\\}\\), zero limiting readout, a nonaffine \\(\\phi^{(1)}\\), and a nonconstant \\(\\phi^{(2)}\\). Keep the activation regularity assumptions above and mutually orthogonal inputs. Neither derivative needs a fixed sign, and both may vanish on intervals. The conclusions in this section hold on a fixed short interval along the globally existing solution; they do not assert strictly positive hidden speeds for all future times.\n\nThe first-layer roots are independent \\(N(0,\\sigma_1^2)\\). Define\n\\[\nQ_{ab}\n=\n\\mathbb E[H_{0,a}^{(1)}H_{0,b}^{(1)}].\n\\]\nFor a standard Gaussian \\(g\\), put\n\\[\n\\mu=\\mathbb E[\\phi^{(1)}(\\sigma_1g)],\n\\qquad\nv=\\operatorname{Var}(\\phi^{(1)}(\\sigma_1g)).\n\\]\nIndependence gives\n\\[\nQ=vI+\\mu^2\\mathbf1\\mathbf1^\\top.\n\\]\nHere \\(v>0\\): a continuous nonconstant activation cannot be constant on a full-support Gaussian law. Thus \\(Q\\) is positive definite. The initial second-layer tuple\n\\[\nY_a:=Z_{0,a}^{(2)}\n\\]\nis Gaussian with covariance \\(\\sigma_2^2Q\\) and full support. Its coordinates need not be independent when \\(\\mu\\ne0\\).\n\nThe following temporary quantities recur throughout the expansion:\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\nS&=\\sum_a y_a\\phi^{(2)}(Y_a),\n&\nU_a&=S(\\phi^{(2)})'(Y_a),\\\\\nP_a&=(W_0^{(2)})^*U_a,\n&\nB_a&=(\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})P_a,\\\\\nV_{ab}&=\\mathbb E[U_aU_b],\n&\nD_{ab}&=\\mathbb E[B_aB_b],\n\\qquad T_a=y_aB_a.\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{26}\n\\]\n\nThe matrix \\(V\\) is positive definite even when the second activation has flat parts. If \\(\\sum_a c_aU_a=0\\) almost surely, continuity and full support give, for every \\(s\\in\\mathbb R^m\\),\n\\[\n\\left(\\sum_a y_a\\phi^{(2)}(s_a)\\right)\n\\left(\\sum_a c_a(\\phi^{(2)})'(s_a)\\right)=0.\n\\tag{27}\n\\]\nWhere the first factor is nonzero, the second is zero. Where the first is zero but some \\((\\phi^{(2)})'(s_a)\\ne0\\), its corresponding partial derivative is nonzero, so nearby points have nonzero first factor; continuity again makes the second factor zero. If every derivative is zero, the second factor is already zero. Consequently,\n\\[\n\\sum_a c_a(\\phi^{(2)})'(s_a)=0\n\\quad\\text{for every }s.\n\\]\nThe derivative \\((\\phi^{(2)})'\\) is nonconstant, since a bounded activation with constant derivative would be constant. Varying one coordinate forces each \\(c_a=0\\). This proves positive definiteness without dividing by \\(S\\) on a possible positive-probability zero set.\n\nThe first reused transpose call has representation\n\\[\nP_a\n=\n\\sum_c H_{0,c}^{(1)}\n[Q^{-1}\\mathbb E(YU_a)]_c+\\Gamma_a,\n\\qquad\n\\Gamma\\sim N(0,\\sigma_2^2V),\n\\tag{28}\n\\]\nwhere \\(\\Gamma\\) is independent of the first-layer roots. Hence, for any nonzero vector \\(c\\),\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\!\\left[\\left(\\sum_a c_aB_a\\right)^2\\right]\n\\ge\n\\sigma_2^2\\lambda_{\\min}(V)\n\\sum_a c_a^2\n\\mathbb E[((\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}))^2]\n>0.\n\\tag{29}\n\\]\nA continuously differentiable nonconstant first activation has nonzero derivative on some interval, of positive Gaussian probability. Thus \\(D\\) is positive definite and every \\(T_a\\) is nonzero in mean square.\n\nDefine\n\\[\nM_a=\\sum_b y_bQ_{ab}U_b,\n\\qquad\nA_a=(\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)})T_a\n=\ny_a((\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}))^2P_a,\n\\]\n\\[\nR_a^\\kappa\n=\n\\kappa_2M_a+\\kappa_1W_0^{(2)}A_a.\n\\tag{30}\n\\]\nEvery \\(R_a^\\kappa\\) is nonzero. To prove this without a cancellation assumption, subtract from \\(A_a\\) its mean-square projection onto the span of the initial \\(H_{0,b}^{(1)}\\), calling the remainder \\(A_a^\\perp\\). Conditional variance in (28) gives\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\mathbb E[(A_a^\\perp)^2]\n&\\ge\n\\mathbb E\\operatorname{Var}(A_a\\mid\nZ_{0,1}^{(1)},\\ldots,Z_{0,m}^{(1)})\\\\\n&=\n\\sigma_2^2V_{aa}\n\\mathbb E[((\\phi^{(1)})'(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}))^4]\n>0.\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{31}\n\\]\nConditioning the initial matrix on the previously revealed forward and transpose calls gives\n\\[\nW_0^{(2)}A_a\n=\n\\sum_b\\alpha_{a,b}Y_b\n+\\sum_b\\beta_{a,b}U_b\n+\n\\sigma_2\\sqrt{\\mathbb E[(A_a^\\perp)^2]}\\,\\gamma_a,\n\\tag{32}\n\\]\nwhere \\(\\gamma_a\\) is standard Gaussian independent of the previous second-layer coordinates, and\n\\[\n\\alpha_a=Q^{-1}\\mathbb E[H_0^{(1)}A_a],\n\\qquad\n\\beta_a=V^{-1}\\mathbb E[PA_a^\\perp].\n\\]\nThe formulas follow by conditioning simultaneously on the known products in both directions; the remaining matrix is an independent Gaussian projected off the explored input and output spans. Their finite-rank output projection vanishes in normalized mean square. This is a separate marginal calculation for each \\(a\\), not a claim that all \\(\\gamma_a\\) are independent.\n\nSince \\(M_a\\) depends only on \\(Y\\), it cannot cancel the independent Gaussian in (32). Therefore\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[(R_a^\\kappa)^2]\n\\ge\n\\kappa_1^2\\sigma_2^2\n\\mathbb E[(A_a^\\perp)^2]>0.\n\\tag{33}\n\\]\n\nSubstitution in the integral equations yields\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\nW^{(3)}(t)\n&=2\\kappa_3tS+o_{L^2}(t),\\\\\n\\delta_a^{(2)}(t)\n&=2\\kappa_3tU_a+o_{L^2}(t),\\\\\nZ_a^{(1)}(t)-Z_{0,a}^{(1)}\n&=2\\kappa_1\\kappa_3t^2T_a+o_{L^2}(t^2),\\\\\nW^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}\n&=\n2\\kappa_2\\kappa_3t^2\n\\sum_b y_bU_b\\otimes H_{0,b}^{(1)}\n+o_{\\mathrm{op}}(t^2),\\\\\nZ_a^{(2)}(t)-Y_a\n&=2\\kappa_3t^2R_a^\\kappa+o_{L^2}(t^2).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{34}\n\\]\nBounded continuous activation derivatives justify the difference quotients along these mean-square converging increments by domination. Boundedness of the first activation itself is unnecessary. Direct substitution in the velocity equations also gives\n\\[\n\\frac{\\dot Z_a^{(1)}(t)}t\n\\longrightarrow 4\\kappa_1\\kappa_3T_a,\n\\qquad\n\\frac{\\dot Z_a^{(2)}(t)}t\n\\longrightarrow 4\\kappa_3R_a^\\kappa\n\\quad\\text{in }L^2.\n\\tag{35}\n\\]\n\nThe middle matrix moves nontrivially even when tested on each fixed initial activation. Its leading response to \\(H_{0,a}^{(1)}\\) is \\(2\\kappa_2\\kappa_3t^2M_a\\). Indeed, with \\(c_b=y_bQ_{ab}\\),\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[M_a^2]=c^\\top Vc>0,\n\\]\nbecause \\(c_a=y_aQ_{aa}\\ne0\\).\n\nPut\n\\[\nA_1=\\sum_a y_a^2D_{aa}>0,\n\\qquad\nA_2=\\sum_{a,b}y_ay_bQ_{ab}V_{ab}>0.\n\\tag{36}\n\\]\nFor the second inequality, decompose \\(Q=\\sum_j v_jv_j^\\top\\). Its entrywise product with \\(V\\) is\n\\[\nQ\\circ V\n=\n\\sum_j\\operatorname{diag}(v_j)V\\operatorname{diag}(v_j)\n\\succeq\n\\lambda_{\\min}(V)\\operatorname{diag}(Q_{11},\\ldots,Q_{mm})\n\\succ0.\n\\]\nAdjunction gives\n\\[\n\\sum_a y_a\\mathbb E[U_aR_a^\\kappa]\n=\n\\kappa_1A_1+\\kappa_2A_2.\n\\tag{37}\n\\]\nConsequently,\n\\[\n\\begin{aligned}\n\\kappa_1K^{(1)}(t)\n&=\n4\\kappa_1\\kappa_3^2t^2\n\\operatorname{diag}(D_{11},\\ldots,D_{mm})\n+o(t^2),\\\\\n\\kappa_2K^{(2)}(t)\n&=\n4\\kappa_2\\kappa_3^2t^2(Q\\circ V)\n+o(t^2),\\\\\ny^\\top K^{(3)}(t)y\n&=\n\\mathbb E[S^2]\n+\n4\\kappa_3(\\kappa_1A_1+\\kappa_2A_2)t^2\n+o(t^2),\\\\\ny^\\top K(t)y\n&=\n\\kappa_3\\mathbb E[S^2]\n+\n8\\kappa_3^2(\\kappa_1A_1+\\kappa_2A_2)t^2\n+o(t^2).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\tag{38}\n\\]\nThe initial readout block is positive definite: a linear dependence among the \\(\\phi^{(2)}(Y_a)\\) would hold everywhere by full support, and varying one coordinate contradicts nonconstancy. Thus all three blocks are positive definite and individually nonconstant for sufficiently small positive times.\n\nFor each input and each hidden layer, (33)–(35) give constants \\(c_{\\ell,a},C_{\\ell,a}>0\\) and a common \\(T_*>0\\) such that\n\\[\nc_{\\ell,a}t\n\\le\n\\sqrt{\\mathbb E[|\\dot Z_a^{(\\ell)}(t)|^2]}\n\\le\nC_{\\ell,a}t,\n\\qquad 0<t\\le T_*.\n\\tag{39}\n\\]\nThe speeds survive the width and step limits at every fixed positive time, but start at zero and vanish linearly as \\(t\\downarrow0\\). Squared displacement has a positive \\(t^4\\) coefficient; integrated squared speed has a positive \\(t^3\\) coefficient. There is no time-independent positive speed lower bound on \\((0,T_*]\\).\n\nBy contrast, the summed loss has a nonzero initial slope:\n\\[\n-\\dot L(0)=4\\kappa_3\\mathbb E[S^2]>0.\n\\]\nAfter shrinking \\(T_*\\), its descent rate remains at least half this positive value throughout \\([0,T_*]\\). Individual residual magnitudes are not asserted to decrease.\n\nFinally, every initial hidden marginal is a nondegenerate Gaussian. The first activation is nonaffine by assumption; the bounded nonconstant second activation is automatically nonaffine. Therefore the best affine-fit error\n\\[\n\\inf_{\\alpha,\\beta}\n\\mathbb E[(\\phi^{(\\ell)}(Z)-\\alpha Z-\\beta)^2]\n=\n\\operatorname{Var}(\\phi^{(\\ell)}(Z))\n-\n\\frac{\\operatorname{Cov}(Z,\\phi^{(\\ell)}(Z))^2}\n{\\operatorname{Var}(Z)}\n\\tag{40}\n\\]\nis initially positive for every hidden marginal. Its moments are continuous along the mean-square continuous paths, since both activations are globally Lipschitz and have at most linear growth. All hidden variances and best-affine-fit errors consequently have common positive lower bounds on a possibly shorter \\([0,T_*]\\).\n\nThese quantitative constants depend on the fixed activations, geometry, and positive hyperparameters, but not on width or learning rate. Global existence is proved on every finite horizon; the strict motion and nonlinearity bounds in this last section are asserted only on the stated fixed short interval.\n"}]

## Ordinal 2431 message assistant final_answer
Yes—a substantial generalization is possible. But the scope needs to be precise:

- For **one input, or any fixed finite collection of orthogonal inputs**, the global population limit extends far beyond arctan.
- For **arbitrary-angle inputs**, including the earlier three-input configuration, I do not have a complete global extension. The earlier local result does not by itself give one.

Here “global” means that the limiting flow exists, is unique, and is approximated by finite-width GD on **every fixed interval \([0,T]\), however large \(T<\infty\) is**. It does not mean that convergence is uniform as \(T\to\infty\), or that feature velocities remain positive forever.

The [full generalized theorem and proof](/tmp/GLOBAL_ACTIVATION_TRANSFORM_EXTENSION.md) contains the details. The central extension is as follows.

## The broader theorem

We can allow different activations, \(\phi^{(1)}\) and \(\phi^{(2)}\), in the two hidden layers. Assume:

- \(\phi^{(1)}\) is continuously differentiable, with bounded, globally Lipschitz derivative. **The activation itself need not be bounded.**
- \(\phi^{(2)}\) is bounded and continuously differentiable, with bounded, globally Lipschitz derivative.

“Globally Lipschitz derivative” means that a fixed constant \(L\) bounds
\[
|\phi'(z)-\phi'(\widetilde z)|\le L|z-\widetilde z|
\]
for all real \(z,\widetilde z\).

Neither activation needs to be monotone, odd, or analytic. Its derivative may vanish or change sign.

Consequently, the same activation can be used in both layers when it is arctan, tanh, sigmoid, erf, softsign, \(z/\sqrt{1+z^2}\), sine, or cosine. The first layer additionally permits softplus, GELU, and SiLU, provided the second activation satisfies the boundedness condition. ReLU and derivative-jump activations are not covered by this proof.

To specify the network and scaling, take \(m\) fixed inputs \(x_a\in\mathbb R^d\), with
\[
\frac{x_a^\top x_a}{d}=1,
\qquad
\frac{x_a^\top x_b}{d}=0\quad(a\ne b),
\]
and any fixed labels \(y_a\), including arbitrary choices of \(\pm1\).

Both hidden layers have width \(n\). The forward pass is
\[
z_a^{(1)}=\frac{W^{(1)}x_a}{\sqrt d},
\qquad
h_a^{(1)}=\phi^{(1)}(z_a^{(1)}),
\]
\[
z_a^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h_a^{(1)},
\qquad
h_a^{(2)}=\phi^{(2)}(z_a^{(2)}),
\]
\[
f_{n,a}=\frac{(W^{(3)})^\top h_a^{(2)}}n,
\qquad
r_{n,a}=f_{n,a}-y_a,
\qquad
L_n=\sum_a r_{n,a}^2.
\]

Here \(W^{(3)}\) is the same rescaled readout throughout. A broad family of permitted initialization and learning-rate choices is:

| Weights | Independent Gaussian entry variance | GD learning rate |
|---|---:|---:|
| \(W^{(1)}\) | \(\sigma_1^2\) | \(n\kappa_1\eta_n\) |
| \(W^{(2)}\) | \(\sigma_2^2/n\) | \(\kappa_2\eta_n\) |
| \(W^{(3)}\) | \(\sigma_3^2n^{-2\beta}\), any \(\beta>0\) | \(n\kappa_3\eta_n\) |

The \(\sigma_\ell,\kappa_\ell\) are fixed constants, with positive learning-rate constants. Use physical time \(t=k\eta_n\). A sufficient joint-limit condition is
\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\longrightarrow0.
\]

Thus neither the original readout exponent \(\beta=1\) nor the specific choice \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) is essential.

Under these assumptions, the finite-width GD trajectories converge to a unique autonomous population gradient flow on every fixed \([0,T]\). For example,
\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}\max_a
|f_{n,a}(t)-f_a(t)|
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]

The conclusion is stronger than prediction convergence: it includes loss, the kernel blocks, joint hidden-layer trajectory distributions with second moments, and the prescribed forward and adjoint measurements of \(W^{(2)}\).

There is also an initialization extension: the limiting readout need not vanish if its initial coordinates have a fixed **bounded** distribution. An order-one Gaussian readout is not covered by this particular global proof.

## Why arctan is not essential

The important discovery is that we do not actually need arctan’s explicit transformation
\[
F(z)=z+\frac{z^3}{3}.
\]

What we need is a way to handle the first-layer activation derivative without multiplying approximation errors by an uncontrolled backward coordinate.

Recall the population backward field:
\[
\delta_a^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}(\phi^{(2)})'(Z_a^{(2)}).
\]
For orthogonal inputs, the first-layer equation is
\[
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
=
-2\kappa_1r_a
(\phi^{(1)})'(Z_a^{(1)})
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]

The difficulty is the product of the activation derivative and the backward response. That response has controlled RMS size, but it need not be bounded at every population coordinate.

Instead of dividing by the activation derivative, define a scalar function \(J(s,z)\) by
\[
\frac{\partial J(s,z)}{\partial s}
=
(\phi^{(1)})'(J(s,z)),
\qquad
J(0,z)=z.
\]

Think of \(s\) as an auxiliary clock: starting at \(z\), move according to the scalar velocity \((\phi^{(1)})'\).

Because that velocity is bounded and Lipschitz, this scalar evolution exists uniquely for every positive or negative \(s\). If
\[
M_1=\sup_z|(\phi^{(1)})'(z)|,
\]
then simply integrating its velocity gives
\[
|J(s,z)-J(\widetilde s,z)|
\le M_1|s-\widetilde s|,
\qquad
|J(s,z)|\le |z|+M_1|s|.
\]

Now introduce a displacement \(X_a^{(1)}(0)=0\), and write
\[
Z_a^{(1)}(t)
=
J(X_a^{(1)}(t),Z_{0,a}^{(1)}).
\]
Give this displacement the evolution
\[
\dot X_a^{(1)}
=
-2\kappa_1r_a(W^{(2)})^*\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]

The chain rule gives
\[
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
=
(\phi^{(1)})'(Z_a^{(1)})\dot X_a^{(1)},
\]
which is **exactly the original gradient-flow equation**. We have changed proof coordinates, not the training dynamics.

For arctan, this construction satisfies
\[
F(J(s,z))=F(z)+s.
\]
Thus the new \(X_a^{(1)}\) is the displacement
\[
F(Z_a^{(1)})-F(Z_{0,a}^{(1)}),
\]
rather than the unshifted transformed variable.

Unlike division by \(\phi'\), the scalar-flow construction still works when the derivative is zero. At such a point, the scalar flow simply stays there.

The key stability estimate is now
\[
\left|
\phi^{(1)}(J(s,z))
-
\phi^{(1)}(J(\widetilde s,z))
\right|
\le M_1^2|s-\widetilde s|.
\]

So first-layer activation differences are controlled directly by clock differences, with a constant independent of width and of the initial coordinate \(z\). This replaces the special arctan estimate.

The rest of the population dynamics retain their ordinary form:
\[
\dot W^{(3)}
=
-2\kappa_3\sum_a r_aH_a^{(2)}.
\]
For any fixed first-population field \(V\), the middle-weight update means
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(W^{(2)}V\bigr)
=
-2\kappa_2\sum_a
r_a\delta_a^{(2)}
\,\mathbb E[H_a^{(1)}V].
\]

This is the population version of the finite rank-one matrix updates. The adjoint \((W^{(2)})^*\) is retained on the same state; it is not replaced by an independent Gaussian matrix.

### Why this works for every finite time

The crucial estimates are not small-time expansions.

Since these equations are the original gradient flow, the loss is nonincreasing:
\[
\sum_a r_a(t)^2\le \sum_a r_a(0)^2.
\]

Boundedness of the second activation therefore gives a coordinatewise readout bound:
\[
\|W^{(3)}(t)\|_\infty
\le
\|W^{(3)}(0)\|_\infty
+
2\kappa_3\|\phi^{(2)}\|_\infty
\sqrt m\,\|r(0)\|_2\,t.
\]

Consequently, every \(\delta_a^{(2)}\) is bounded on each fixed finite interval. The remaining equations give
\[
\sqrt{\mathbb E|\dot X_a^{(1)}|^2}
\le C_T\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}},
\]
and
\[
\|\dot W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
C_T\left(
1+\sum_a\sqrt{\mathbb E|X_a^{(1)}|^2}
\right).
\]

Here the operator norm means the largest amplification of RMS size by \(W^{(2)}\). The second inequality uses the fact that the first activation grows at most linearly.

Adding these inequalities produces a linear growth bound: the sizes can grow at most exponentially on the prescribed interval, and cannot become infinite at a finite time. Together with the stability estimates, this gives global existence and uniqueness.

It also gives genuine autonomy. At any later time, we may use the **current** \(Z_a^{(1)}\) as the starting points for \(J\) and reset the auxiliary displacements to zero. The future depends on the current fields and middle-weight action, not on retaining the earlier clocks.

### Why finite GD converges to this flow

The existing coarse-Euler/oracle argument survives the activation change. At a fixed auxiliary mesh, there are only finitely many Gaussian matrix calls and rank-one updates. The new map \(J(X,Z_0)\) grows at most linearly, so it satisfies the growth hypothesis needed for the fixed-program empirical-limit result, including reused transpose calls. This is the relevant specialization of [Tensor Programs III, Theorem 2.10](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2009.10685v3).

The actual discrete step also admits an exact comparison. For a scalar update
\[
z^+=z+\eta(\phi^{(1)})'(z)b,
\]
one can express the result as
\[
z^+=J(\eta b+e,z),
\qquad
|e|\le L_1\eta^2b^2,
\]
provided \(L_1\eta|b|\le\tfrac12\). The proof uses the scalar clock integral where the derivative is nonzero; at a derivative zero, both updates fix \(z\).

In the network, \(b\) is a coordinate of
\[
-2\kappa_1r_{n,a}(W^{(2)})^\top\delta_a^{(2)}.
\]
Its RMS size is bounded uniformly in width, while its largest coordinate is bounded by \(C_T\sqrt n\). Accumulating the coordinate defects and ordinary Euler errors therefore gives
\[
\text{GD-to-finite-flow error}
\le
C_T(\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n).
\]

This vanishes under the stated learning-rate condition. Combining it with the oracle limit and the two coarse-mesh approximation errors proves the joint GF/population limit.

## What survives—and what remains open here

The broader theorem can retain the nontriviality properties you asked about. With positive initialization variances, positive layerwise learning-rate constants, zero limiting readout, nonaffine \(\phi^{(1)}\), and nonconstant \(\phi^{(2)}\), there is a fixed \(T_*>0\), independent of width and learning rate, on which:

- Both hidden preactivations move, and the middle-weight action changes.
- The training kernel changes.
- The summed loss decreases at a strictly positive rate.
- Both activations have a strictly positive error when approximated by their best affine function on the current preactivation distribution.

The hidden RMS velocities are of order \(t\) near initialization—not positive at \(t=0\), but nonvanishing in the width limit at every fixed small positive time. These strict conclusions are proved in the final part of the linked proof; they are not asserted forever.

The unresolved boundary is **nonorthogonal inputs with a nonlinear first activation**. Writing
\[
G_{ab}=\frac{x_a^\top x_b}{d},
\]
their actual first-layer equation is
\[
\dot Z_a^{(1)}
=
-2\kappa_1\sum_b
G_{ab}r_b
(\phi^{(1)})'(Z_b^{(1)})
(W^{(2)})^*\delta_b^{(2)}.
\]

The derivative now belongs to input \(b\), not necessarily input \(a\). The scalar transformation no longer removes it. Dividing by the \(a\)-th derivative introduces ratios that may be unbounded—even for arctan.

That is a missing global stability argument, not evidence that the global limit fails. If the first activation is affine, its derivative is constant and this obstruction disappears: the global theorem then extends to arbitrary fixed finite input configurations, with merely \(\eta_n\to0\). But that exception does not preserve a genuinely nonlinear first layer.

So the established generalization is broad in **activation choice, initialization, and learning-rate constants**. Arctan’s special formula is unnecessary. The global arbitrary-angle, genuinely nonlinear two-hidden-layer case remains a separate unresolved extension of this proof.
